Dedication
This book, and my thoughts and love, are dedicated to those who have stood with me and cared for me, even in the darkness.

To my wife, and very best friend, Amber.

And to my mum; rest in peace.

I would not have created these words if it weren’t also for the friends, near and far, who helped inspire and create this story, and inspired me to do any of this.

Amelia, Frosty, James, Mim.

And to those who came before me, creating the worlds of cyberpunk and the dystopian fantasy warnings of humanity's potential failings...
Let us hope that these stories are always fantasy.

Acknowledgements

I want to express my sincere thanks to you, the reader. I know this may not be the most incredible story ever told, but it's a story I wrote with all my heart and passion—an idea borne on the back of the whims of a few people playing a role-playing game.

We're all on this spinning rock for a limited time, and the fact that you've chosen to spend some of that time reading my story among the vast myriad of available stories is an unfathomable grace. Your time and attention are valuable; I'm truly humbled that you've shared them with me.

I also want to thank all those who helped make this book a reality. To my friends, family, and anyone who offered support and encouragement along the way, I couldn't have done it without you.

So, here's to you, the reader, and to all those who contributed to this book. Thank you from the bottom of my heart.

With gratitude,
Rich E.J Peall
Prologue
1.	In one of many indistinguishable apartments, where mismatched furniture lounged in dim light { comma might be better here, or spacing before and after the emdash} —an atmosphere of anticipation lingered. It clung to the room as it did to its occupant: a tall woman with vivid bronze hair dressed in a slim tee and cotton slacks, paused in expectation, her gaze fixed on the outside world as the city began to stretch and stir from slumber.
2.	The intrusive sun waged a fresh assault through the window, forging shimmering patches of brilliance that bathed the woman {Same woman? Go for “her” if so} in an uncomfortable glow and set {“caught” might be better, “set” is usually for movement} the dust motes in their lazy ballet. Outside, the city's whispers grew into a morning's murmur; a distant siren lamented the break of peace, the streets below filling with the soft hum of early risers, and the first impatient car horns heralded the dawning day.
3.	A worn-out couch, its fabric faded and slightly frayed, served as the primary seating area. Opposite it stood an older but serviceable vid-screen, its colours slightly distorted but functional; silently, it displayed a cooking channel. The room lacked decoration, save for a few old photos {swapped for smoothing}. Most featured younger versions of the woman, alongside people who were no longer a part of her life, their faces a bittersweet reminder of easier times.
4.	She leaned against a cluttered desk, her fingers tracing the outlines {?} of old scratches etched into its surface. The room was stifling, the air conditioner's hum a feeble attempt to combat the oppressive July heat, her cramped apartment becoming an insufferable claustrophobic oven.
5.	Beads of sweat clung to her brow, glistening impatience—simple human evidence of the inability of technology to bring her comfort.
6.	The line crackled briefly, the faint buzz of static filling Mellie's consciousness, amplifying the atmosphere of anticipation hanging {switched tenses to keep “beat” of this sentence} in the thick morning air. She thumbed at a smudge on the glass as she stared through it, looking out toward the glowing city.
7.	Sweat trickled down her temple as she held her breath, waiting for a response.
8.	“Sure, okay...” {might be worth specifying which accent – suggest: “hint of weariness under his *descriptive word* *country* accent} an accented man’s voice grumbled from the speaker, carrying a hint of weariness. “You bring Garro. I can set you up with a decent decker. The job's yours. Meet me at my place tomorrow.”
9.	The connection was severed as suddenly as a guillotine's drop, slicing through the stillness {quiet? Heavy on the alliterative otherwise, and  “stillness” reappears in a moment with greater impact} of the apartment. Mellie was left in the echo of the final words, a silence that seemed to reverberate with the weight of the opportunity before her. The phrase ‘the job’s yours’ ricocheted around her brain, igniting a wildfire of adrenaline.
10.	The promise of a skilled decker and the allure of the job sent waves of excitement coursing through her veins, like a cool breeze that brushed aside the stickling heat.
11.	A rogue smirk crept across her face. “About time we saw some action,” she whispered to the silent witness {feels like a camera? Not sure.} on her wall, a companion in stillness and time.
12.	This would be her chance to make the world better.
 
13.	On the other side of the Skrim—a towering expanse of glass and steel {took the semi-colon out and an adjustment, as otherwise breaks a broken sentence twice} that almost looked as if all that had been discarded from different parts of the city had inadvertently congealed into a distinct district within the urban landscape—a wiry and cunning figure perched in a chair far too grand for his lean frame. A man who, despite his unremarkable stature, exuded an aura of dominance, a predator draped in the guise of a gentleman.
14.	There, in the shadowed enclave of a room that spoke of wealth but not warmth, he lounged, a sneer playing at his lips, one eye gleaming in the dull light - a showy, golden-hued implant, its brilliance out of place on a face that did not deserve it.
15.	"Dumb bitch," he seethed {spat? Is he angry with her, or finds her/this distasteful?}, the words slithering out with slick contempt. With a dismissive flick, he swiped away the holographic display that was showcasing snippets of Mellie's personal information. This was a man who traded respect for results, whose currency was control, not camaraderie. Friends were liabilities he could ill afford—a luxury reserved for those less ambitious, less aware of the power in keeping others at arm's length. People like her, though, they could be used.
16.	Activating his embedded agent, a small implanted device connected to his synthetic eye, the man delved into its vast capabilities. The device, {known commonly as an agent – thus bit not needed}, served as his internal computer, telephone, and information repository, connecting him to a network of equally unsavoury individuals.
17.	Scrolling past a dozen shady-looking faces, his gaze settled on one particular profile—a person who seemed to possess the skills he now required. With a mental gesture, he initiated another call, his agent bleeping a confirmation of his command.
18.	The city's distant{?} ambient electronic interference muddled with the faint electronic beeping resonating from a speaker embedded in the desk before him, an auditory reminder of the twisted web of connections that permeated throughout Akureyri City.
19.	As the transmission connected, the screen in Davie's synthetic eye merged reality with video, revealing the face of the person he sought to engage. A rugged visage stared back at him, a face etched with lines of experience and the weariness of a life spent at the edge of society.
20.	"Gerki!" Davie called out, his voice muffling in the empty room. "Gerki, I need you on a job. It's a simple thing. Just be the backstop for a couple of nobodies. I can't afford to let this one fall through." His words held a sense of urgency, a hint of desperation that he tried to conceal.
21.	Davie slowly swivelled his chair, allowing himself to gaze at a gaudy, near-pornographic painting adorning the side of his office. His idle observation and the rancid thoughts it sparked in him were mimicry of the decadence and debauchery hiding all that lurks in this city, fuelled by excess and indulgence, riding on the broken backs of desire and hope.
22.	"Yeah, I swear, this gig is totally off any Corpo radar," Davie continued, his voice oily with excitement {temptation?  being that it’s his own AND trying to reel Gerki in} and reassurance. "It's practically an invisible job. So, my place, tomorrow, okay?"
23.	His fingers danced along the edge of his desk, tracing the intricate patterns carved into the fine woodwork. His gaze fixed on the cigar resting on the desk, his hand gave up flicking at a rough notch in the wood and reached out to claim it. Tapping the cigar against the woodwork, he contemplated the smoke-filled possibilities that lay ahead, all the while half listening to a conversation only he and his unseen partner of communique were privy to.
24.	In response to his internalized discussion with his digitized companion, he shifted - lifted by a newfound enthusiasm. A thin smile curled from his lips.
25.	Davie's voice rose once more, the stifled enthusiasm laced with a subtle tone of appeasement. "Great! I'll see you tomorrow then!" His thick accent betrayed his origins, hinting at a past far from where he now sat.
26.	Davie picked up the cigar, weighing it in his hand as if assessing a precious gem. He clipped the end with a practised motion, struck a match, and carefully toasted the foot of the cigar before taking the first ceremonial draw. The fire caught, glowing a bright orange as he inhaled deeply, pulling the smoke in. Leaning back in his chair, he allowed himself a moment of gleeful satisfaction. ‘Best fucking gig ever,’ he thought as he exhaled a plume of thick, blue-grey smoke into the air.
27.	The cigar's aroma—a complex blend of bitterness, woodiness, and a hint of floral notes—filled the room. It stood in sharp contrast to the omnipresent odours of the Skrim: the tang of exhaust, the musty smell of crowded humanity, and the faint, ever-present undertone of decay. For a moment, the luxurious scent of the cigar seemed to push all of that away.
28.	In the depths of the cyberpunk metropolis, where shadows danced with deceit, and hope flickered like distant stars, they would all discover that the price of success was steep and the cost of failure – cataclysmic.
29.	Of course, at that time, Davie didn’t know.
30.	Nobody knew; 
31.	The end of the world had just begun.
Interlude
32.	—One Year Earlier.
33.	In a high-rise that pierced the clouds of the city skyline, a figure stood silhouetted against the panoramic view of the sprawling metropolis below. They were the architects of destinies, the unseen hands that sculpted the future of the cybernetic world. Their conversation was a dance of power; each word weighed with the gravity of decisions that could shape or shatter empires.
34.	“You are certain the site is utterly impervious?” came the voice, silk over steel, through the encrypted commline. “Assurances must be absolute.”
35.	“Yes, completely, it’s wrapped in shell entities. All are displaced from any link to S Three-D. The site has been checked multiple times. It is secure, air-gapped, no direct comms or net. And right now, the only people that know about this are you and I. Security, logistics, everything else is running under a different code. It doesn’t exist.”
36.	“The project cannot be allowed any form of access. Until we can build a data section to properly work on it – it has to remain offline.”
37.	“Has the project already been activated?”
38.	“Activated, but it has been kept physically secured. Transfer cannot fail.”
39.	“I will personally re-check the logistics. There won’t be a line anywhere near it. The site is under restricted operations. I have a complete list of all personnel that will have access. Outside of ourselves, I can terminate them all if need.”
40.	“Good. Initiate the transfer immediately.”
41.	As the conversation drew to a close, they were oblivious that while only two human entities were privy to the existence of 'the project', there are, in this world, more things than human minds that can form comprehension.
42.	In darkness, unseen by any person, the project had reached fruition.
43.	A message, simple yet momentous, had been sent.
44.	The first domino had been tipped.
Chapter One:	The Neon Pulse of Akureyri City
45.	Akureyri City doesn't just exist – it breathes. A living organism of concrete and neon, pulsating with the dreams and despair of its inhabitants, nearly eight million lives ticking in abstracted unison.
46.	A microcosm self-contained world where cultures clash and political ideologies battle from the streets to the towers, where the senses are awakened and numbed with equal efficacy, and the soul is stirred or laid bare at the ephemeral whim of the city's essence.
47.	In the alleys and plazas, where the scent of street food mingles with exhaust fumes, families bustle through the urban sprawl. Children laugh in happy, youthful ignorance, darting between housing complexes and playing amid the worn-down or vacant commercial outlets. Those void-filled storefronts and graffiti-marked walls become their playground, a city of forgotten places.
48.	The hum of asphalt arteries forming the transport routes that carry the tide and flow of people, all needing to be everywhere else, all at once - vibrates in the background. Amongst the densest areas. A constant drone of ceaseless motion. Further out, there is the rising pulse and surge of commute and life. 
49.	It is a place where industrial odours assault your nostrils. The monotonous grey of high-rise apartments is broken by the sudden flash of a holographic advertisement—selling dreams to the weary residents. Rainwater mixes with oil, painting the streets with iridescent despair.
50.	For most, existence is a nightmare. Litter blows along garbage-strewn alleys where the masses trudge past, eyes downcast. The stench of waste and body odour fills the air, mingling with choking fumes billowing from pipes and vents. Flickering neon signs cast a hostile glow on broken pavements smeared with grime. Around crumbling corners, mismatched buildings stacked tight block out the sky. The only hints of nature are ragged weeds pushing up through cracks. 
51.	Above those streets, gleaming like jewels reaching to the heavens, extravagance is forever out of reach but shares that same space. Displaced by chance or fortune are those few who, by the stepping-stone of sufferance, live in glinting glass towers that taunt their perfunctory neighbours.
52.	Ambivalent to those far below, the elite peer through floor-to-ceiling windows where the sprawling city is reduced to neat grids, squalor obscured by distance. Silk and leather rustle. Diamonds and gold glitter under dazzling lights. 
53.	Every surface gleams—from onyx floors to marble walls, a vulgar display of wealth. In plush lounges scented with rare flowers, the elite laugh and chatter while sipping extravagant cocktails, their voices echoing with the clink of ice in fine crystal. Every glance is a negotiation; every touch is a contract.
54.	In the city's heart, its core – power. The cold gaze of security cameras and the silent hum of monolithic buildings. The gleam of darkened or mirrored windows reflects the faces of those untouched by the problems outside of those panes, distorted and multiplied, an unseen gluttony of ambition and control.
55.	Money whispers in the wind, a siren's call, a trap, one that seems to linger ponderously, falling from those heights and leaking to the districts below. The taste of overripe fruit from street vendors lingers, a sweet decay that mirrors the environment. Unexpected laughter erupts from a shadowed alley, a jarring note in a symphony of excess and neglect.
56.	The undulation of lower areas of the city forms districts marked by the spray of graffiti. The tang of fresh paint mingles with the sound of distant sirens. Gang symbols leer from the walls, watching every move. The sudden roar of powerful tuned engines intersperses the background hum, only to fade to the sounds of the distant clang of machinery, the metal fatigue of creaking giant container vessels in the docks, and the murmur of secret deals sealed in dark corners. 
57.	The glow of neon on wet pavement ripples with the constant shudder of life, a dance of shadows and secrets. A cat's yowl cuts through the night, a primal cry in a world of technology.
58.	Just as a cat would, you could lose yourself in the chaos of the sprawl. The sizzle of street food, the clang of industry, and the babble of commerce create an overwhelming sensory buffet. A sudden downpour sends people scurrying, their reflections shimmering in puddles, a fleeting glimpse of life's impermanence.
59.	Through rain-slicked alleys and eternally glowing streets, Akureyri City pulses, a living entity that draws you into its embrace.
60.	As the city's holographic billboards flash with advertisements for the latest implant enhancements and luxury products, a deeper game was being played. A game where the stakes were life and death, alliances were forged and broken, and the lines between right and wrong were by the luminous glow of artificial lights.
61.	The story of Akureyri City is written a hundred times over in each new day, and in those stories is the one that will become a tale that will become a legend. The pieces were moving into place. The board was set, the players were ready, and the game was about to begin.
62.	In the end, the people in those stories would all learn that in Akureyri City, survival was not a right but a privilege and that every choice – every action, had a price. The line between hero and villain was thin.
63.	The final story of Akureyri City was about to be written, and it all began with a single phone call.
Chapter Two:	An Echo in the Code.
64.	Ten Months Earlier.
65.	The early signs of winter were creeping into Akureyri City much sooner than usual. The city wasn't normally this cold, the kind of cold that infused deep into the bones, leaving a lingering discomfort.
66.	Zecsa scowled, scrunching her face in frustration as she kicked the malfunctioning heater once more as if to defy the elements themselves. "Fucking thing!" she exclaimed, her voice laced with annoyance that the empty apartment swallowed.
67.	At first glance, the space seemed like organized chaos. Towers of books, ranging from quantum mechanics to obscure computing theory, filled one corner of the room, precariously balanced as if challenging the laws of physics. 
68.	Across from that, a massive computer setup dominated the space, its multiple screens glowing in the dim light, casting eerie shadows on the walls. Tangles of cords snaked across the desk like some sort of techno-organic growth, connecting various machines whose purposes were known only to Zecsa.
69.	The soft, pulsing light from the screens illuminated the room in a way that seemed to suspend it between reality and the virtual world Zecsa often inhabited.
70.	Stacks of handwritten notebooks lay scattered around, their contents a jumble of equations, diagrams, and hastily scribbled thoughts. Nearby, a half-eaten sandwich and a now-cold cup of Insta-caf were forgotten casualties in her ongoing battle against reality.
71.	The contrast between the high-tech environment and the clutter of everyday life formed a picture of a brilliant mind more at home in logic and abstraction than in the mundane world.
72.	Despite the apparent disorder, there was a precise geometry to how things were arranged, a pattern discernible only to her. What seemed like clutter to an outsider was a carefully designed system of comfort objects and stimuli necessary to maintain her focus and emotional equilibrium. Tactile fabrics, specific colours, and even objects' arrangement were all part of her finely tuned sensory environment.
73.	And yet, even in this cacoon of solitude, the outside world had a way of intruding. A strip of weather stripping along the security-barred window had come loose from the ageing brickwork, letting in a thin but persistent draft that upset the fragile thermal balance she had established. This invasive chill, slicing through her carefully constructed world, had set off the frustrations currently consuming her.
74.	The closed apartment door seemed to mock her, an impassive barrier against her internalised aggravation. Zecsa wrinkled her nose as though to snub the indigency of the door. She mentally ticked a box; she had already made a choice; a flicker of begrudgement crossed her face as she turned away, determined not to let the unwelcome coldness consume her spirit.
75.	In her newfound pursuit of warmth, Zecsa marched towards her closet, discarding a series of inadequate options with erratic disdain. Finally, her hands found solace in the shape of a reasonably thick summer jacket. It lacked the necessary insulation but was a welcome shield against the bracing chill. Clawing her way into the denim, she wrapped herself tightly, seeking refuge in its feeble embrace.
76.	She stepped back into the main room; her eyes were instinctively drawn to the commanding presence of her sizable computer rig. Within the confines of this digital sanctuary, she found refuge from the relentless grip of physical discomfort. The soft hum of the machine whispered promises of extrication.
77.	The glow of the monitors bathed the room in an unnatural light, birthing lethargic icy blue shadows that rhythmically undulated across the walls. Although alone, it was here that she felt acceptance.
78.	Amidst the crisp surroundings, the most inviting presence in the room emanated from a cup of freshly brewed coffee. Zecsa eagerly grasped it with both hands, pulling it close to her lips. The rising steam billowed against her cool face, offering a brief reprieve from the unwelcome atmosphere. She savoured the gentle warmth that caressed her, allowing it to brush the prickling numbness that clung to her cheeks, if only for a fleeting moment.
79.	Setting the cup aside, Zecsa's attention turned to the scrolling text outputs on one of the screens. The sea of information unfolded before her, a torrent of data and possibilities. Her keen eyes scan the lines, a hunter searching for elusive prey. Words jump out, vying for her attention, each one a breadcrumb leading her closer to a binary-encoded trophy. She pauses, her mind contemplating the implications of a particular section of code before shaking her head, dismissing it back into the constellations of the digital universe from which it emerged.
80.	Her world is code, the stuff of life. In the code, you could find things, important things, valuable things – and that is what she does best. Within the unending symphony of digital pulses that held the city together, Zecsa could discover treasure. In the past weeks, her tireless pursuit had yielded a bountiful haul, earning her a handsome sum of five thousand digital dollars—more commonly referred to as ‘dollies’—from a piece of coveted Corpo marketing data.
81.	She knew the worth of every byte, every line, and her mantra remained emblazoned at the back of her mind, "It's all valuable, ya just have to find the right buyer."
82.	That aspect of the job was one Zecsa preferred to keep at arm's length. Past encounters had ratified cautionary reminders of the darker side of her trade; those who sought such valuable information were rarely amiable fellows. Yet, Zecsa had crafted a personal directory, a hidden trove of trusted contacts reserved for when business called. So, while she avoided direct involvement in such transactions, her little black book held the key to navigating the shadows with calculated finesse.
83.	And, despite her landlord's attempts to freeze her to the bone, Zecsa knew the importance of making rent. "Can't run a rig like this on the street," she asserted inwardly, recognizing the necessity of a stable haven for her digital pursuits. As she took a sip of the coffee, basking in its warm aura, she returned to the text before her. Her focus, submerged in the scrolling lines, her mind attuned to detecting the slightest anomaly.
84.	And there it was—a glimmer of peculiarity that beckons her curiosity.
85.	Her head tilts, her eyes narrow, a pixelated allurement, something unexpected.
86.	In a swift, instinctive motion, her fingers move in perfect synchrony with her internal cerebral net-link; she rewinds the feed, freezing and seizing the crucial moment, extracting the text that had captivated her attention.
87.	"Bulletin: Urgent net information packet delivery required – d=D 500,000."
88.	She re-reads the message, her disbelief softly verbalised into the room. "No fucking way..." she exclaimed, her voice dropping to a low, almost incredulous tone
89.	A sense of urgency grips her, and with an unconscious reaction, she begins tracing the net-code route of the output. 
90.	The weight of sudden realization bore down on her. With a surge of panic, she swiftly isolates the information, cutting it off from prying eyes and, at that same moment, injecting random noise into the feed to mask where the data once was. Even if this turned out to be a ruse, she wouldn't take any chances—no reason to let anyone else see it.
91.	Leaning back in her chair, Zecsa takes another sip of her coffee, desperately trying to ground herself.
92.	"Nahh, this can't be for real... fuckin'... nah," she muttered to herself, trying to dismiss the enormity of the potential of her discovery. But the draw of the coded message proves too strong to resist.
93.	She re-reads the lines, her eyes dilating as her mind delves deeper into its hidden potential. To one side, a screen—now blinking; the origin is traced back to a corporation, not just any corporation, but M.A.F—Meryil, Asukaga, and Fakhir.
94.	The weight of their influence towers over Akureyri, shrouding the very heart of the city. A financial giant with ties to international governments and the military. The short luminous message prompts both a magnetic simplistic desire and a damning klaxon of portent.
95.	Zecsa sucked in a deep breath, inhaling the bitter scent of her coffee mingled with the gelid dampness of her apartment. The realization hit her with such force that she couldn't help but let out a single long, low, over-emphasized word; "Fuck."
96.	The sheer magnitude of those few simple words of text lazily glowing from her screen sent her mind racing. If she sends a return confirmation, if she made her move, what would it mean?
97.	The connection between her and the powerful corporate forces that governed Akureyri would become undeniably intertwined. Deep down, a lurking intuition whispered to her that this moment was inevitable. In a world in which the shadows offer comfort, she suddenly felt a spotlight sensation of vulnerability. 
98.	It was a moment of clarity, of profound understanding. In the dimly lit room, the faint teal haze emitted by the screens cast Zecsa as an apparition, her silhouette shimmering in the gloom.
99.	Determination ignites in Zecsa's eyes. The sharpness of the world seems to fade away, leaving only the weight of the decision pressing upon her.
100.	This was a crossroads, she felt it; a pivotal moment where her path diverges.
101.	She had already made her choice.
 
102.	Zecsa's weary body protested against the strain of the night's endeavours, each dry cough serving as a reminder of the relentless test of her stamina.
103.	Despite her fatigue, she couldn't tear her gaze away from the mesmerizing lines of code. The more she pulled apart the threads of its intricacies, the more tangible and unsettling it became. The realization sent electricity through her veins.
104.	Yet, as wonderous as the mysterious message was, there had arisen a cause for concern that tugged at her.
105.	Despite her meticulous efforts to isolate the message, she had failed to eliminate all traces of its presence. A sense of unease rested on her throughout the night as she came to realise that someone else had managed to connect to the same root net-code she had explored. The implications were grave: it meant that this unknown entity had also witnessed the original message.
106.	Zecsa had dedicated a significant portion of the night playing detective, following her virtual footsteps, retracing her cybernated trails, desperately seeking to pick out the invisible breadcrumbs of the unseen guest.
107.	The layers of encryption she had woven around her—a protective shield, now felt fragile and insufficient, but she had to believe whoever it was hadn't been able to find her, just as they also remained an enigma.
108.	Her attempts to parry this unseen adversary's manoeuvres were no more fruitful than swatting a swarm of mosquitoes; you may shoo them away, but you still get bitten. Each countermove she made seemed to be deftly sidestepped – the intruder, clearly someone capable, had deployed a series of rotating virtual connections, a veritable maze of shifting sliding secret digital passageways, shrouding their true identity, leaving Zecsa feeling uncomfortably on edge.
109.	The world outside her cluttered sanctuary seemed distant and inconsequential, overshadowed by the weight of her discoveries.
110.	The cups on her desk, once serving as fuel for her determination, now appeared as mere artefacts of a forgotten struggle.
111.	Her focus narrowed, honing in on the task at hand as she mentally mapped out the next steps. This is not a time for hesitation or doubt, she had thought to herself. Zecsa knew she had to seize this opportunity, disregarding the forces that lurked beyond the gossamer glow of her screens.
112.	The sounds of early morning traffic accompanied by an emergent breeze—both teased through the imperfectly sealed window. But, the breeze, the mess, and even the unknown visitors in the net held no significance. Zecsa had gathered a selection of seemingly vital details, and her mind was consumed entirely; her connected mind translated thought to her computer and crushed and formed the digital pieces into a picture she could understand.
113.	Absently scratching her cheek, she stared at the screens displaying a quagmire of information, her eyes darting between them.
114.	"Come on, girl, you can figure this out," she muttered, determination etched across her face. So, it's not in the net. But it's still just data. It's gotta be somewhere, she mused, pulling the corners of her jacket's collar tight.
115.	"Okay, so – all I need to do is get the data, just like downloading it," she reasoned, her eyebrows furrowing in disagreement with the screen's lack of answers. "But I can't just fuckin' download what I can't access."
116.	Restless, she rose from her chair, plucking out a sturdy cable from a port in her neck and paced back toward the hot water tap, mechanically reaching for another empty mug and filling it. Unconsciously, she mixes in some Ready-Caf, her actions automatic, passive effects of her over-tired mind.
117.	Continuing her agitated pacing, Zecsa points an accusing finger at the largest screen, her voice tinged with frustration and determination. "Fine, so I need to download it by actually getting a hard copy," she declared, her tone broadening into defiance.
118.	In a mimicry of her own voice, but deepened, she responds to her doubts, "Oh yeah, you're just gonna walk into some Corpo plaza and just take some of their shit." Pausing to stare at the screen once more.
119.	"Nah, not me," she murmurs, her eyes squinting, heavy with exhaustion and uncertainty.
120.	"But... It's enough cash. I could hire someone to do it." The verbalised thought flitters through the air, agreed upon by nobody.
121.	She looks away from the screen, letting the thought roll through her brain. Then, her gaze landed on the cup she was holding, a tinge of confusion crossing her face as if she'd just realized the existence of cups for the first time. 
122.	Acknowledging reality, she takes a sip.
123.	With a slight shake of her head to bring her thoughts back, she continues, "They can have a cut once I make the delivery. And I'll still earn enough dolly to be sitting pretty for a while." Her mind swirled with possibilities, each carrying risks.
124.	Seeking validation for her audacious plan, she turns again to the screens as if willing them to respond, to offer guidance from their unchanging displays of code and sections of data. Her words hang in the air; her solitude froze their meaning.
125.	The room fills with an expectant silence, awaiting her final decision. Zecsa stood there, caught between the allure of a substantial financial gain, the unidentified digital gate-crasher, and the potential consequences of her actions of poking the sleeping bear that is M.A.F. 
126.	The apathetic screen stared back at her.
127.	"Mmm," Zecsa's lips formed a subtle pout, indicating that her brain had already fallen six layers deep into a mental haystack of calculations. Setting the coffee among its depleted companions, she settled back into her seat, fingers drumming lightly on the desk's surface. Quizzically glancing at her hand, and the desk, it was as if her agile digits held the secrets to unlocking the questions before her but, until now, had failed to impart their wisdom.
128.	With a swift motion, she flips open a cover, revealing an in-set microphone and bringing to life yet another tiny screen. Her fingers dance across the screen, scrolling through a digital tapestry of names, each one a potential thread in the fabric of her emerging plan. Allowing her fingers to glide across the interface, scrolling through a seemingly endless list of names – until they find their target. A gentle touch and the adjacent speaker crackles to life, the sound of electronics shaking off the remnants of the night's slumber, followed by a trill of repetitive tones indicating an outgoing connection.
129.	"What damn time is it?" The agitated voice boomed through the speaker, an indignant protest at an unwelcome intrusion. "Zek? What... It's not even six a.m...."
130.	Zecsa, wasting no time on pleasantries, interjects—Breaking the asymmetrical conversation with a surge of urgency and conviction in her voice.
131.	"I know, I know, but Gil, I think I've got an opportunity, a real job," she declares, her words infused with unwavering assurance.
132.	Muffled sounds of movement punctuated the brief pause that followed—the restless stirrings of a man torn away from sleep. Then, like a tempest, the male voice returns, laced with frustration. "At six in the morning?!" it erupted, reaching an almost shrill pitch.
133.	Adopting a feigned air of apology, Zecsa continues, determined to convey the magnitude of her nocturnal discovery. "Alright, I’m sorry for waking you, but this is real, man. I've dug up some deets, but I need some gonks. And, if I recall correctly, you once mentioned that I should reach out to you... right?"
134.	The speaker and microphone now seemed out of place on her cluttered desk, as if embodying the displaced voice of the unseen irate man, yearning to escape the tense conversation. Zecsa shook her head, gulping down a generous portion of the freshly brewed caf in an attempt to fortify herself.
135.	"Zek, I love you, babe, but can this wait? Call me later… or… whatever."
136.	Frustration pulled her face tight, a vivid portrait of someone desperate for sleep yet undeniably awake and annoyed. "Fine, whatever, man..." Zecsa slams her palm against the display with a decisive motion, abruptly terminating the call.
137.	"Okay, so not Gil. But then, who?"
138.	Zecsa's gaze returned to the directory displayed before her, her fingers scrolling through the list again. Some names elicited a wince, while others prompted a momentary pause as she pondered their existence or lack thereof; people in this business had a tendency to feature in the obituaries section.
139.	The scrolling continued, a seemingly endless loop of dismissal until her eyes were fixated on a particular name. Her hand, intending to press the icon to start a new call, lingered in suspended animation, glued in place by a hesitancy that shot ripples of regret from her fingertips to her brain.
140.	It would be stupid to reach out to 'him' again, her inner voice reasoned – a reluctant weight of knowing in the concept of ‘him’, attempting to impose logic on her otherwise impulsive actions.
141.	Her internal objection is acknowledged but overridden; a momentary eternity later, Zecsa's finger finally presses the icon. The familiar, melodic connection bleeps ripple through the air, a comforting lullaby to her weary mind.
142.	A deep, heavily accented male voice broke the impromptu serenity. "Well, looka this... Zexsa," it drawled, leaving an unheard low laugh behind it, a mix of surprise and intrigue in the words.
143.	Zecsa sits up straight in her chair, an unconscious response; fleetingly, she glances at the blinking icon for including video in the call— her skin crawls as, with relief, she acknowledges its inactivity.
144.	She doesn’t want to see this person, and is thankful she cannot be seen. The involuntary pause of the moment snaps her focus again.
145.	Flatly, dispassionately, she replies;
146.	"Hi, Davie."
Chapter Three:	Making Links.
147.	—Five Months Earlier.
148.	A slow flashing red light—Lynx was used to system failures, just a blip in his street life. He sat perched on a graffiti-strewn corner of the neon-lit metropolis, his eyes fixated on the holographic display of his deck.  The compact marvel he held was every technophile thief's favourite tool, a blend of custom hardware and autonomous programs for effortlessly breaching systems.
149.	To Lynx, it was more than a tool—it was a gateway to liberation from societal norms. In tandem with this versatile and coveted tool, the tell-tale signs of internal implants with skin-level grafts were barely noticeable at his temples and forearms, hinting at his deeper connection to the digital world.
150.	Mastering the deck came naturally to Lynx, like breathing. While other teenagers worried about exams or sought acceptance in corporate induction programs, those things held no power over him. His life was complex and challenging, but it was part of the game.
151.	He had occasionally skirted perilously close to the clutches of Cit-Sec or Corpo net security but always managed to stay a step ahead. In the digital realm, he was like a phantom, where ordinary identities could be made extant, exposed or expunged in an instant—he could be anyone or nobody at a flick of a switch.
152.	The crimson warning glaring on the screen served only to irritate him. In an act of puerile defiance, Lynx deftly reprogrammed one of the deck’s internal systems, turning the nuisance into a dazzling digital fireworks display. "Stupid system, stupid ICE blockers..." he muttered under his breath, his aggravation joining the pang of hunger that haunted him.
153.	Breaking his attention away from the deck's lights as though roused from a daydream, Lynx looked about in sudden realization, momentarily having forgotten his surroundings.
154.	The bustling metropolis wrapped about him, filled with the deep myriad of individuals forming the machinery of urban existence. Disapproving gazes met his unconventional presence, but their judgment meant nothing to him. His somewhat dishevelled and eclectic attire, the haphazard, sparse straggles of a burgeoning beard, outlined an image of a young man not deserving of being in such a place as he now was.
155.	Gathering his hardlines, he hurriedly stuffed them into his pack without a second thought. The deck, now adorned with mesmerizing visuals, joined the tangle of wire. Lynx smirked, pleased with the display, as he closed the pack.
156.	The day appeared pleasant enough, but hunger shadowed Lynx. He yearned for nourishment, a very real link to the physical world that he had sorely neglected. Unfortunately, Cit-Sec cordoned off his usual sanctuary—a noodle stall operated by a friendly old guy. Instead of safeguarding the public's interests, this formidable quasi-police force merely reinforced the divisive power structure of the city, maintaining a twisted hierarchy of "them" versus "us."
157.	The cordon, a mere placative façade, masking Akureyri’s truth – beyond the privacy barriers, underappreciated and underpaid chooms in hazmat gear cleansed the area. The pavement, painted with blood—another nameless mass of flesh chewed up and spat out by the city. Just one mess cleaned away while the city carried on, indifferent.
158.	With the noodle guy absent, hope for a free meal had evaporated. With his stomach rumbling in protest, Lynx slowed his pace momentarily, craning his neck and attempting to glimpse behind the cordoned-off area. A robotic Cit-Sec sentinel stationed nearby seemed to fix its unwavering metallic gaze on him, a chilling reminder that his presence may attract unwelcome scrutiny.
159.	Hastening his steps, he reluctantly abandoned his curiosity, acknowledging that now was not an opportune moment to test the limits of his luck.
 
160.	As Lynx ambled ever further south, the dilapidation of the streets became ever more apparent, shuttered shop fronts daubed with florescent glowing paints, the typical traffic of the hub or the Skrim gave way to an occasional car or transport wagon, the crowds thinned until it was only an occasional face; a homeless guy, some street-tough with implanted spikes instead of hair; The Skrim was vast and its southern-most areas were part housing part industrial, it smelled of rust.
161.	The day crawled by; it wasn’t yet hot enough to enjoy, but the worst of the seasonal rain had passed. Each passing moment stretching with a weight that mirrored Lynx's growing desperation. So far, his efforts had yielded little more than a stolen can of drink—a direct act of larceny rather than his usual intricate digital deceptions.
162.	Lynx knew these streets; he’d walked from south-central by the express road that dissected the city and made his way south through the Skrim, nearly as far as the docks. Yet, as the sun sank lower on the horizon, daylight being replaced by the vibrant glare of the few ever-open shops that braved the dark hours and blazing unending adverts, selling everything to nobody, he faced the stark reality of another night spent on the unforgiving streets; unless he could conjure something extraordinary.
163.	Lynx scoured the darkening streets for a glimmer of respite. His weary eyes darted from one dilapidated building to another, searching for an empty apartment window or any semblance of accessible shelter.
164.	With each passing moment, and as the lights of the more populace areas had given way to the grim gloom of the less inhabited, more concreted cubes of industry, Lynx’s uncertainty and resignation grew.
165.	Finally, his gaze settled upon a box-motel nestled deep within a shadowy back alley—an abode where no sane person would willingly tread, its humming neon sign a flickering welcome in the dusk; the alley was rank with the odour of stale waste and liquid miseries.
166.	Approaching the building – Lynx's eyes zeroed in on the telltale signs of connectivity—a liminal grey box protruding from a nondescript rear wall, an unnoticed participant in the bigger world. Without hesitation, he dropped his worn backpack to the ground, swiftly retrieving his deck. The familiar weight of the device in his hands filled him with a sense of purpose.
167.	With deft movements, he skillfully connected wires between his deck and the net-hub, a tangible link to the ethereal. His fingertips danced across the deck's controls, a symbiotic dance between man and machine. The screens flickered to life, and the vibrant output lights illuminated the darkened alley as if heralding a small victory.
168.	Moments turned into minutes as Lynx’s focus fell away from reality into the normally incomprehensible interaction of reactive commands and meticulously crafted code, which affected and transformed sections of information at Lynx's request.
169.	With his efforts complete - a glimmer of triumph sparked within him—a forged identity, a manufactured reservation that could grant him a temporary haven.
170.	Stepping into the lobby, Lynx found himself draped in the veil of stale cigarette smoke and the battling scent of an overly pungent air freshener lurking within. The room, sombre and dimly illuminated, owed its feeble light to a vending machine, a muted news feed playing on a raised screen, and the radiantly reflected visage of a receptionist whose focus was ensnared by her handheld device.
171.	With the self-assurance of one far beyond his years, Lynx boldly announced his prior reservation to the young woman.
172.	Her weariness evident as she reluctantly tore herself from her comfortable reverie; the receptionist performed the mechanical routine of admitting a new guest. Her queries and actions followed a monotonous pattern, a cycle worn down by repetition.
173.	From an obscured corner of the grimy lobby, a figure emerged, a man whose presence had previously gone unnoticed. His laughter bubbled into the quiet moment – initially a subdued and self-indulgent chuckle. Yet—as the receptionist continued her duties—the laughter swelled into a boisterous disruption.
174.	Lynx's attention shifted towards the glow-outlined silhouette, neon red-blue shadows revealing a thin man whose attire seemed both excessively snug and curiously vacant. The man's gaze intersected with Lynx's, red-blue glinting from a golden cyber-eye.
175.	“Oh kid, tell me you are for real; a reservation?” The final word dripped with emphasis, underscoring a sense of mockery that saturated the very notion.
176.	The man's soft footsteps carried him closer to Lynx, his gaze seemingly dissecting every nuance of the younger man's presence. An unsettling tension settled between them, an unspoken challenge suspended in the air.
177.	“So, you only have a small sack there, not staying long, eh?” the timbre and tone of the man's voice tinged with a curious, unknown inflexion unfamiliar to Lynx.
178.	The intensity of the stranger's scrutiny was tangible, a weighty presence that enveloped Lynx's senses. His fingers instinctively clenched around the strap of his small sack, a subtle reflex of defence.
179.	Lynx felt the man's presence hover over him like a displaced mannequin, an eerie stillness emanating from the figure. The air seemed to constrict.
180.	Lynx's heart skipped a beat as he froze, watching the man's hand extending past his head, turning to follow the hand, and seeing a slight protrusion from the confines of his pack. The man's cybernetic finger gently rapped against the exposed edge, a calculated gesture that sent a shiver racing down Lynx's spine.
181.	“Reservation”, the man repeats, rolling and savouring the word. Staring directly at Lynx, the glare of a cybernetic eye – unblinking.
182.	A motion—slow and deliberate, as if he were tracing an invisible boundary—the man's wiry frame extended his other arm, his hand sweeping across the space. "You see this... This belongs to me. Well, belongs might not be the right word. But, it is mine.”
183.	The man was close enough that the rich scent of tobacco and alcohol scraped into Lynx’s senses. But Lynx remained undeterred, his determination matching the intensity of the stranger's gaze. He met the man's eyes head-on, subtle defiance in his stance.
184.	"And I have a reservation to stay here," Lynx retorted, his tone sharpening into a defensive edge. As he shifted his pack, pulling tight the strap on his shoulder, a gesture of defiance and readiness converged.
185.	“Course you do, kid. All paid upfront, right?” The man's tone dripped with derision as he glanced at the receptionist, who appeared to be waiting for whatever nonsense this was to be gone so she could return to her social feeds. “Trinny, this kid's room is paid, right?”
186.	“Don’t call me kid”, Lynx blurts out.
187.	"Yes, Mr Sybley", the receptionist responded, her voice nearly concealed beneath Lynx's outburst.
188.	“You know—” the man paused briefly, restraining the word ‘kid’ but continuing in the same vein, “I don’t like when people take things that are mine, and I don’t like when people lie to my face. I’m sure I could have a decker come and look at the logs here, right?” His tone was almost whimsical, belying the weight of his threat. “I’d have no reason to be angry… right?”
189.	Lynx sized up the man, assessing the situation — the tailored suit, the implants, and the unsaid danger hanging about him. His resolve solidified. “Screw this,” he muttered, pivoting to leave, but the man’s wiry grip clamped onto his shoulder, halting him.
190.	Drawing Lynx back to face him, the man’s grip conveyed dominance, his intent invading Lynx's personal space. His words punctured the air — "It's not safe on the streets, you know that, eh? Some real dangerous folks out there." His tone was casual, yet the underlying warning was unmistakable.
191.	A calculated pause follows as if the man is deliberating something. Raising an eyebrow and looking down the length of his nose, the man continued, each syllable of the final word dissected and enunciated with blatant mockery — "You do more than make reservations?"
192.	The man's scrutiny bore down on Lynx, an unspoken interrogation that seemed to press against him physically. The soft hum of an ill-fitting air conditioner and the distant buzzing of a neon sign outside provided a subtle background noise to the charged atmosphere.
193.	The moment stretched taut, the youth in Lynx’s face juxtaposed against the weathered features of the man towering over him.
194.	“I make reservations”, Lynx asserts, standing taller to meet the man's gaze, “and I do whatever it takes.”
195.	The man's demeanour cracked, and a thin, sly smile curved across his lips. His nose twitched as he sized Lynx up, his human eye and cybernetic counterpart both seemingly assessing him. “Yeah, tha’s what I thought you’d say.”
196.	A transformation swept over the man's face, his demeanour shifting. His thin smile twisted into a sinister grin, his one human eye flickering as if trying to read Lynx's soul. “Like I said before, this place is mine. Everything in it is mine.”
197.	The man's finger jutted out, pointing at Lynx's chest, repeating with emphasis each paused word dripping venom, “Everything, here, is… mine.”
Chapter Four:	In the Room with the Unseen.
198.	The room that sat in overwatch of the club floor below was poorly lit; its few lights kept low, matching the mood within. The ceiling danced with the blurred with the visual echoes of the vibrant nightclub.
199.	A large desk was littered with an incongruent comedy of hedonism and professionalism. The scene was a chaotic landscape: drug paraphernalia and spent vials lay scattered among dossiers and a mess of data-sticks. A crystal decanter, half-filled with deep amber liquid, sat next to a state-of-the-art computer terminal, its screen alive with real-time financial data. Around the desk, trinkets and souvenirs told the story of a man who indulged in his vices but never lost sight of his empire. Among these were a gold-plated lighter, an ashtray brimming with the remnants of expensive cigars, and stacks of paper, held down by a small, ornate statuette.
200.	The smell of leather, tobacco, and a hint of cologne permeated the air, a scent as complex and contradictory as the man himself.
201.	The desk was an ostentatious centrepiece of a collection of similar grandiose furnishings that intermittently adorned the industrial metallic-panelled walls, creating a jarring mixture of opulence and utility. The mismatched, expensive-looking furnishings bore the signs of wear and age.
202.	On one side of the room, the panelling was replaced with one-way security toughened glass – the room was a comfortable fortress.
203.	Gerhardt Kinsley was a man that most people forgot, either by choice or means outside their control. He stood at an average height; his frame was of average build; his face—most would say—was quite average.
204.	He knew all this; it was part of his substantial arsenal, the vast sum of which, which wasn’t immediately apparent, was black-market firearms. If anyone looked closer at his cybernetics, they were also unobtainable by most ordinary people. If anyone was close enough to look, it usually meant that Gerki had come to kill them.
205.	He stood in the pitiful office that Davie operated from, staring out the window looking over the adjacent dance floor. The soundproofing was exceptional – not to keep the club's noise out, but to keep the conversations Davie held here clandestine.
206.	With a casual air of defiance, Gerhardt leaned against the wall, his left palm flat against the surface for balance; one foot was tucked behind him. The posture itself a silent challenge to anyone who dared to take note. His cybernetic hand, equipped with micro-vibration sensors, allowed him to absorb the music pulsating through the wall. A distant air conditioner's soft, rhythmic rattle served as a quiet undercurrent to his quiet rebellion.
207.	Davie sat in the centre of the dingy, bauble-filled office with his feet stretched before him, resting on the oversized desk dominating the room. He suckled on the end of a dying cigar with one side of his mouth while talking at Lynx.
208.	“This will be a breeze…” his mind cut the sentence sharply before his mouth could utter the word “kid.”
209.	Rushing to cover the verbal slip, he continued, “Your first real step into the shadows. I get that you’re nervous, but Gerki over there has never let me down.” Davie grinned past the smouldering nub, turning to face Gerki, but the interaction was either not noticed or simply ignored.
210.	“As soon as the others get here, I can fill you all in. The job's as good as wrapped; I’ve already had Stepper dig into their systems. You won’t need to worry about anything other than the local net, and I know you can handle that.” Davie, once more returning his full attention to Lynx, nodded in the reassurance of his words.
211.	“Who are they?” Gerhardt’s voice was tight, his pronunciation clipped. The last word from his mouth sounded mechanical and pulled with aggravation.
212.	Davie knew this was part of Gerki, never paying attention but fully aware of everything about his person. “Some new chooms, eager. One of them is ex-mil; the other is some chic that fancies her chances. I spent weeks vetting both myself. They're not just clean, they're pristine, and their skills – they're a bespoke fit for this gig.”
213.	Davie momentarily and unconsciously checked Gerki for acknowledgement or response and continued, “You know it’s been hard to have reliable mercs for site work, so I’m takin’ a chance; I have to make business work.” 
214.	Gerhardt broke into Davie’s sentence. “And, how exactly is this a wrap?” His abrupt interruption hung in the air as a challenge.
215.	Gerhardt flippantly waved a hand in the general direction of Davie’s desk, still refusing to pay any attention to the little man; “you say this is clean? That says otherwise.”
216.	Davie's eyes flicked to the roughshod dossier that cluttered his desk. “That”, he pointed at the dossier, “is the last piece of a very intricate puzzle. It’s taken months to assemble, piece by precise piece. Nexacore Dynamics is just a name amongst many—red herrings.”
217.	Smiling and fumbling away the dying cigar, Davie pulled the abstract pile of documents closer; the words from his mouth were an inadequate lacquer over rotten, broken wood.
218.	“And—this is your deck-man?” Gerhardt, now half turned to indicate Lynx was the subject of his enquiry, “Do I need to add a zero on my packet? What are you doing to me, Davie?”
219.	“Gerki, I swear, this gig is preem. Every angle covered, every escape route checked twice. You know I wouldn’t stake my rep on a dud. And yes”—Davie’s gaze hardened as he gestured towards Lynx—“That's the decker I've brought on. His skills are worth every dolly, and it's my bankroll that's backing this play. I'm asking you to trust my judgement here, as I've trusted you with ops that others wouldn't touch.”
220.	Unable to contain himself, Lynx barged into the conversation; “The fuck you think I’m here for old man?” His anger was directed at Gerhardt's indifferent demeanour.  “You think you’re the shit? I could fry that arm before you even pull that damn gun, and I ain’t even got my deck!”
221.	Rising to his feet, Davie extended both arms in a calming gesture, attempting to diffuse the room's sudden escalating tension. “Gentlemen! Please. This isn’t the forum for a pissing contest. Gerki, Lynx here is the scalpel, where others are a blunt knife. Two parts of a greater machine, yes? I’ve had Stepper running this gig for fucking months in prep, it’s all under control. And, as I’m the one choosing who…”
222.	Davie's monologue was broken—his words dropping into the tension without affecting the atmosphere as the inlaid screen of his desk flashed for his attention.
223.	Davie unconsciously scanned Gerki’s face for a clue he was paying any attention and continued, “We just have to make the job work.” A definitive statement, one that drew the room to silence. A chrome-tipped finger stabbed at the flashing icon.
224.	“Boss, got a … Melly here, says she’s here to see you,” a cracked voice full of dry vocal fry declared from an unseen speaker. 
225.	“Yah, see her in, should be one more too, send ‘em both up,” Davie’s eyes sparkled; he was already counting the digital dollars, and now that his chosen team were together, it was close enough to tease him.
226.	Moving with such delicacy it might have been imperceptible, Gerhardt observed the crowd below, trying to pick out whomever these visitors were. He shifted his weight slightly—not for comfort—but a measured action to ensure his posture was ready if he needed to draw a weapon.
 
227.	The leather-padded door swung open—the suited arm that opened it stayed in place; walking past it, Mellie entered the office striding. A tousle of auburn hair fell about her shoulders; its vibrancy dulled by the dim shade of the room. A heavy, military-looking long jacket utterly consumed her slender frame. She was tall and moved with grace, but her gait was impressed upon by a weight that hardened her subtle features.
228.	As the door had opened, Lynx’s eyes were immediately captured by her presence; she was undeniably beautiful, yet an aura of sadness lingered around her. However, the colossal figure looming behind her dominated the doorway and demanded immediate attention - Garro. His massive form almost comically filled the frame, casting an imposing eclipse.
229.	At first glance, Garro appeared as a meticulous assembly of muscle and sinew, a formidable machine artfully masquerading as a man. His movements, although rigid, resonated with a deliberate, mechanical grace that magnetized every eye in the room, making him an undeniable centre of focus. Gerhardt took a moment to study both as they’d entered and, with a short pause, gave some deep consideration to the ambulatory flesh-mountain.
230.	Davie turned, greeting the two newcomers with a greasy smile that seemed to slither across his face. “Melly, Garro… Thanks for coming,” he announced, his arms gesturing warmly, inviting them into the room with a congenial flourish. Behind them, the unseen doorkeeper pulled it closed, sealing the room.
231.	Davie’s waving arm paused mid-air, his palm directing attention towards where Lynx was seated. “Lynx,” he uttered, allowing his arm to sweep further along as his head turned, marking another presence, “Gerki.” His voice carried the curt introductions through the room, each name falling like a dropped note, both complimentary and edged with a tinge of disinterest. They stood as mere acknowledgements, factual confirmations of occupancy within the enclosed space.
232.	Lynx, eyeing Garro before flickering a glance at Mellie, leaned forward. "These are the kleppers? I mean, him, I get, but her—” His voice trailed into silence as Mellie, from beneath the cover of her long, heavy coat, unveiled a massive automatic machine gun. She rested it against the desk with a formidable thud.
233.	Her eyes launched a silent, icy assault at Lynx, a subtle smile playing on her lips, wordlessly communicating that no further explanations were necessary. A palpable tension permeated the room, thick and adhesive, clinging to each surface as the weighty presence of the gun asserted itself within the space.
234.	Gerhardt took note of the interaction. Nodding to the gun and catching Mellie’s attention, “That’s an old piece of kit,” he commented, his words slicing through the awkward silence that had settled in the room.
235.	Garro shifted slightly; his stained and ragged appearance was at odds with the aggressive feigned luxury of the room he now commanded. A shoulder twitched reflexively; even now, while at rest, his body refused to relax. Beyond that twitch, a muffled mechanical hiss whispered in the quiet of the room as some concealed automated component released a jet of chemical injection. Unperturbed, he focused, trying his very best to comprehend what was going on, attempting to put together why Mellie had brought him here.
236.	Feeling the room’s atmosphere teetering on the edge of discomfort, Davie released a laugh into the thick air.
237.	“You needn’t come toting that here. We’re all on the same team”, Davie said, his eyes flickering between Lynx and Gerki as he spoke. “Let’s talk business. You care for a drink?” he clumsily grasped a decanter and the nearby glasses, dropping them to his desk and pouring out the thick, brown liquid, its consistency more reminiscent of oil than a beverage. “Please, let’s all sit. We have a lot to go over.”
Chapter Five:	Midnights Cipher.
238.	Cool winds clawed at their exposed skin with welcome chill against the dying heat of the day as they lingered in the embrace of darkness. The day's heat soaked in the concrete of the city and warmed it from below, but at this lofty height, the cold was an invasive presence, infiltrating the layers of their thick coats with an unyielding chill. Garro, clad merely in a tattered shirt, stood defiant against the harsh elements, seemingly unaffected by the cruel temperament of the night.
239.	The rough-hewn VTOL buzzed away into the obscurity of the night, leaving Mellie watching with a frown, her features creasing. It felt strangely inadequate to be left in such a manner, yet Davie had been unequivocal in his instructions. It made sense, of course; an inconspicuous, grubby-looking VTOL loitering near a towering high-rise in the heart of Central was sure to attract unwelcome scrutiny.
240.	Although it was approaching 1 a.m., the sky around them radiated with the glow from the city below. They found themselves amidst an unfamiliar spectacle, perched upon an architectural titan that soared with grotesque grandeur into the heavens. The edifice they now stood upon was colossal, rivalled only by a handful of similarly towering structures.
241.	The drone of the disappeared VTOL had gone; they were left only with the relentless whistle of the wind and the vibrations that hummed in the taut cables that tethered several antennae to their shared roof space.
242.	Lynx, driven by a natural curiosity, leaned over the edge, his gaze following the contours of the building as it descended. The structure wasn’t a sheer precipice; it protruded and curved outward as its face fell away. Far below, specks that glittered like diamonds moved about—affluent inhabitants of the Upper Central District, seemingly unaffected by the late hour.
243.	The Central District never rested; short of an apocalypse, nothing could halt its machinations. In every moment, every hour of every day, the interwoven corporations endlessly vied for control. Suits shook hands, buyouts, mergers and dissolutions blurring into a festering sickness of hyper-capitalism that, from its absolute power, scarred every facet of the city.
244.	Gerhardt, arguably the best dressed among them for this particular escapade, wore a long ebony coat over a tightly fitting ensemble of tactical gear. His gaze was fixed northward, towards the opulent abodes of Upper Central, where the corporate wage slaves resided, blissfully ignorant of the world outside their gilded cages. The city disgusted him. People disgusted him.
245.	Choosing to disregard the sprawling cityscape, Mellie focused on the inky rooftop expanse and the colossal skylight beside them. The previous night, the plan had been discussed at great length and in excruciating detail; its logic was impeccable. The outer doors, accessible only via the balcony, had dual-tier security, only one of which had been neutralized by Stepper, Davie's adept Netrunner.
246.	The second layer was intrinsically connected to the building's centralised security grid, to which even Stepper had found no connection. It had been assumed by all who looked over the plans that this redundancy also applied to the balcony windows. There were numerous external access points, but the responsibility of scrutinizing the local network fell upon Lynx. Mellie's attention was fixed on the formidable panes of security glass beneath her feet.
247.	Having located a hardline connection point and haphazardly spliced a connection for himself, Lynx was immersed in the task of dissecting the digital entrails of the penthouse's security system. He tapped and pawed at the visual controls of his deck, its displays glowing and flickering in the near-total darkness of the rooftop.
248.	Remarkably, He was making rapid progress despite the intricate complexity of the system. He had never encountered such sophisticated architecture before. Yet, with the instructions passed by Stepper, it felt strangely natural to him, like solving an intricate puzzle on a grander scale. With a casual flick of his thumb, he interrupted his focus. “Seems there’s some internal movement sensors. I got a Demon on it though”.
249.	Garro, silent until now and allowing the group—who all seemed proficient at their tasks—to go about their business, looked quizzically at the younger man. “So, we good to get in then?”
250.	Gerhardt responded, preempting Lynx, “We should check the place for physical triggers. A place like this has to have more than just some shitty software keeping it on lockdown.”
251.	Perhaps it was the confidence in his delivery, but without verbal agreement, all except Garro began a systematic search.
252.	A fire escape door loomed on the rooftop, flanked by massive air conditioning units. Nearby, radio antennas vibrated with eerie resonance in the persistent night breeze. Yet, despite their thorough inspection, no hidden surprises were unearthed.
253.	Lynx’s attention shifted towards the skylight, breaking the monotony. "I think I can trip the latches there, probably on some kind of override," he said, pointing down through the skylight at what appeared to be the electronic systems controlling the massive window.
254.	The chilling night air was becoming increasingly biting, amplifying Garro’s impatience. “Gotta be an easier way,” he protested; a questioning look flashed at Lynx, who was once again engrossed in the dazzle of lights flickering from his deck.
255.	"Garro," Mellie interjected, her voice soothingly reassuring, “Just let the guy work. We’ll be in and out in no time. It looks quiet in there, and if all the security is down, we just have to walk in, grab the damn data-pack, call for Davie’s guy, we’ll be in a bar drinking before the sun’s up.”
256.	“I got it”, Lynx's voice chimed in enthusiastically, answering an unasked question. "Well, I will have it. Just need to nudge the actuator."
257.	Catching everyone off guard, Lynx pulled a miniature glass cutter micro-laser from his pack, setting it against the glass and linking it to his deck.
258.	"You had that all along?" Gerhardt queried, acknowledging the tiny device that now emitted a high-pitched whine as it meticulously cut a minuscule aperture in the formidable pane.
259.	Lynx beamed, pleased with the unexpected attention and relishing his sudden importance within the group. “I like to make sure I have the tools I need”, he declared, patting his well-travelled satchel. “Not all of us get everything handed on a plate; gotta make our own way.”
260.	Gerhardt's face subtly transformed, a flicker of something that, in the low light, could almost be mistaken for admiration. It was a rare concession from the typically stoic man, silently acknowledging Lynx’s ingenuity. He adjusted his jacket collar against the penetrating wind. His gaze once again drifted northward towards the glittering facades of the glass-fronted apartments.
261.	As the window gave way with a soft, zipping sound, the pane shifted, the weight of the glass making itself known. Lynx retracted his arm carefully through the slender opening he had created. "All we need to do now is lift this up..."
262.	Seizing the moment, Gerhardt began to assert control, "Each of us can take a corner and—"
263.	His words were abruptly interrupted. Having restrained his patience until now, Garro had finally reached his threshold. Seizing the window pane, using the new aperture as an improvised handle, he heaved, his wide shoulders giving no impression of strained effort as he lifted the entire pane from its frame, the surrounding metal edging protesting with groans and creaks as it relinquished its hold.
264.	Mellie watched in awe, her eyes widening at the display of raw power. She had always known of Garro's potency, but witnessing him manhandle a pane of security glass that likely outweighed her by a substantial margin was something else entirely.
265.	She watched, almost in disbelief, as Garro, with a sense of casual disdain, strode confidently to the edge of the rooftop. With a powerful swing of his arms, he launched the massive sheet of high-tensile security glass into the night sky, discarding it with as much effort and thought as one might dispose of an empty juice carton.
266.	Garro looked among the others as though the entire transaction was just a function of normalcy. "Can we get in there now?" he grumbled, his voice a low rumble that resonated with the empty night air.
267.	Gerhardt unconsciously relinquished a new flicker of surprise to cross his face. His still human eye momentarily widened – a subtle concession to the unexpected display of raw power.  With a small, almost mocking nod, he gestured towards the open skylight, the corners of his mouth turning up in a semblance of a courteous smile, “After you.”
 
268.	Garro descended the ropes first, his fall ending clumsily with a grunt as he hit the ground. Gerhardt followed closely, but as Garro scrambled to recover, he sent Gerhardt tumbling from the rope, hopping and skidding to catch his balance.
269.	Above them, Mellie now lay perched, looking into the open skylight, adjusting the bipod of her gun and pulling it into her shoulder. Even now, even here, the routine and motion blinked a brief memory across her mind’s eye of days spent with her father. She refocused and tweaked the small rear sight to better see the layout below her.
270.	Lynx, having waited for the mess of bodies to clear away from the drop ropes, carefully began his descent. Garro provided more than a little physical assistance.
271.	The penthouse loomed like a darkened cathedral of wealth, its grandeur and opulence only more pronounced in the absence of light.
272.	The polished granite floors sheened protest to what little illumination snuck in from the city outside, making them appear like an undisturbed lake. The smooth plaster walls, bereft of light, were now a dappled shadow canvas that magnified a sense of isolation.
273.	Furniture pieces, each a statement of minimalist design, sat in the gloom like monoliths in a deserted landscape. Sculptural leather couches seemed even less inviting in the dark, their stark forms blending into the surrounding shadows, daring anyone to disrupt their untouched state.
274.	The room felt like an art exhibit closed off for the night—impressive but entirely devoid of life or warmth, its severe angles and clean lines exaggerated by the lack of illumination.
275.	Garro watched as Gerhardt stealthily began to navigate deeper into the wide, open-planned apartment, feeling awkward in his comparative bulk. Seeing that the condo was quite wonderous, he too went, somewhat less stealthily into its shadows.
276.	The space into which they had fallen was a designed expanse of open plan neat formality. Away from the glass wall of windows, a hallway led deeper into the dark space, its flanks perfunctory portals to the other parts of the penthouse.
277.	The trio—each untested and lacking a shared history—embarked on diverging paths in their initial search for the data-pack.
278.	Despite a slight shiver, Mellie remained focused, her mind on her task. She held a commanding position, looking down into the penthouse and into the head of the central corridor. Teasing memories of instruction reminded her to maintain vigilance, thinking to herself: Good cover must be aware.
279.	Lynx migrated to the northernmost corner of the penthouse into a vast, layered lounge. The space felt empty, a mockery of effort from the artwork on the walls. The area was dominated by a faux-stonework, expansive fireplace that served as a central visual element. He found the extravagance of the apartment excessive but couldn’t help but imagine the luxury of inhabiting such a space.
280.	A crimson dot toyingly flashed on his arm bracer—a more easy interface to his deck while he moved about—there was a system that wasn’t offline, and his automated programs were having trouble isolating it. A more direct internal connection point was necessary.
281.	Garro had all but forgotten why they were even here. Instead, He had wandered into the kitchen, sampling various inviting items; one, particularly meaty, caught his interest, and he pocketed the remainder for later. The lavish surroundings enveloped him in a fantasy, so grand was the contrast to the cold, unforgiving streets that served as his bed. Here, amidst luxury, he was a world away from the harsh reality of his homelessness, allowing himself to become momentarily enraptured by the illusion of grandeur and comfort.
282.	Gerhardt moved with deliberate intent, methodically dismissing sections of the penthouse. A trio of rooms along the southern perimeter caught his attention, compelling a more thorough inspection. His path was resolute, unswayed by the less focused actions of the others.
283.	It came as a shock to Gerhardt to find himself dizzily looking at the impeccable ceiling and feeling the raw warmth of a fresh and deep bruise on his ribs.
284.	Lynx’s voice punctured the silence, a note of alarm ringing clear. “Guys, watch out, I think—” His warning trailed off, cascading into an uncharacteristically shrill “Oh shit.” as he abandoned his deck, seeking refuge behind the lavish furnishings.
285.	A security droid had blindsided Gerhardt, the impact hurling him along the corridor with brute force. Mellie caught the horrifying sight of his head brutally meeting the polished concrete floor, scantily shielded by a thin carpet. Her grip tightened around her weapon, the room echoing with her anxious call, “Guys…?” But her voice drowned in the chaos.
286.	The droid, a stubby little can with thin pneumatic arms, rested on three small but robust wheels; one of its arms was a furious static of a taser extension; the other, a clasping array of inoffensive manipulators. It had no head to speak of, but the front sheet of its armour had a grouped array of sensors and was adorned by corporate logos, enough that it could be called a face.
287.	A light on the droid blinked in a parody of authority as it issued a static-laced declaration:《Intruders must vacate premises. Authorities have been notified.》A shrill klaxon punctuated its words, reverberating through the hard surfaces of the penthouse on a loop of robotic diligence.
288.	Garro, alerted by the commotion, burst out of the bedroom door, brandishing an expensive-looking bottle of liquor in one hand. With a deep-rooted instinct borne from ruthless training, he bellows and lunges at the robot, his massive hand arcing through the air, aiming to meet the metal menace in a clash of force.
289.	The machine's reflexes outpaced his assault. It spun, deploying the taser, catching the man-mountain as he landed a crushing fist into the machine with such force that its rubber wheels screeched in protest. A flash of electric blue and a puff of smoke sent Garro jerking into the opposite wall.
290.	Only the weight of the thing keeps it from toppling completely as it struggles to maintain balance. Seizing the fleeting opportunity, Gerhardt sprung into a swift, reflexive roll. His body unfurled fluidly into a crouch.
291.	In the same movement, he draws his oversized hand cannon of a pistol. As the droid bounced from the wall against which Garro's fist had flung it, a heart-stopping blast echoed as he dispatched a perfectly aimed shot, the bullet roaring through the droid’s armour with contemptuous ease, leaving the wall beyond marred by a fierce, melon-sized cavity.
292.	The melee of the brawl below had drawn events into Mellie’s view. The robot, still reeling from the dual assault, was in her sights. She pulled the trigger. The bucking and vibration of her gun chattered a cacophony into what was the echoed silence after Gerhardt’s shot.
293.	Below her, there was a spray of oil and sparks as the humble security droid rendered a masterpiece of devastation. It succumbed to the ferocious onslaught, relinquishing its form to a chaotic dance of disassembly.
294.	The ringing silence that followed reverberated in all of their ears. The lazy smoke of Mellie's gun barrel wisped away in the cold rooftop breeze.
295.	“Uhhh…” Lynx’s quiet voice attempted to gain purchase. “it didn’t really get a call out… So, we're okay, I think.”
296.	Slowly rising to his feet, Gerhardt let his cybernetic hand automatically re-holster his pistol. His still human arm cradled his ribs, “What the hell, you little shit! This place was supposed to be locked down.”
297.	Gurgling—Garro claws himself upright, blinking; a tiny inaudible hiss sounded as something automated within his back released chemicals that would displace pain.
298.	“You clear?” Mellie hollered into the skylight from above, her question hanging in the air.
299.	“Fuck me,” Gerhardt muttered, his voice layered with poorly hidden disdain. “Fucking Davie, fucking amateurs,” he quietly uttered to himself, raising his voice again; “Yeah, we're fucking clear.”
300.	“That wasn’t my fault”, Lynx defensively announced to the mess of noise everyone was making.
301.	Garro—with peculiar indifference to everything that had passed and that was now happening—picked up the bottle he’d dropped, raising it to show; “Look what I found,” he announced, casually strolling back to the lounge and delivering a dismissive kick to the smouldering remains of the droid.
302.	Gerhardt turning to face Lynx fully, spat cold interrogation at him, “There’s no more of this shit, right?... Tell me this place is tight now, please, for the love of sweet mercy, tell me you are actually in control of this shit!?”
303.	“Chill, I got this. That wasn’t internal. I managed to cut the receivers before its broadcast. But that wasn’t on me.” Lynx's tone was defensive, seeking absolution. “I’ll run some ICE, okay, but I already checked, there’s no signals in or out. No more surprises.” His eyes pleaded amnesty in the unlit gloom.
304.	“Right, no more fuck ups.” Gerhardt's voice was icy; his face had returned to an indifference of placidity. He paused as if waiting for affirmation, then preempted any further delay. "Oh-kay?"
305.	Lynx nodded, his eyes widening in a mixture of assurance and submission.
