306.	“You,” Gerhardt points at Garro. “Stop jerking about; we should be gone already. Find the fucking data-pack.”
307.	Confused and somewhat aggrieved at being yelled at after potentially saving the guy's life, Garro shot a questioning glance towards Mellie for support. He turned to retrace his steps, rubbing a burned patch of his shirt, feeling the sting of raw, burned flesh beneath. Another silent hiss follows him as he pulls his mind into the present and wonders where to start looking for the thing.
308.	Gerhardt had turned to re-approach his original destination, passing the ruined security droid and striding to the door furthest from their entry at the back of the corridor. He twists the handle, feeling its tight refusal; his cybernetic arm shifts a panel from about itself, and the built-in micro-manipulators extend like arachnid legs to automatically pick the mechanical lock.
309.	Gerhardt swings the door open. He draws in a sharp breath and exhales slowly in repressed surprise. “Lynx, I think I might need your help,” he calls.
310.	The room before him is swamped and adorned in every inch by complex interlinked computer hardware, each piece humming or blinking in passive activity.  The technological jumble feels startlingly incongruent in the otherwise ostentatious and sparse corporate apartment.
311.	“What? I got everything off, only meat-space locks left.” Lynx's voice responds from a distance.
312.	“No, I mean- come here.” Gerhardt huffed indignancy from his nostrils.
313.	“The hell you want me to…” Lynx stopped in his tracks. A moment of flooding recognition gives way as he continues, “…Ah, I see.”
314.	“So, it’s got to be in here, right?” Gerhardt's cybernetic eye scans the room. Though the embedded technology can't directly identify the data-pack, it does help map out the room's details, leaving his human faculties to fill in the gaps.
315.	“Woah, that’s a Yantiri nine-k”, Lynx exclaims, momentarily distracted by the hardware before him. He quickly regains composure. “Uh, well, makes sense it’d be here. Look at this place…”
316.	“There, what’s that?” Gerhardt points cautiously at a panel of facias, all blinking except for one with a front-loading slot. Certainly, a data-pack would fit there? He thought as he left the question to Lynx.
317.	Lynx, stepping over a tangle of wide-bore connecting cables, approaches the bank of circuits and blinking lights; as he surveys the room, in doing so, he quizzes silently in his mind: There’s no screens or neural tabs anywhere. What the fuck is this? It was a peculiarity for a Netrunners nest like this not to have any interface at all.
318.	Hitting a panel release button—a few adjacent lights blink in acknowledgement. The panel with the front-loading slot slowly ejects a blank, shoebox-sized object. Save for its connectors, its black exterior is entirely nondescript.
319.	“Oh yeah”, Lynx’s voice rolled with excitement; Davie had been vague about the data-pack's appearance, but given their exhaustive search and the room's obvious significance, this must be their target. “This is it; we need to get the fuck out of here…”
320.	Gerhardt had already spun from the room and was yelling down the corridor, “Mellie, get on the com. We need a ride!”
 
321.	The air in the penthouse seemed to hold its breath as Gerhardt's cybernetic eye scanned the scene. Ignoring the obvious, he searched for tiny details that might have been enough to trace them. His eye cycled through different bands of light, ensuring they had left no traceable element of their presence.
322.	Garro's footsteps softly thudded through the spacious room as he gave one final, longing look at the sprawling cityscape beyond the floor-to-ceiling windows. His grip tightened around the stolen bottle of liquor—a memento of their successful escapade—He hauled his massive form up the drop rope.
323.	Mellie had already made swift work of packing her gun together, a makeshift carry-bundle of metal death. She turned to see the VTOL swinging in close. Perhaps this was going to be easy money after all, she affirmed to herself.
324.	Lynx, his eyes still reflecting the thrill of their victory, studied the penthouse one last time. He had been a part of something larger than himself – a team, a plan, a daring heist. The memories of this night would forever shape him.
325.	As they each made their way to the rooftop and, in quick order, began boarding the waiting aircraft, the biting wind greeted them, a begrudged familiar acquaintance shocking them back into reality.
326.	Mellie paused at the rooftop's edge, her eyes tracing the zig-zagged trails of streets below. The city teemed with life, its neon veins alive with energy. It was a picture of contradictions —both beautiful and perilous—and tonight; she was most literally on top of it all.
327.	Settling into a seat aboard the VTOL, Lynx clutched the data-pack tightly. It symbolized their unity and purpose. He took in the moment, shedding a smile at each of his newfound companions; Mellie was reaching to pull herself in, Gerhart stared out to the middle distance outside the transport, and Garro grinned back, sharing his moment of triumph.
328.	"Time to go," Mellie announced, fighting back the drone of the engines, her voice a blend of resolve and relief. "Let's get out of here before anyone catches on." She stepped onto the waiting VTOL; the engine hummed a promise of freedom.
329.	Lynx's infectious grin lit up his face as he glanced out the window. The penthouse they had infiltrated disappeared from view, becoming another fleeting memory in the sprawling cityscape. With a surge of power, the VTOL lifted into the sky.
330.	As they soared above the neon-drenched streets, it felt like the city called to wrap them in its embrace. Mellie stared at the horizon; a medley of emotions swelled within her. They were outlaws, dissidents, a thorn to prick the corporate skin—her first notch in a fight only she could win.
331.	The penthouse became a distant speck in the spanning horizon of buildings; the nocturnal wind tousled their hair as they embarked on their journey; they vanished into the night, their fates now intertwined with the city below.
Chapter Six:	Echoes of Triumph.
332.	Amidst the muted, repeating sounds of the city outside, among the smells of high-carb indiscriminate foodstuffs and the chatter of the other patrons, the luxury of the penthouse felt like a distant dream, too far displaced from what life now was; another afternoon in a diner for the forgotten.
333.	Lost in thought—Mellie's gaze was drawn to the raindrops as they meandered down the grease-matted window, looking out over a city street adorned with graffiti and litter, a few people dashing in the downpour.
334.	Davie had made good on his promise; a substantial deposit of digital dollars now cushioned her account, a temporary reprieve against life's uncertainties.
335.	The warm petrichor scent of rain-washed streets wafted in through the slightly ajar window, a subtle reminder that even amidst the urban cacophony, nature's touch persisted.
336.	Garro's argument with a vendor filtered into her consciousness, pulling her from her introspection. His voice had raised in a blend of accusation and confusion, a rough melody that fell away into the background noise of traffic from outside.
337.	Mellie's fingers idly traced the rim of her cup, where the last dregs of Insta-Caf had cooled. The bitter brew was a comfort, a small indulgence, equal parts enjoyment and endurance. 
338.	The afterglow of their triumphant heist had been punctuated by the ecstasy of weeks of parties that bled into the early hours—good memories born from a city that often left its inhabitants devoid of genuine experiences.
339.	Garro's approach, one hand covered in a smouldering grease, shattered her contemplation. His fist was gripped around an amalgam of several different lumps of sauce-dripping meat. “That guy was trying to rip me off.” His soft, low voice cowed from the social misunderstandings he had bullied through.
340.	"Did you even pay for those?" Mellie asked, the question lifted by exasperation and mild amusement.
341.	Garro's grumbling response was barely discernible through a mouthful of meat, a sight that would have been laughable if not for her growing understanding of his complexities.
342.	“I got no cash.” Garro, the art of conversation lost on him; he bluntly responded around the open-mouthed chew of something from his meat-platter fist.
343.	Despite the windfall of digital dollars that had recently graced their accounts, Garro had found a way to end up penniless.
344.	It was a disheartening realization. As soon as the funds had cleared, he'd made a beeline for a handful of underground medical practitioners, colloquially known as ‘rippers’. Mellie had initially assumed he was buying narcotics, a not uncommon diversion in their world.
345.	However, she had learned otherwise through sporadic conversations over the weeks they'd spent together. The medication was specialized, typically available only to military personnel—unless you knew the right people.
346.	Garro's past as an infantryman had exposed him to experimental procedures conducted by the military-industrial conglomerate Asukaga. In a gamble to augment their soldiers, they had replaced nearly his entire spine and made extensive modifications to his shoulders and legs. 
347.	Most of the alterations were internal, but underneath that ragged shirt, his back was a patchwork of exposed metal and angry red flesh.
348.	Whatever the reasons, the surgeries weren’t a total success, and he was discharged from the military. No longer was the cost of investment worth their attention—a failure to be discarded.
349.	It left him in a perpetual state of agony that was controlled and kept at bay only by liberal and continued injections of those very specific pain-blocking meds, an impasse of unhappiness, no longer able to legally get what he needed most. They had let him go, likely expecting him to die, certainly paying no heed or showing any remorse to the human that they had mutilated and spat out.
350.	Mellie's gaze lingered on him, her empathy unspoken but deeply felt. His story resonated with her, conjuring memories of her father, pulling at her gut. She wondered, what would he have done if I’d not found him?
351.	From Garro's perspective—blissfully dampened by the medication that sustained him—life was a blur. The drugs numbed not just his physical pain but his emotional and cognitive faculties as well, acting as a chemical veil that shielded him from the full weight of his existence.
352.	Mellie drew her thoughts along; it’s probably best for him, were he more aware he’d likely turn cyber-psycho in a moment.
353.	As she watched him chew, Mellie's heart ached with pity and understanding. His resilient ignorance in the face of adversity was both admirable and heartbreaking. The meds he so heavily relied on were a double-edged sword, numbing not just his physical agony but also his connection to the world around him; he could never truly ever be himself again.
354.	Though muted by his condition, Garro's presence was a reminder that even amidst the city's darkness, genuine souls could still be found. She had grown fond of the big lunk and was happy to share a little time—a drink, a meal, a brief respite from their complex lives.
355.	The rain outside continued its steady descent, a companion to Mellie's contemplation. She realized that beneath the excitement of their exploits – beneath the neon-lit nights and shadow of the mega-corps – each of them was an individual fighting their own battles.
356.	With its towering structures and endless hustle, the city served as an extension of their lives—beautiful yet brutal, intricate yet chaotic.
357.	Garro smiled a toothy, sauce-smeared smile; his eyes were happy. She returned the smile, but it stirred a mix of emotions within Mellie – a profound sadness for his plight and recognition of the fragile beauty of the person hidden away inside.
 
358.	The makeshift surgery room was saturated with the acrid smell of burnt metal, mingled with the sterile bite of antiseptic. A viewscreen on the wall silently displayed an unfamiliar entertainment show, its edges cycling through scrolling advertisements. Though not a cramped space, the room was dominated by a large surgical chair and its accompanying machinery, leaving little room for comfort.
359.	Gerhardt's eyes lingered on the dark, carbon-laced casing that now shielded his newly augmented arm. It was a luxury, no doubt, but an investment he deemed necessary for his continued survival.
360.	The tiny sparks of the graft machine fixing in place the last few sealing clasps were dazzling stars in the turgid brown surroundings of the somewhat cramped, very interpretively titled ‘surgery’.
361.	The sparks burst in the cold fluorescent light that shone on his arm. It cast a harsh, backlit silhouette of the man—crouched over him as he lay on the technologically advanced reclining chair that served as the operating table—in this illicit clinic of a ripper doc.
362.	The surgeon was a wiry Asian man with a head more machine than flesh. His cranium was a marvel of intersecting layers of optical enhancements, each more intricate than the last. Snaking cables arced from the back of his neck and forearms, indicating that the real work was being done through a digital interface rather than human hands. His nano-molecular adjustments could not be executed with biological dexterity alone.
363.	Gerhardt breathed, forcing relaxation, letting the octopus of a machine and its host finish their work. His newly modified arm would include a smart-link hardline connection, giving him a critical edge, especially in handling firearms.
364.	It was a strange sensation but not unpleasant; he could ‘feel’ a new tendon, as if it had always been there. Moreover, he knew flexing that tendon would jut out the connector head of the smart-link cable from its concealment.
365.	The integration was invasive, but to Gerhardt, the fusion of technology and flesh made him feel more complete. He had already tested the software that now merged with his thoughts; the connection linked his cybernetic eye's data with his aim, allowing him to intricately read combat scenarios with a precision that exceeded anything a wholly human marksman could achieve.
366.	In the cutthroat world he navigated, this kind of edge could mean the difference between life and death.
367.	The arm wasn't just an indulgence; Gerhardt knew that well. His eyes took on a faraway look, contemplating the dire circumstances that had led to this expensive modification. He had intended on using the cash Davie had paid him for that heist job to help tally against a looming debt—an ephemeral portents—that had been dogging his steps for months.
368.	The criminal underworld in Little China, a southwestern district of Akureyri City, was as unsubtle as it was menacing. He'd already received a 'friendly' visit from a group of tattooed thugs in ill-fitting suits, a reminder he could ill afford to forget.
369.	He knew the next visit would be far less congenial. The threat was a constant, gnawing presence, a variable he couldn't control that added uncertainty to a life he had crafted to safely maintain.
370.	Gerhardt was a man accustomed to dealing in violence, trading pain for profit. He would happily split lips, break bones or even outright kill other people, all so he could subsidise his own life. Money for pain – that was his whole world.
371.	He went about his business with chilling detachment, never pausing to consider the suffering he inflicted—often finding he indulged in his work with a predatory pleasure, savouring the dominance over life and death. However, the thought of others, just as merciless, turning the tables on him pulled bile to his guts. He was staring into a mocking mirror—reflecting an uncomfortable glare on his existence—a rare crack in the facade of his unyielding psyche.
372.	The job he undertook with Davie had, by way of proffered payment, granted him breathing room. But he knew it was only a matter of time before the debt collectors would return, more insistent than before. They likely wouldn’t accept another placative offering of instalments. A full settlement was out of the question. This new arm wasn't just an upgrade; it was a necessity. Fixers would often pay more for wet work. And he needed more, and quickly.
373.	《you’ll need to make sure you only use that with a type three interface connector》
374.	"Neen yoh beekakku, shee-yong nee deh hei Sankey jeemeyan Lyan-jee-ki "
375.	The two languages were spoken in a jumble, mediated by an auto-translator. The surgeon had spoken in a variant of Japanese, typical of the residents in the area known locally as Sheaow Jon-Gwo. A synthesized English version was interlaced with the original dialogue, its robotic timbre clear enough to be understood.
376.	Gerhardt blinked, his focus coming back into the room.
377.	"Ohr wah-Itta, jhee shee-yong Lyan-jee-ki heh Sankey jee-myen, samon-ah kereh-bah nee ken-yeng shao-hwai dong-shee."
378.	《I said- only use the connector with a type three interface, or you’d probably fry something. 》
379.	The irate-looking ripper’s instructions guided attention to his pointing at the smart connector port on the arm.
380.	Nodding, Gerhardt let his mind twist into the neuro-controllers, his fingers flexed within the grasp of his new hand, its digital tensile feedback already feeling almost second nature.
381.	As the ripper disengaged the grafting rig – a surge of satisfaction washed over Gerhardt. The arm wasn't just a piece of machinery; it was a promise, a tool that could tip the scales of fate in his favour.
382.	"Ahree-gahtoh, sen-say. Wah-tah-she noh sheer-yoh-koo wah aye-mah-sen, nee jyen luh mah?" Gerhardt's less-than-perfect pronunciation elicited a frown from the ripper. The ripper tapped the implanted band at their neck—their auto-translator—but the thanks were clearly understood.
383.	The ripper, withdrawing the electronic vipers that connected him to his tools, replied, "Shi de, shi hao-deh, keh-shih neen yoh genghuan neide neyjing jingpian zai bujiu, dan dianlu pian haishi zuijia deh. Ruguo neen you shijue wending, keneng shi neen de naozi bu zenmeyang…”
384.	《Yes, it's fine. You might need a new inner lens soon, but the chips are in top form. If you've got visual distortion, it could just be your brain,》the synthesized voice of the translator relayed.
385.	The automated voice failed to capture the ripper's dialect-infused hidden insult. Unaware of the slight, Gerhardt simply nodded, taking the general advice at face value.
386.	Gerhardt rose from the elaborate surgical chair; in doing so, plucking away a set of connections that had been inserted in the top of his spine where machine merged with flesh and was woven into his brain stem, they automatically released and whirled like injured worms retreating into sod as they retracted into the chair. 
387.	Time to find a reason to put this to the test, he thought to himself. As if summoned by his thoughts, his implanted agent buzzed—someone was trying to contact him on an encrypted line.
388.	Gerhardt brought up the caller's details on the screen projected within his cyber-eye. A smile crept across his face. Fortuitous timing, Davie, he thought, accepting the call and throwing a cred-stick at the ripper.
Chapter Seven:	The Maw of the City.
389.	The city was a drone of noise, often indicative of which part of the sprawling concrete you happened to be within; it was a noise that was there without fail. The buzz of motors and traffic, the hum of chatter amongst people, an occasional distant siren – an audible tear shed by the city in its pained endurance of the lives of millions of people, all thronging in their individual shared existences.
390.	Among that unceasing mass of concrete, glass, metal and meat, the mishap cohort of Mellie, Lynx and Garro drifted, akin to detritus atop a meandering river, carried along by the larger currents of life. Spring had marched by; the monotony of hot, sticky days throbbed the passage of time—grey and beige, grey and beige, grey and beige.
391.	The trio had yet to find a compelling reason to seek another job. The heat sapping any desire for more than casually enjoying a cool drink.
392.	Lynx still had credits to spend, and his enthusiasm had pulled them along in a vortex. Opting to forego another day sat in some dive in the Skrim, Garro and Mellie now accompanied Lynx as he headed to a club frequented by like-minded tech-enthusiasts, one his usual lack of cash precluded his presence.
393.	As they walked, Lynx's eyes sparkled with excitement. Oblivious to the lack of understanding from his companions, he animatedly delved into the intricacies of hacking programs, system-routing functions, and his own plans for bending the city's technology to his will.
394.	Garro's attention was elsewhere, captivated by the mundane details of daily life around them. His gaze followed a woman in a bright red and puffy jacket walking oddly down the street. Such simple scenes fascinated him—people going about their lives without the looming threat of danger. The thought lingered in his mind, a stark contrast to his reality.
395.	Mellie, by default, became the recipient of Lynx's overflowing excitement.
396.	“I heard one guy there had access to an ICE that can fry everything, from your deck to your brain. And, if some choom was jacked in, that’s it! Poof! But, it's not like I need that; I probably couldn’t blow the creds to get it either.” Lynx's voice was a rapid, energetic stream, almost too loud for the public setting.
397.	"Uh-huh," Mellie managed to insert, unable to keep up with Lynx's technical jargon.
398.	“Davie said I needed to get some tracer’s see, and there’s some net-head on the bibbies says he has a Mastiff program for sale, would be a damn sight better than my Data-Raven.” Lynx continued, still not pausing for breath.
399.	Mellie understood some of the terminologies, but most of it flew over her head. She had made a conscious decision long ago to avoid any kind of chrome – and certainly never have any brain-embedded computer programs. Her father had been obligated to have such implants for his job, and it was that very technology that had led corporate enforcers straight to him.
400.	Mellie was so engrossed in Lynx's relentless stream of tech-speak that she failed to notice the two Cit-Sec officers ahead, even though their red-hazard-striped black uniforms stood out ominously in the bright light of the languid cloudless day. It was already too late as the trio crossed the intersection.
401.	"Hey, you, big guy..." an officer called out, singling out Garro from the crowd.
402.	If the black-clad uniforms stood out, Garro was an even more prominent feature among the otherwise normative bustle of pedestrians. The passing footfall of hurried workers and dejected solemn-faced denizens was broken by his giant frame and tatty oil-stained clothes. Around him, a wake of humans shifted their flow. More than enough to draw attention from Cit-Sec—ever vigilant for any they deemed undesirable in the more discerning sections of the city.
403.	“You, Stop. Hey, big guy…” The officer demanded as he reached to pull at Garro’s plateau of a shoulder. But before the command could fully escape his lips, Garro moved with unexpected speed. A mechanically augmented punch sent the officer, fully laden with tactical gear, flying into the roadside gutter.
404.	Around them, bystanders stared in silent disbelief, momentarily frozen by the shocking turn of events. Lynx’s hyperbole had ceased, and Mellie froze – her brain doing its best to catch up with time and quantify what was happening.
405.	The immediacy of that pause shattered as the shouts of “Cyber Psycho! Officer down!!” exploded from the remaining Cit-Sec officer, his urgent voice thrust into their shared awareness. Across the street, a woman screamed.
406.	Mellie spun in a moment that took a lifetime, turning to Garro, her arms extended in reflex to push him into a door well.
407.	Lynx had already flung himself against the side of the nearby Cit-Sec vehicle behind which, on the opposite side, the officer now slammed his flat palm against an uncovered red button.
408.	Garro didn’t shift. He looked down at Mellie and then back to the crumpled form of the officer he had launched. Cold instinct flooded his body and took over; he scanned for the other officer. Every muscle that had lain dormant now fired, creasing him into a buckled form of unrealised tension as his scowling eyes hunted.
409.	Mellie bounced off of Garro’s chest; her efforts to move what appeared to be a building sent her barrelling past him as her momentum carried her, attempting to eject herself from the immediate scene.
410.	All about them now, the raucous din of the fleeing public as they too now had their routine diverted with the undeniable cognition of the most terrifying words any ordinary citizen within the city could hear spoken aloud.
411.	Time collapsed – a pointless endeavour. Everything was now happening at the same moment, merged together in a mess of dissonant events; Garro, unable to immediately see his new target, stepped into the street; The back of the black wagon unfolded; Lynx was pulled sharply backwards; Mellie’s feet each wanted to be in front of the other.
412.	Garro watched in astonishment as the unfolding mechanical undulation of the Cit-Sec vehicle stopped looking like the back of a wagon—and started looking a lot more like a response droid. Heavy armour plates clicking and shifting into position to surround its skeletal design in boxes of impenetrable shielding.
413.	Lynx didn’t have time to yell. In swift motion, a gloved hand hauled him by his jacket collar, displacing him entirely. His voice left his mouth as a heavy vehicle door slammed shut behind him; he and his pack landed as a tangled bundle, slumped in the back of the Cit-Sec wagon.
414.	The response droid had taken only a matter of seconds to come to rest on its tripod of feet, a hunched head of sensors and lenses protruding forward, and at each of its flanks, it wore a bustle of automatic firepower.
415.	A Cyber-Psycho was not something to be toyed with; extreme prejudice and overwhelming firepower were standing orders for anyone in the security services, and right now—Garro had been assigned that title.
416.	The droid's speed defied its size as if it moved too fast. Its programming was far quicker than human reactions, and it already sought to isolate its target; even Cit-Sec, with all the control they exerted, didn’t like the blowback of newsfeeds filled with stories of innocents caught in a crossfire. Even if they had no genuine regard for the well-being of the citizens, they at least had to hold the apparent veneer of not mindlessly killing just anyone.
417.	In the shedding moments, coming to an abrupt realisation that this was too much even for him, Garro spun as though a delay of Mellie’s earlier physical prompting had reached his consciousness. He half-stepped, half-jumped into the apparent shelter of the recessed doorway.
418.	Mellie, having gone far enough now to realise there were no footsteps following, certainly not the notable sound of Garro running, turned to see the droid positioning into the centre of the road at the head of the nearby junction.
419.	Situations can go bad sometimes. It’s just a fact that you take on board when you decide to become a mercenary or a runner. But this was not just a bad situation. It was the worst situation.
420.	Mellie pulled from an inner holster under her coat the small handheld machine-pistol she carried for emergencies. This definitely ticked off as an emergency. She spat bullets at the droid – too late.
421.	The droid hefted its spiney, boxy form to face Garro; the tiny guns disgorged a buzz of energy; multiple barrels each vomited a slew of caseless bullets.
422.	The wall about Garro disintegrated into puffs of dust and shrapnel; the windows beside him and behind him smashed and fell to glitter. Brick dust created a cloud about him and the sparks of bullets striking metal span starlets of sparks into that twinkling cloud. What the droid lacked in accuracy, it compensated for with intensity.
423.	Lynx looked from the window at the flare of light the droid illuminated the already bright street with; he watched as Garro slumped to the floor, his shirt and trousers erupting ribbons of blood. Lynx hammered a palm at the toughened glass separating them and, realising futility, glanced to his pack… his deck…
 
424.	Mellie was racing across the street, making effort to control the bursts of her tiny gun. The impacts of her bullets sprayed a grinder’s wheel of sparks from the armour of the droid.
425.	She flung herself behind a parked car; in the instant of landing, the vehicle chassis buzzed and rippled as dozens of tiny bullets ripped into the opposite side from where she lay.
426.	She had intended to try to occupy the metal monster's attention. This is what that attention felt like.
427.	Her thigh burst into a zap of pain, burning white hot as a happenstance bullet from the torrent found its way passed or through the vehicle.
428.	There was no way out now. Fumbling at her coat, she grabbed out the only ammunition she had brought. There was no way to have predicted this, and she felt entirely at the mercy of a significantly better-suited opponent. She sucked in a tight breath and jolted to a firing position using the car's shape to shield her.
429.	Rapid small bursts chattered from her gun. Gotta keep on top of ammo; gotta get Garro; where’s Lynx? The thoughts ricocheted through her head. It wasn’t thought that was in control now. Reflex and adrenaline were masters here.
430.	The robot had become obscured by the ejections of a smoke canaster; somewhere beyond the cloud, the other officer was still there. The metallic triplet clank of the droid approaching sent neurons and muscles to action as Mellie watched, a passenger from her own eyes.
431.	She noted she was doing well at managing her ammo count, replacing a spent ammunition clip with surprising efficiency and, again, jolting to shifted positions to fire from. Worryingly, she also noted that the barrier of the car she was behind had—in only moments—stopped resembling a car.
 
432.	Lynx looked back from the corner of the alley he had launched himself into at the gunfight now playing out. There was no way Mellie would survive, he contemplated, and Garro’s already dead—nothing I can do here.
433.	Hacking the lock control of the Cit-Sec Wagon had been too easy, but how many perps end up arrested and still have their hands free and have access to a deck?
434.	Even with these considerations, it was a trivial matter, overriding several fail safes and—with some code injection— making the lock self-trigger as though unlocking was part of the vehicle’s lock-down procedures.
435.	Making it out unseen behind the officer had made Lynx’s heart beat clean out of his chest; thankfully, the guy was busy extracting something from a side-mounted stowage, enough distraction to give Lynx time to flee. But now he needed to escape the area before the Psycho-Squad rocks on to scene.
436.	Lynx, separated by distance, looked on in helplessness. He focussed again on the vehicle Mellie was sheltering behind. The car's front side was so badly twisted by gunfire—it would be impossible now to call it a car. Shattered glass lay everywhere as smoke billowed into the street. A sorry place for anyone to resign to as their final resting spot.
437.	It was a chilling sensation as he called on his years of street survival; he knew—if he was to survive, he had to go now. Mellie and Garro would just be more meat for the hungry city. He yanked his pack up and bolted to the safety of the trash-strewn alley, back to what he knew.
 
438.	Controlled bursts. Mellie couldn’t think of anything else. The change in the droid's firing pattern registers only as a distant observation in Mellie's adrenaline-fueled mind. Her body and mind were slowly starting to become recognisably her own again, but the immediacy of the threat, now only yards away was too great still for her to be distracted.
439.	Unaware of the droid's design limitations—its small ammo bins, its shift from destruction to suppression—she interprets its altered behaviour as a slight reprieve, an opening for her to take more shots.
440.	The ringing sound of a higher-calibre weapon interrupts her thoughts, a chilling reminder that the droid isn't her only problem. She expends her penultimate clip, aiming at the fearsome tripod of destruction.
441.	Similarly ignorant not only of the droid's capabilities but also of the fact that not all of her bullets were wasted. In the blur of reality that was the explosive world about her, one of her shots had punctured a central pneumatic line. The droid had been disgorging fluid and had come to a complete stop, two of its legs frozen, locking it in place.
442.	Another concussive shot, this time one that unavoidably announced itself into Mellie's consciousness as the shell of the car flowered outward just inches from where she crouched… That was high power - and penetrative, she realises. Her cover was meaningless now.
443.	With adrenaline still puppeting her actions, she was moving before her brain allowed her the grace of comprehension to the fact. Her holdout gun rattles off a burst of bullets as she crossed the street, her lengthy stride bounding toward Garro.
444.	Nothing was registering to her now—her senses utterly overwhelmed to the point of numbness. She's aware only of the immediacy of the moment, the need to reach safety, the fierce determination to survive.
445.	She reached Garro, her momentum carrying her into a tumbling slide that ends at his feet. Her knees and elbows scrape painfully against the concrete, and shards of glass cut into her skin.
446.	Mellie's focus narrows to a single point: the man she's desperately trying to drag to safety. Garro's massive frame should be impossible for her to move, but whether it's adrenaline or sheer willpower, she finds the strength to pull him along. Miraculously, his legs are moving too—unsteady, faltering steps that nevertheless aid their escape.
447.	Hot, wet jets of blood spray over her; Garro jolts as whatever was still shooting hits home on his gigantic frame, but she doesn't let go. Her fingers dig into his flesh, clinging to him as if her life depends on it—because it does. He coughs, a thick bluster of blood falling from his mouth—but still his legs moved.
448.	No force could hold Mellie back. Her legs kicked each step into the ground. Garro’s weight pressed on her impossibly as she lolloped into a side alley. The slick crimson drool that trailed behind would be too simple for the security forces to track.
449.	She let Garro slump to the wall, pulling her tiny automatic gun up and thrust its radiant barrel head into Garro’s guts; the smells of the iron tang of blood and the acridity of burned flesh congealing against its heat choked the air.
450.	She moved with a deftness that was instinct and training cascading from her—ripping off the wrecked shirt from Garro and tearing it to ribbons of tourniquets.
451.	There was enough spilt viscera that her jacket arms were red to the shoulders. Her efforts were nowhere near enough, and he was still leaking a worrying amount of blood, but it should allow them to break a trail.
452.	Heaving Garro to her shoulder again, her hair now dark and matted with sweat, she thinks she hears him mumble something. But if he does speak – his words are lost, dissolving into a garbled mix of spittle and blood. Her legs raged in hot agony as she hauled him off, her wounded thigh biting and snapping at her.
453.	Then, a flicker of hope crosses her mind: she remembers seeing graffiti that pointed the way to a ripper—a back-alley surgeon. The memory is hazy, the directions vague, but it's all she has.
454.	Is it close enough? She has no way of knowing. But it has to be. It just has to be.
Chapter Eight:	What Comes Around.
455.	Garro laughed heartily. Of course, his laugh was too grand for the silly quip that Lynx had made. Nonetheless, he happily rolled his mirth into a broad belly chuckle as he play-punched a ham fist into Lynx’s shoulder. A simple joke or some impromptu slapstick gave him a little joy. He lived in moments and had come to lap up simple pleasures others might overlook.
456.	Mellie returned to their table, setting down three tubs of steaming noodles. The topping, almost meat-like in appearance, looked appetizing in its sticky brown sauce. She sat down and grabbed the complementary chopsticks. "Come on, Lynx, you must know something. There's no reason Davie would have called now. I took our names off the merc board," Mellie said, glancing at Garro.
457.	It had been only a week since they sat shivering in the spent adrenaline of that day. The ripper had taken only 12 minutes to save his life; for a street doc, he moved like a pro, and his work was immaculate. She recalled; 18 bullets… And those were the ones that didn’t go all the way through. She took in Garro's enormous, grinning frame as she thought about it.
458.	Lynx, slurping down a half-chewed mouthful of noodles, pulled her attention back. "I know as much as you do, but I haven't really spoken to that jerk for days, ya know?"
459.	Mellie nodded. "Right, so, we're just meeting at the club like last time, yeah?" Her question was met with a noncommittal shrug from Lynx. She looked back at Garro and said, "Hey, Garro, you fancy going to meet Davie again?"
460.	Garro looked up from his noodle box, sauce glistening on his unshaven jaw. "Sure, we got another job? I need more creds; gonna run out of stims soon," he said, gesturing with a giant thumb over his shoulder.
461.	“We’ll make sure you get your meds, don’t worry”, Mellie assured him. The ripper doc's words echoed in the back of her mind: “he should be dead, but that rig of his is some preem gear, no doubt saved his life. A few of the rounds scraped some of it, but it’s armoured, would take a lot more to put him down.”
462.	An indestructible gorilla of a man, she thought, forever jailed behind pain.
463.	In the conversation lull, Garro eagerly announced, “Hey Lynx, I got a new game-rig.”
464.	Mellie blinked in awe of the apparent utter disregard he had ever suffered mortal wounding only days before as if it had never happened. Garro was already proffering the handheld gizmo to Lynx for inspection.
465.	“Oh shit, this is older than me!” Lynx’s joyous report drew another smile from Garro as he fumbled about the grey-cased, book-sized electronic device. “…doesn’t even have a jack input.”
466.	“Yeah, I only got one game, but it’s hella good.” Garro’s deeper voice shared a moment of interconnection with Lynx’s vibrancy. “You know where I can get more?”.
467.	Lynx's face turned contemplative as he idly pushed the buttons on the game-rig.
468.	Finishing her noodles, Mellie nodded to garner attention, “When you two kids are done playing with toys, we should get going.”
469.	Garro poured his near-empty container into his awaiting mouth, and, feeling Mellie’s prompting, he picked out a sauce-covered noodle from the remains in the tub and flicked it at a rat that sat nervously not a few feet from where they sat. He watched as the tiny creature sniffed, seized the offering, and scurried away. Garro's face broke into another satisfied grin. “Alright, lessgo”
 
470.	The internal agent roused Gerhardt with a soft bleep, the audio connection snapping to life as he pushed himself up in the stifled dimness of his apartment.
471.	Engaged in a one-sided dialogue with an unseen presence—his voice dipped lower with exasperation, “Yes, what do you want now?” the minor adjustment of tone – a reflection of the person he spoke with.
472.	Even though it was 2 p.m., he had set the window to blackout while he caught up on some much-needed rest. And now, half listening to a conversation in his head, he fumbled at the window controls to revert it, allowing the hazy sunlight to wash into his open-plan living area.
473.	“Yes, I remember saying that, but I need some real money, not whatever you're proposing – it sounds shit.”
474.	The ongoing exchange accompanied his movement through the apartment, searching for a shirt and setting water to heat. “How much? I mean, really – just give me a straight number, please.”
475.	He performed a stuttered dance of familiar repetition as he sluggishly made ready for another day. Amidst this familiarity, his routine stumbled on an unusual note: a small blinking icon on a wall panel; its presence indicated that someone had visited his door while he slept but hadn't left a message. He cleared the pestering blink from the panel, dismissing the absent visitor.
476.	Continuing the conversation, he dryly accused the air before him, “Do you always have to sound like a prick?”
477.	Gerhardt collected up and spilt the cold remnants of an undrunk cup into a sink. He stopped and leaned against the counter, his face squinting with a passing thought. He noticed his own dulled, mottled and warped reflection in the metallic backing of the section that posed as a food preparation area. That version of himself seemed to dislike what he was hearing as much as he did.
478.	“And you can guarantee that?”
479.	A calculated pause saturated him, his gaze lingering on the empty cup.
480.	“Okay, fine. But consider that you owe me one. Another one.” He pushed the cup under the water dispenser.
481.	“At the club? Fine.”
482.	Inhaling deeply, he stretched his still-human arm across his chest, feeling the taut muscles in his torso strain against the fresh implants of his other arm. Still needs some time, he thought, reflecting on the still-fresh surgery.
483.	A fleeting glance towards the corner revealed an open recessed wardrobe. Displayed within were the uninspiring variations of black and grey outfits that were his usual attire. Yet, concealed within a discrete compartment, rested his rifle. A sneer of petulant resignation pulled his face as he very slightly shook his head. Not for this, he thought.
484.	The water dispenser announced with a splutter, signalling the end of its cycle, interruption enough to pull him back from his contemplations of what firepower might best be suited to what was asked of him. First a drink, then I’ll figure out what I’m doing, he decided, tearing open a packet and pouring its contents into the hot water.
485.	“Vid on, show me the news – local and central,” he commanded.
486.	A holographic display dripped into existence behind him, and a weary female voice began recounting the recent news; he reflexively glances at the time displayed among the rolling cascade of news items – Taking a sip of his still-hot drink and mentally gauging whether he had enough time to meet a reliable black-market contact before heading to the Halcyon Club in the Skrim.
 
487.	Davie's perplexed expression remained fixed on the now-blank panel on his desk, his furrowed brow echoing his confusion. "What in the hell is up his ass?" he muttered to the empty glass perched nearby. The room absorbed his words in its quietude.
488.	Davie and Gerhardt had a long history; he thought he knew the guy. Yet, in this moment, as he stared at the silent panel, he questioned whether he truly understood the man. Something was off, a disturbing change he couldn't quite identify.
489.	With a contemplative sigh, he stood and walked toward the expansive window, which offered a bird's-eye view of the mostly vacant club floor below. A handful of staff members buzzed about, engrossed in their tasks, while a technician diligently repaired some broken fixtures along a distant wall. These were just unimportant details involving unimportant people doing unimportant things—yet they provided a backdrop against which he could think.
490.	Giving himself the mental ownership of the space, he flung any concerns of Gerki from his train of thought, choosing instead to once again allow his signature self-satisfied smug grin to wrap about his face with the happy recognition of a new deal in his lap.
491.	The same crew from a while back, in a similar gig, but for even more money, and this time - no complications. In his role as someone who ‘made things happen’, he straddled a tight line of trying to extort cash from both ends of the deal.
492.	Akureyri had its share of people willing to pay to get things done, and Davie prided himself on knowing who they were. He also had a knack for connecting with the right people—people like Gerhardt, who actually got things done. Everything that happened in between those discussions was just a matter of calculation: how much could he extract from each transaction?
493.	Gazing out across the expanse of the club, a cascade of vibrant crimson hair swiftly caught Davie's attention – Mellie had arrived. Following closely, he observed Lynx, a sleek shadow trailing Mellie, and then the imposing figure of Garro, dwarfing them both. With the players once more ready, the final task was to ensure that they all danced to his tune.
 
494.	Mellie's voice cut through the ambient hum of the club like a sharpened blade. "I distinctly remember telling you I didn't want to be in your register for now," she said, her words tinged with frustration. Standing before Davie, she was a vivid presence—her striking hair cascading around her shoulders, her posture defiant.
495.	Davie, his demeanour a practised blend of smugness and charm, leaned against the bar. The low light of the club, devoid of its usual clamour and shine, cast a glint in his eye and matching cybernetics. "You know how it works, Mellie. Business is business," he retorted, a casual shrug betraying the minimal weight he placed on their interactions.
496.	Beside Mellie, Lynx's dark eyes shimmered with a trace of impatience. "Look, we're not here to play games. What do you want, Davie? Why call us in?" His voice edged higher than he'd ever used with Davie, and he couldn't shake the feeling that he was crossing a line.
497.	From the shadows behind them, Garro seemed to swell with quiet intensity. "And what's in it for us?" His broad arms were crossed over his chest, making him an additional pillar on the club floor.
498.	Mellie shot Lynx a fleeting glance—her eyes silently chastising him for his audacity—before turning her focus back to Davie. "Well, you got me here. So, spit it out."
499.	Davie's lips curled into a confident smirk. "A super easy gig, easier even than the mess you made last time," he said, his tone both uplifting and smooth, locking eyes with Mellie as if issuing a challenge.
500.	An uneasy silence hovered momentarily as they collectively tried to gauge the atmosphere. It was Davie who broke the quiet. "Come, grab a drink. Let's go to my office; I can fill you in."
501.	Mellie quickly scanned Lynx and Garro for reassurance; both offered subdued shrugs of acceptance. Garro reached over the adjacent bar to grab a bottle.
502.	Davie maintained his smirk as he watched the scene unfold, then gestured for them to follow him.
503.	As they followed Davie through the dimly lit corridor, Mellie's mind churned with suspicion and curiosity: what’s the job that this guy called us? The sounds of staff filling glasses and the low rumble of speakers waiting for patrons faded behind them, leaving an air of quiet anticipation to envelop the group.
504.	Upon entering Davie's office, they settled into worn leather chairs clustered around an ostentatious but weathered central table.
505.	Mellie leaned back, her eyes locked onto Davie's. "Alright, what's this 'super easy gig' you're talking about? What's going on?" Her voice was tight and impatient.
506.	Taking his seat as if it were a throne, Davie allowed himself a moment to settle in before speaking. "Upfront. This is a strict zero-knowledge gig, so I'm guessing it's some off-the-books Corpo operation. I chose you guys because—well, maybe I have a soft spot for you." The feigned kindness felt out of place coming from him.
507.	“It’s simple enough, Garro there could front this one”, Davie said, using an unlit cigar to point at the somewhat uncomfortably seated Garro, who was happily slugging back mouthfuls of whatever the bottle contained. The intended insult bounced off of him unnoticed.
508.	Davie continued around the absence of response, “I got some co-ords and a time—instructions for a mixed team of specialists—which I suppose is what you lot are now. Plus, I got a bucket of creds with explicit instructions to keep this message, and my talkin’ to you now, off of any records. Purely black-book stuff.”
509.	Davie took a moment to assess Mellie’s posture. His still human eye flicked to read her face and posture. “So… Not being on my register right now is exactly what I need.”
510.	Lynx couldn't contain himself and interjected, "That's all a bit vague. Coordinates? Specialists? I'm going to need more details."
511.	Davie's veneer cracked as he snapped, “You’ll do what I fucking say you do.” The words were cold.
512.	The sudden outburst tensed Garro, causing him to lurch forward in his undersized chair. Mellie waved him back, shaking her head.
513.	Turning her attention back to Davie, Mellie matched his energy. "Look, you little fucking toad..." Her voice escalated, her cold, focused eyes piercing through the tension to land squarely on Davie.
514.	The room was cut in two by a voice emerging from the periphery that held a cold, calm weight of authority. "Enough, Davie." Gerhardt's entrance felt like a gust of wind sweeping through the room. Though neither tall nor loud, his presence seemed to fill the space, his calculating gaze sweeping over the group. His face was stern, tinged with a rigid impatience that brooked no argument.
515.	Mellie's anger seemed to momentarily falter in the face of Gerhardt's arrival, replaced by a blend of annoyance at the interruption and begrudging respect for Gerhardt bringing Davie into check.
516.	Lynx shared a quick, upward nod with Gerhardt, a fleeting but polite acknowledgement of the other's presence. Garro's tense posture relaxed as he took a moment to register Gerhardt's intervention.
517.	Seemingly unfazed by Gerhardt's entrance, Davie leaned back in his chair. "Well, well, look who decided to join the party," he mused, a glint of amusement flickering in his tight eyes that spoke of disgust at being scolded in his own club.
518.	Gerhardt's voice was icy, his words measured and deliberate. "Mellie's right. You are a toad, and this gig stinks," he said, locking eyes with Davie in silent command.
519.	Davie coughed, breaking the volley of angry stares aimed his way. "Stinks? Gerki, I already told you, ten-kay is a lot of money, even if it does stink."
520.	Lynx’s entire form wilted as he blinked against that announcement. Garro huffed a heavy breath and raised an eyebrow.
521.	Mellie simply sat back, crossing her arms, with pursed lips, her eyes still in the throes of an assault against Davie.
522.	“Ten Kay?” – Lynx questioned, head and eyes checking for the reality that everyone here had heard the same thing; “as in, ten thousand dollars?”
523.	Davie let the room settle about him, summoned a deep nasal breath and, putting down the baton of his cigar, regained his composure and smile. “Yes, ten thousand. If you had let me finish, I—”
524.	Gerhardt's steely voice cut in again. "Ten-kay for what, though? You've given us nothing. And honestly, this stinks all the way to the docks and back. I know this is Corpo dross, and you know how I feel about that."
525.	The warm air was thick with unspoken, divergent expectations, the will of each individual threatening to unravel the already tenuous cohesion of the group. In the ensuing silence, Gerhardt's gaze shifted from Davie to Mellie, his expression softening ever so slightly.
526.	Gerhardt twitched his head sideways, keeping his focus on Davie, "Mellie, I never pegged you as a chump, right?” He turned to face her directly, his tone even. “We need more than just promises and some flashy numbers."
527.	Mellie's eyes held their ground, her expression unyielding. "He's right, Davie. You can't just throw numbers at us and expect us to jump."
528.	Davie's smirk faded, replaced by a more earnest expression. He leaned forward, steepling his fingers as he surveyed the room. "Alright, alright. You know me—I wouldn't come to you if it weren't worth your while. The details are sensitive, hence the hush-hush. But I'll spill what I can."
529.	Gerhardt's stance relaxed infinitesimally, an unnoticed indication of his cautious receptiveness. "Go on."
530.	Davie took a deep breath, his eyes shifting among them. "I got a client—A high-end Corp exec. They've got an agenda, something off-the-books. They need a team that knows how to get things done on the quiet, someone who doesn't mind a bit of dirt on their hands," he said, carefully choosing each word.
531.	Lynx leaned in, his scepticism concealed by a visible glint of eagerness in his eyes, as if the numbers that the others had rebuked were calling out to him. "So, we're talking black ops for some corporate bigwig?"
532.	Davie nodded, his eyes meeting each of theirs in turn. "Exactly. Your skills are perfect for this job. I don't have all the specifics, but trust me, the payday is worth it. They're willing to pay extra for complete discretion. This isn't about reputation; it's about getting in, getting out, and getting paid."
533.	Mellie's eyes narrowed, her gaze shifting from confrontational to contemplative. "So, what's the catch? Why are they being so secretive?"
534.	"You're asking the right questions, Mellie. The catch is that you'll need to act fast.” Davie said, his chair creaking as he leaned back, adopting a posture of nonchalant detachment. “Like I said, I've got some coordinates, but this gig is now or never. If it isn’t you, it’s some other crew. The payout is a kind reminder of their urgency."
535.	Gerhardt interjected, his voice tinged with practicality. "And the risk?"
536.	Davie looked squarely at Gerhardt, his demeanour turning grave. "Look, I won't lie. I don't know what exactly they expect from you. But with your shared skills, I'm confident you can handle it. Smells like a Corp covering their tracks, so I assume they'll have resources to cover yours as well. No trace, no paper. You'll be ghosts."
537.	Mellie's arms had unfolded, her fingers drummed against the arm of her chair as she pondered. "So, a job where the risks are a mystery, yet promises a lucrative payoff. And if things go south, I doubt any Corp would do anything other than cover its own back. We'd likely be left to fend for ourselves."
538.	Davie nodded, his eyes earnest. "I won't sugarcoat it. For this gig, at this price, you're going to wade through some shit. But if you pull this off, you could walk away with enough to retire early if you want."
539.	Silence settled over the room as each person considered Davie's offer. Finally, Gerhardt broke the stillness, his gaze steady and his tone measured. "We'll discuss it. Give us a few minutes."
540.	Davie's grin reappeared, this time radiating genuine satisfaction. “Fair enough. You know where to find me when you've made up your minds.” With those parting words, he stood up and straightened his jacket, reclaiming his charismatic presence as if donning a familiar cloak. Then he exited the room, leaving the group to their deliberations.
541.	As Davie left, the tension in the room seemed to dissipate, replaced by a sense of camaraderie among the group. Mellie leaned forward, her eyes meeting Gerhardt's. “So, what do we think?”
542.	Lynx and Garro shared a quick, knowing look. Then Lynx fixed his eyes on Gerhardt, his expression resolute but tinged with excitement. “It sounds fucking terrifying, but if we plan this right, that payout is dumb money.”
543.	Garro's deep voice rumbled in agreement. "Yeah, I've faced worse than uncertainty before."
544.	Gerhardt looked over the team, a small smile tugging at the corner of his mouth. “Alright, then. We plan. If we're in, we're in together. Can I trust each of you?” His gaze lingered on each team member, searching for sincerity.
545.	Mellie met his gaze with a steady, unwavering look. “You can trust me. This could be big.” Her eyes then shifted to Lynx and Garro, seeking their confirmation.
546.	Lynx nodded firmly. “Hell yeah. You’ve got my word. For that cash—let’s do this.”
547.	Garro simply added, “Where Mel goes, I go.” his tone embodying a quiet strength.
548.	Their responses, each unique yet unified in purpose, solidified their bond. Mellie’s gaze lingered a moment longer on each face, reading the unspoken commitment in their eyes. “Then we're agreed,” she declared. And just like that, a disparate set of hopeful souls finally joined in unison, sat planning for the unknown.
Chapter Nine:	Never Trust a Corpo.
549.	The spotting rain was cool, the rank stench of chemicals, hot metal, and industrial effluent was omnipresent. The street, long, wide and dark - It was an area of the city just southeast of the Skrim that was a forgotten relic of the older part of the area referred to simply as the Docklands.
550.	An industrial, mechanical churn of automated drones and self-routing transport rigs were its principal inhabitants. A city of millions demanded a lot of materials and goods; this was just one of the sections of the city through which such things moved.
551.	The area was so industrialized that even during daylight hours, it was rare to encounter another person. The constant mechanical clatter and hum, devoid of any sign of life, created an oppressive atmosphere, highlighting the area's detachment from the city's more populated districts.
552.	As evening approached, the growing darkness only intensified this sense of isolation, deepening the shadows and pulling the place further from the realm of human comfort.
553.	Gerhardt had been the first to arrive, but he had also been the one to scout the place out before nightfall. The coordinates Davie had passed on to them were for a dilapidated chemical refinement lab. By the look of the place, it had been boarded up for years.
554.	Lynx had done an info-scrape on the place, finding only that the land lease of the building was still in the current ownership of some offshore Corp. Even so, as he stood watching the building now, Gerhardt didn’t like it. It felt wrong.
555.	Gerhardt looked up, his gaze fixing on the telecoms tower attached to a building not five hundred yards from their designated point. The sightlines were clear, and the tower would make an ideal vantage point. The heft of his high-powered rifle was entirely comforting to him. Against the uncertainty and anxiety of this enigma of instructions, it felt like a lantern to keep back the darkness of his trepidations.
556.	Instinctively summoning the internal display of his cybernetic eye, he saw the current time blink into existence as an overlay on his field of vision, realizing he'd need to start climbing if he wanted to be in position before the others arrived.
557.	It was a simple plan. He was going to provide overwatch, a sure thing that he expressed would be no problem; Garro was, of course, to be the point man for the operation; Mellie was close support and Lynx the backup, along with whatever tech nonsense he had to get done.
558.	They’d had only a matter of hours to prepare their plan, but with some surprising efficacy – between them, they had put together a decent idea of how to run their show. Gerhardt felt a sense of recognition to Mellie; she’d been quite acutely clever in dealing with the info Lynx had brought up.
559.	The primary concern of each of them was that, if there were any signs things were going south, everyone was to get safe under Gerhard's watch, and then he would make good his escape using a route he’d already picked out and already stashed his bike ready for. Short of Cit-Sec showing up, they should be in command of whatever is going down.
560.	The climb up the tower wasn't easy. Twice, Gerhardt felt his feet betray him, slipping on the precarious surface. Each time, his cybernetic arm's powerful grip saved him from a perilous fall into the rusted debris that littered the workyard below.
561.	But with skills and nerves born of his trade, he made his way to the halfway bridging girders that would be his nest.
562.	He set his rifle out, securing a cable lanyard to his tactical vest, wrapping it about a nearby angled girder, and finding a position that gave him the best purchase. He was ready.
563.	For the final touch, he drew a smart-cable from his wrist and plugged it into his rifle. As he did so, he willingly surrendered control from his human eye to the augmented vision of his cybernetic one and the now connected digital scope of the rifle; his vision became tattooed with information—ranges, inclination, temperature, wind. He could feel the rifle in his mind.
564.	Now, all that remained was to wait—about ten more minutes in the cold, silent perch. The metal beneath him, slick and cold, began to leach its chill through his thick jacket, doing nothing to quell the unease that gnawed at him. That feeling intensified with each passing moment.
 
565.	Mellie, Garro, and Lynx struggled to share the cramped back seat of the city-cab, an experience that felt far from glamorous given the high-stakes gig they were en route to. At least Lynx had stashed his pack in the trunk, granting them a modicum of extra space.
566.	Compounding their lack of elegance – Garro's stomach chose that moment to loudly announce its discontent. They exited the cab hastily, and though none of them spoke Mandarin, they were confident the driver's parting words were anything but complimentary.
567.	“Sorry.” Garro quietly uttered in the rising silence of the soft static of rainfall and the distant hum and buzz of machinery.
568.	With puffed cheeks of held breath and his off-hand waving away the memory, Lynx exhaled with an over-emphasized blustered cough.
569.	In an effort to divert attention from the awkward situation, Mellie focused on her own concerns. "I should have brought my gun," she stated, her voice tinged with annoyance. Her hand patted her coat pocket, a subtle action that offered her the comfort of knowing her machine-pistol was at least close at hand.
570.	Lynx had pulled from his pack and donned a headset visor. Lynx, busy with his headset visor, was attaching several leads to his deck. After securing the connections, he stowed the deck back in his pack and looked quickly between Mellie and Garro. "I know we said Garro was gonna take the lead, but—do I have to walk behind him?" He made a face of mock disgust.
571.	Garro shrugged and rolled his head sideways, indicating their target, “We should get moving though. Time’s gettin’ close.”
572.	“Alright big man, we’re moving”, Lynx replied in a jestingly nervous response.
573.	Earlier in the day, Lynx had spent the afternoon hacking into the local grid and had set up a broadcasting signal repeater for himself; he now had access to the nearby cameras and, incidentally, control of someone’s vid-screen. But all the preparation still left him with an uneasy twist in his gut.
574.	The trio moved solemnly down the darkened road, a stretch designed for automated vehicles and thus left largely unlit. The rain had lessened to a misty drizzle, imparting an oily sheen to their hair and skin. This glistening layer captured the sparse light, giving off an iridescent glow of purples and greens.
575.	As they closed in on their destination, walking the distance of the last block, the air grew heavy with tension that pulled a thought from the back of Mellie’s mind, what in the hell is worth ten kay in this shit hole?
 
576.	Gerhardt watched them disembark from the cab, the three of them lingering momentarily as they made last-minute preparations before proceeding on foot. He tracked them with his scope’s reticule as they walked toward him. It will take precisely no effort to kill each of them, he thought; distance and ballistic drop already ticking into place within his mind.
577.	As he breathed, the controls of the rifle buzzed in unison with his arm’s own systems, adjusting to even the subtlest shifts in his unnervingly steady breathing. He watched as they drew closer.
578.	The sudden chirp of the agent cut through the night's stillness, jarring him momentarily. Connecting the call—Lynx's voice came through, tinged with an unmistakable note of nervousness.
579.	"Can you see us?" Lynx inquired.
580.	"I could see you before you even got out of the cab," Gerhardt replied tersely. Distractions were unwelcome. "Keep it quiet unless there's something critical."
581.	“Sorry, yeah, okay – I’m gonna keep the line up though, okay?”
582.	Gerhardt exhaled softly, his equipment compensating for the subtle shift in his posture. "Fine, but keep it quiet. Understand?"
583.	Caught in the undertow of his own apology, Lynx hesitated for a beat before asking, "You don't see anything out of the ordinary, do you?"
584.	“Nothing and no one. Now, please, keep quiet. I need to focus.” He settled back against the rifle, finding his position again. Although alive with the static of a connection, his agent remained silent.
 
585.	“Yeah, he said there’s nobody there.” Lynx kept his voice low as he tried to sound in control in front of the picturesque Mellie. Even now, soaked wet, mired in oily waste and shadowed by the dim lights, Lynx thought she looked amazing, more so for the steely determination in her eyes.
586.	“Good, let's stick to the plan. Garro—” The final instruction was understood without being spoken.
587.	With a firm grip on the wire mesh fence, Garro pulled. The fence protested with a loud, jarring twang as its cables and fastenings strained against his strength. Despite the noise, which felt magnified in the silent surroundings, the sheer fact that Garro single-handedly tore away an eight-foot section of fencing seemed to justify the disruption. For a moment, they all felt as though every reflective window around them had turned into watchful eyes.
588.	“You’re up kid.” Mellie's low voice brought the silence back to them as the fence settled to the ground under Garro’s assistance.
589.	Lynx darted forward to the connections box; Garro and Mellie followed in unison. He fell to a seated position by the box and pointed. Garro nodded and, with both hands, took a grip of the box’s metal enclosure – deftly ripping it and its bolt fittings from the wall, leaving a naked tangle of wires into which Lynx’s hands darted.
590.	Lynx nodded sharply, causing a visor to drop over his eyes, and began using a pair of matched projected holographic control pads that glowed from each wrist. His movements were instinctual; the green-cyan glow from beneath the visors illuminated his cheeks in the gloom.
591.	“Yup, no worries, I just have to drop a little code bomb. There’s basically no ice in here – won't be a sec”, he reported.
592.	Mellie looked upward, her gaze searching the dark where she believed Gerhardt to be perched. She made a circle with her thumb and finger, signalling that things were progressing.
593.	Garro swung his head about, doing his absolute best to try and survey the distant shadows; he knew how to operate in a field of battle. The darkness, and the silence it carried, were a new and different set of enemies to learn.
594.	“Just one more loop, almost there.” Lynx provided another update, though the specifics of his actions remained unfathomable to the others. His confidence and determination defied the ambiguity of his words. “Okay, I’m on the local net now, n’ checking - I just… Hmm, wait,” his tone shifted abruptly.
595.	“What is it?” Mellie pressed, her voice barely above a whisper yet laden with urgency.
596.	“The logs, they’re like- a decade old. This system is dead as a bot dipped in hot sauce,” he responded, bemusement colouring his voice.
597.	Mellie's brow furrowed at Lynx's revelation, her mind working to process what he was relaying. The notion that the system they were infiltrating had been idle for a decade sent a ripple of doubt through her thoughts. The carefully crafted plan suddenly felt fragile, like a house of cards on the verge of collapse.
598.	Lynx's fingers continued to dance across the controls. Illuminated by his visor casting an otherworldly glow on his features, he muttered to himself. His voice—a mix of confusion and intrigue, “It's like a digital graveyard in here, Mel. There's zero recent activity– just ancient logs, literally nothing.”
599.	Mellie exchanged a tense glance with Garro, her mind racing. The realization hit her like a shockwave – if the system had been inactive for so long, their entire endeavour might have been for nought. A flurry of thoughts raced through her mind, each more unsettling than the last.
600.	Garro's fists clenched reflexively, the tension in his posture mirroring the uncertainty of the situation. He grunted in frustration, the urge to act clashing with the reality of the tense yet quiet moment.
601.	Mellie's voice, though soft, bore a current of desperate urgency. “So, what are you telling us? Are we walking into a trap? Or is this some kind of sick joke?”
602.	Lynx’s visor flickered as he adjusted his stance; concern smothered his voice. “It's hard to say. The system's so old-fashioned that even another code bomb might just blow out the whole rig. There should be upkeep logs. There’s just nothing. Somebody—or something—erased them.”
603.	Mellie grimaced, clenching her fists. She had expected challenges, but not like this: a complete lack of information, on an already enigmatic job, leaving them exposed and uncertain. Her nails bit into her palms as she unconsciously mirrored Garro’s tense clenching. “So, what's the call, then?”
604.	Garro leaned in, although carrying a trace of resignation; his words were simple and to the point. "Doesn’t feel like a trap. Shit would be going off already, but still not good." He swung an arm to indicate their quiet solitude—he knew what traps felt like. It wasn’t like this.
605.	Mellie's nod was reluctant, her annoyance tempered by begrudging respect for their unseen and unquantified foe. “Alright, let's not get caught up in this. We came here to complete a job. If the system's a graveyard, then it's a graveyard we exploit.”
606.	Lynx's fingers never stopped their nimble assault on the control pads as he concurred. “Ar’ight, I’ll see what I can do from here. Someone might be expecting us, or they might just be as surprised as we are. Either way, no point stopping now.”
607.	For a moment, the tension that had been threatening to capsize their operation transmuted into a fresh resolve. Mellie inhaled deeply, her gaze lifting to the dark structure that loomed over them, its towering presence making her feel diminutive in comparison. “Alright, Lynx, finish up and let's get inside. We've got a job to do.”
608.	Lynx’s visor dimmed slightly as he disconnected from the console; the holographic control pads blinked from existence—their emitters retracted into hidden compartments in his bracer wristbands as he pulled his visor off. Rising to his feet, he nodded. “After you.”
609.	Garro shifted, his muscles tensed and ready despite the futility in the engulfing darkness. He felt like a warrior with no clear battle; if he could reach out to grapple the silence, he would. “So, we goin’ in?” he asked, a trace of frustration in his voice, trapped by inaction.
610.	Mellie's eyes darted between her teammates, her voice carrying a mix of resolve and a touch of wry humour. “Yeah, get us in there - Let's just hope it's not more dead logs.”
611.	“Hey, Gerki, we're headed in,” Lynx announced softly, gazing roughly in the direction where he presumed Gerhardt to be. “Not sure what you'll be doing from up there, unless you just want to watch.”
612.	In the distance, Gerhardt winced at the nickname. Annoyed, he realized Lynx had picked up bad habits from Davie. With a sigh, he responded, "I told you to keep it quiet. Now— why are you going in? What's the situation?"
613.	“Place is dead, been dead for years, Garro’s gonna check out inside, you commin’?”
614.	Gerhardt's voice crackled in Lynx's earpiece, his tone laced with scepticism. "Dead, you say? This is all wrong, Lynx. Something doesn't add up. Hold there, I’m coming to you."
Chapter Ten:	Alone Together.
615.	Mellie was already leading Garro to the front of the building, gesturing toward a sizeable double door that was barred and boarded up. Garro twitched a smiling nod, understanding her silent request, happy to be doing things.
616.	The metal bars welded over the entrance groaned and zinged as Garro's wrenching grip tested their integrity. A silent hiss emanated from his metallic spine as his body flooded with chemicals. His muscles swelled and tensed with unbridled raw power. One of the weaker bars snapped with a shearing creak, the metal twisting as it gave way.
617.	The remaining defences were scraped away and discarded with ease. With some surprise - finding beyond their shell a foyer that was lit and buzzed with fluorescent lighting, Garro turned to Mellie seeking guidance at finding a dead building apparently waiting for them. His eyes pleaded for someone to declare an intention in the moment.
618.	Mellie looked back at him, her eyes wide, sharing his astonishment. As Lynx approached, she asked without shifting her gaze, “Lynx, thought you said this place was dead?”
619.	“Yeah, utterly de—” He didn’t finish as he followed the indication of Garro’s pointed finger into the light emanating from inside.
620.	The three gathered, looking into the harsh blue-white light, sharing glances of equal parts confusion and accusation aimed at Lynx. The silent approach of Gerhardt went unnoticed.
621.	“That doesn’t look dead”, Gerhardt announced, breaking the collective silence.
622.	Lynx opened his mouth to speak. “I swear, for real though, the logs—”
623.	Gerhardt waved a dismissive hand. “Well, your logs, or whatever, are wrong.”
624.	Seeing that their plan had already broken down, Mellie looked about them and the street they stood beside, finally taking in the enormous multi-storied blank cube of a building, “Well, the lights are on she said, pulling out her pistol. “Let's see if anyone's home.”
 
625.	Stepping into the entrance, the group was collectively met with a chilling sensation that seemed to dig into their bones. The air was still and felt charged, laden with a musty scent hinting at the passage of time. The room appeared eerily untouched, like a snapshot frozen in a moment long past. Old computers on the desks hummed with an unsettling vitality, their screens casting an eerie glow across the room.
626.	Garro moved forward, his sizable frame casting elongated shadows against the walls. “This ain't right,” he rumbled, peering at the computers. “Look at these machines. Still runnin'.”
627.	Mellie nodded; her skin prickled with a growing unease with every step they took. “Yeah, the place is spotless. It's like it's been preserved or something.”
628.	Gerhardt, his rifle now slung as a backpack, had drawn his massive pistol. Unlike his rifle, which provided extra-perceptory vision, the pistol was equipped only with a heat-sensing laser. The room appeared mostly normal, but wherever he aimed, he saw a blur of colours—greens, reds, blues—all indicating different heat readings. It was disorienting, yet the smart-link made the gun feel like an extension of his arm. "It's cold in here," he reported softly. "I mean, nobody's been here for ages."
629.	Lynx's fingers twitched his visor, flickering down into place with a soft glow spreading on his covered face. "I don't like this. I’m gonna check these old rigs." He moved toward the nearest computer.
630.	“No, leave it,” Mellie snapped. “We should keep moving. If there is someone here, or something we need to find, we need to find it, and get the hell out.” She turned to Garro and gave an indicative chin thrust toward an open door; beyond it, an already-lit corridor.
631.	Garro moved with a surprising glide that was quiet enough he could go unnoticed; the buzz of the lights and the trail of small footsteps behind him were the only discernible noises. Attentive ears might pick out the muted click and creak of Lynx tapping at his deck as he moved with the group.
632.	Mellie crouched down at every turn to take stock. They had followed the lights into the building, but the route was clearly not of their choosing. “Gerhardt,” she whispered, motioning for him to come closer, “Look at the lights. One lit door at the end of each corridor. This has to be a trap, right?”
633.	He looked about with furtive glances, his gun tracking as he looked, “Trap, for certain. But it's so cold, so clean – like the place was set up for us.” He turned to Lynx, pulling him into the conversation. “You managed to get anything?”
634.	Mellie also echoed the question with a recursive call; “Lynx?”
635.	He flipped the visor away from his eyes, “It’s just like I said before- dead. I checked all the cams nearby– no sign of anyone, not even any hardware anywhere near this place for hours. Got nothin’ guys.” His eyes apologised on his behalf.
636.	Garro, having joined the small huddle late and not having heard or paid attention to the discussion, interjected bruskly, “This is bullshit.”
637.	Three pairs of eyes took a moment to address the obvious commentary. Gerhardt waved a hand toward an awaiting lit corridor; “Do we keep going, or are we about to pull out of this mess?”
638.	The quiet electric hum of the lights overhead dropped into the silence behind his question.
639.	Mellie, looking among them all, took in a nasal breath. “Fuck it, if Davie’s right and this is worth ten kays to some Corpo idiot, we’ve not stepped in any shit yet, and I haven’t seen a sign of anyone but us. I’m happy to take that fool's money. Let’s keep going.”
640.	Lynx bit down on his unspoken desire to leave; Gerhardt just offered a short nod before moving swiftly away. It was Garro who, standing to his full height, voiced his concern; “Mel, this feels all wrong, you know it.”
641.	Mellie considered his observation, then set her jaw, a sneer tugging at the corner of her lip. Stepping forward, she took the lead, her boots echoing down the sterile corridor. Knowing hesitation could be their undoing, and they had come too far to turn back now, she declared with authority. "We keep moving." 
642.	The group tenuously advanced—their reason for being here no more apparent now than when they were outside or back at the club. The empty corridors dragged, seeming longer than they were, their sterile white walls absorbing the echoes of their footsteps. They passed closed doors, each one a potential gateway to answers, revealing only dark, empty rooms when cautiously opened. The building felt lost.
643.	This repeated as they found their errant pre-determined route ascending stairs, the upper floors giving no more information than what now lay below them. Pausing momentarily at Gerhard's rigid hand gesture, Lynx checks his deck once more, his digital tendrils prying into the building where their eyes cannot.
644.	“Somethings wrong.” Lynx’s voice was quiet, but he repeated louder to get attention, “Something’s wrong.”
645.	Mellie scanned the surroundings before addressing him with a shrug. “It's just as quiet here as everywhere else we've been.”
646.	"No, not that," Lynx's voice was urgent but hushed. "I rechecked the mainframe access. Nothing's changed."
647.	“So?” she questioned.
