1036.	Casting a glance around the still-empty hallway, he tugged at the wire. Its tautness, anchored firmly into the brickwork, told him that pulling any harder would likely bring the wall down around him. Visions of the droid, just one flight of stairs below, urged him to release his grip.
1037.	Peering through the gaping ruined portal into the room beyond was a small space in which one wall was obscured by tech. Everything looked broken. It smelled wrong.
1038.	Heading downstairs and passing the watchful droids, he exited the building and walked through the cordon with a confident, purposeful gait. “Nobody home,” he announced to the two officers, who were now leaning against the back of one of their vehicles.
1039.	Glancing past the corner of the building down an adjoining alleyway, he saw no officers there. Mentally rehearsing what he needed to tell Mellie, ‘No visors down there,’ he continued his heavy-footed march back to their distant corner.
1040.	“There are Cit-Sec all over the place, droid inside too,” Garro reported hurriedly, almost as if he was racing against his own memory. “The address you gave me is why they’re here. It looks like a bomb went off up there. I couldn’t see anything, But– the alley behind was empty. Might be a way in there?”
1041.	As they began to walk from the scene, the others discussed his observations. A roughshod plan was being beaten into submission, Mellie and Gerhardt each trying to find reason to leave; Lynx instant that without investigation, they’d have nothing to go on.
1042.	The afternoon dragged out. The cordon remained.
1043.	The rhythm of the blue lights became more pronounced as the light and warmth of the sun started to dip below the jagged horizon of the sprawl of suburbs.
Chapter Seventeen:	Dead End.
1044.	It was not a good plan. Mellie outright hated the idea of being so close to the Cit-Sec; Gerhardt had protested sternly. However, it was Lynx who had the final say—they would move a street over and find their way through the back alleyways.
1045.	By way of his years of experience living rough, he had explained the relative ease of accessing buildings utilizing the network of external gantries, and fire escapes that clung to the neighbouring buildings. That would be their way in.
1046.	If Garro had been correct about the security, the top floor was unguarded. Despite the sizable contingent of dark-uniformed officers that milled about in the abruptly flashing dusk of the street in front of the building, they might just find a way to circumvent conventional entry points like doors and stairs.
1047.	A house some distance away was playing music far too loudly—the sound reverberated among the tight alleys. Both Mellie and Gerhardt had their pistols drawn, anticipating trouble. They had each taken a corner of their chosen alley, looking into the adjacent street. This road—unlike the one they’d left—was dimming in the evening light, and unlike the bluster of unwanted dark uniforms there, they’d seen only one other person walking its length.
1048.	Garro, meanwhile, was doing his best to silently shift a large refuse container. Amidst them, Lynx stood—a picture of unease, feeling conspicuously out of his element.
1049.	“Up there.” Lynx indicated, his finger gesturing towards a barred window. Visually, it was no different from the other equally secured windows. He tapped the screen of his arm bracer, which now showed a rudimentary blueprint layout of an apartment block. “That has to be thirty-four.”
1050.	With his shoulder braced against it, Garro gave the container one final push. Despite his careful effort, the sound of metal scraping on concrete was unmistakable—a gritty, grating noise that seemed too loud in the quiet of the alley.
1051.	With the metal container now sat under a raised ladder, Garro hoisted himself onto the stinking container. Stretching to his full height, he grasped at the lowest rungs of the ladder, wrestled momentarily and with a heave, brought it down to him, fighting to stop it from sounding a bell against the metal container's top at the last moment. “Okay—here”, Garro’s deep voice rumbled underneath the distant reverberating music.
1052.	Mellie and Gerhardt were a pair of opposite eyes. Their combined respective focus trained at different angles, each looking for any potential approaching person. Mellie cast a glance over her shoulder to monitor Garro and Lynx's progress; raising her spare hand to her lips, she extended a finger in a silent hush.
1053.	Lynx, having slung his deck behind him, climbed onto the ladder, using Garro’s knee for a boost. It shook a little, but Garro reached to grip it firmly as he ascended and steadied it from below. He took the narrow metal stairs that led upward. 
1054.	Reaching a balcony that overlooked his intended target window, he pursed his lips – damn, he had no other thoughts.
1055.	Looking across the alley—the tiny brick ledge where he needed to be seemed impossibly thin. Maybe this isn’t the best way in, he wondered how to excuse his plan to the others. 
1056.	Garro had ascended behind him, the distant flash of the deep blue lights of the Cit-Sec wagons flicking in the air and catching the facia of taller buildings, and the blue tones caught half of his face in shadow as Lynx turned to leave. There was no room for hesitation now—no turning back.
1057.	Looking at Garro and back to the ledge, Lynx gave an indicative nod to the narrow ledge. Garro mused the instruction, rolled his shoulders, circled his neck and launched across the gap.
1058.	In a refusal of his substantial build, he jumped as lightly as a child would—but the force of his frame striking the building opposite was anything but subtle. One hand gripped the window bars while his other meaty fist inadvertently distorted a drain pipe in a clumsy bid for stability.
1059.	One of his feet had landed clumsily, finding no purchase at the ledge—it skipped from below him—with a quiet growl of determination, he quickly pulled himself to level, resetting his footing; the drainpipe squealed a metallic protest.
1060.	Lynx's face contorted into a silent, anxious grimace. His arm involuntarily reached out, as if trying to catch Garro from afar. He watched, dumbfounded, as the man immediately sprang into action. Lynx had only intended to highlight their target, yet Garro's leap was more than that – a casual display of brute finesse. Peering into the alley far below, Lynx realized the sheer audacity of the jump.
1061.	Stealing a brief upward glance, Mellie took in the precarious dance above before her gaze snapped back to the potential dangers at street level, her pistol steady in her grasp.
1062.	Lynx exchanged several silent gesticulations with Garro, waving and making mimicry of his intended message – quietly also insisting: “The window, open – the window.”
1063.	Garro looked to the window—secured by half-inch metal bars, looked back to Lynx and gave his best shrug of confusion.
1064.	With a flurry of urgent yet silent motions, Lynx beckoned to Garro, pantomiming his wish for the window to be opened. Garro's forehead creased, eyes narrowing in confusion as he tried to make sense of Lynx's gestures. Despite his puzzlement, he dutifully tested the metal of the bars; they were every bit as solid as they looked. He thudded the back of his fist on the adjacent wall; the dull report told him it was dense brick.
1065.	Garro's gaze flickered back to Lynx, whose intermittent blue silhouette was a blur of frantic gesturing against the encroaching shadows. The plan had gone off script; the absence of a fire escape or platform meant improvising on this precarious ledge.
1066.	Garro looked down at his wide feet planted on a thin brick ledge. Not much room to do anything here, he pondered. He looked at the window bars again; Lynx was still emphatically miming instructions.
1067.	Fine, I’ll open it. Gritting his teeth, he laced his thick fingers among the bars; his other hand planted, pushing against the brickwork; his entire body tensed. His muscles began to bulge and protest—tight sinew and taught musculature raised in unified tension.
1068.	He pulled.
1069.	The bars dug cruelly into his fingers, and he pulled.
1070.	He did his best to stifle the guttural roar that boiled from within him, his anchor hand now vascular and pulsing, sweat beading all about him; his skin shimmered in the twilight and caught the flashing blue lights. His body began to vibrate such was the effort he gave.
1071.	His muffled roar could no longer be contained, and he heaved. A fizz of his injector sounded as cascading chemicals flooded his body with a cocktail of drugs no human should ever endure; it was ice in his veins. Every thread of his being, coupled with the reinforced endoskeleton he bore, was a force: pushed into the wall and pulled against the metal bars.
1072.	The bars buckled. His gripping fist ran with blood; he choked down the animalistic roar that threatened to betray their position. Without pain to hold him back, with his muscles now tearing apart. He pulled.
1073.	The brickwork gave way before the bars.
1074.	In a sudden and immense release of all of his effort—he pulled from the wall a sizable array of masonry.
1075.	Having to catch his momentum, his offhand snatches out to grasp the nearby drainpipe.
1076.	His weight and inertia tug at the unwilling anchor as the pipe dislodges and crushes in his grip—beginning to give way, a bolt launching from its housing with a ping that echoed down the alley.
1077.	He pulled his centre of balance back against the wall, heaving in deep breaths to drown oxygen into his system—his mountainous shoulders rose and fell. He turned to look at Lynx.
1078.	Lynx recoiled, the visage before him barely recognizable as Garro. The face before him, with gritted teeth in a clenched jaw, was pain, anger and unabated raw power. The sweaty pulsating jugular veins were a hideous distortion of his features; his eyes were cold death. Lynx shivered. He felt like crying out - paralyzed by an instinctive fear. Whatever this snorting, snarling beast was, it was not Garro.
1079.	The fleeting moment passed; Lynx, still emotionally recoiling, tried to reconcile reality in the gloom, and he saw Garro's face relax into a semblance of his usual self, offering the dislodged brickwork with an almost childlike pride.
1080.	Garro was drenched with sweat, but his body was no longer shaking; he cocked his head, looking at the startled face of Lynx.
1081.	Surveying the alley beneath them, Lynx coyly pointed at an extensive collection of boxes and other discarded urban physical noise. Garro followed Lynx’s indication and did his best to thrust the mangle of metal and brick toward it.
1082.	The noise from the smash of bricks among glass and cans was loud; both Mellie and Gerhardt turned away from their sentry to look back along the darkness of the alley and then up at Garro, who now stood beside a sizable hole in the wall.
1083.	Everyone stopped, they listened. The warbling of music continued; a car engine revved in the distance. They waited.
1084.	No response came; the city did not care.
1085.	The neighbourhood they were in rolled on in time. No sirens sounded. Gerhardt looked into Mellie’s eyes, a desperate unspoken message shared among them. He waved two fingers, indicating that she should return her focus to where she had been watching. If the city didn’t care, neither would he.
1086.	Above them, Garro now held out his bloodied hand, beckoning Lynx. Lynx cast a long gaze at the alley below, attempting to judge the distance of the gap between the gantry where he stood and the outstretched dripping paw of Garro.
1087.	Pulling the strap of his deck tighter, he took a rushed large step and—using his landing leg—sprang forward. His effort was pitifully inadequate.
1088.	Hanging limply in the grip of Garro’s bloodied fist, Lynx’s jacket and shirt were now a cradle. With ease, Garro hoisted Lynx single-handedly into the awaiting hole. Brick and plaster dripped from the open wound of the building as he was unceremoniously delivered into its shattered maw.
 
1089.	Crumpling into a ball of technology and flared arms and legs, Lynx found himself in the darkness of the empty apartment.
1090.	All about him, the ruins of a Netrunner’s nest lay shattered. The silence in the building was solid and complete. But, as he rose to his feet, the cracking of glass and brick debris beneath his feet pushed into the space.
1091.	He eyed the door across from him, its frame ensnared by security cables, just as Garro had said. He pressed a series of commands on his bracer. A dim reddish glow shone about him, illuminating the room beyond the spill of the hallway lights from the door opening.
1092.	This was an impressive nest. High-end gear—now all ruined— littered the room. There was a small kitchenette in one corner, while open internal doors revealed the apartment's further confines: the bathroom, a bedroom, and what looked like a small storage room - a cascade of various personal effects were vomited across the floor.
1093.	Dismissing the now useless technology with a nudge of his foot, he sent a once-valuable network head connection rig skittering aside. This was some serious kit, he mused; the red illumination from his bracer cast a subdued light on a matte black stain on the floor. Blood. There was no need to adjust the light; he recognized the sight too well.
1094.	Looking about with his diffuse, glowing arm, he began searching – Conviction settled in; this had to be the place. With the presence of the computer gear and the fact that it was now a crime scene, reality felt too close. Whatever was happening was happening quickly, and was not in any of their control.
1095.	The bedroom offered little beyond scattered clothing and more grim evidence of a bloodied struggle. The entire space was a tomb of violence and desperation. His foot sent a pile of debris skidding across the floor, adding to the disarray but uncovering nothing of value. He paused, allowing himself a deep breath, seeking a moment of clarity amidst the chaos.
1096.	Garro’s head leaned into the breach, his whisper a low rumble that invaded the sombre stillness of the apartment. “Wha’ d’yuh think?”
1097.	Lynx met the question head-on as he was already returning to the living area. “I think whoever Zoe is… or was, is probably important. But I can see shit in here.”
1098.	He cast a disdainful glance at the heap of fried electronics. “I’m gonna try and scan through this lot, but it all buzzed. Tell Gerhardt I need a hand up here.”
1099.	Garro's nod was almost lost in the shadows, his bulky frame an ill fit for the narrow opening. His disappearance into the dim evening was followed by the sound of his weight clanging hard onto the gantry opposite as he made his way to the alley below.
 
1100.	Lynx had been sitting for a few minutes and had discarded a pile of various memory blocks or other data storage gizmos, all of them dead, when Gerhardt's dark form obscured the dim light that the wall hole gave.
1101.	“What do you need me for? Can’t you just plug in and scan the place?” Gerhardt's tone carried a hint of jest.
1102.	Lynx gestured with a sweep of his arm, the red glow from his bracer casting eerie shadows across the debris. “Jack into what exactly? – but that’s not the point. I don’t think I can find anything on my own here. But your arm, with that, I think I can do something.”
1103.	Gerhardt looked down at the soft, reflective red sheen of his augmented arm. “What of it?”
1104.	Lynx gave a dead-faced stare, “I know what you got in that thing, vibro-sensors, along with all the other nonsense. I think I can jury-rig something with my deck; we can make a sonographic recreation of this entire place, and I can pick it apart digitally far quicker than I could doing it by hand” He let another chunk of twisted metal fall from his hand, thudding mutely onto the carpet.
1105.	“And another thing,” Lynx continued, tapping his temple to indicate Gerhardt’s cybernetic eye, “I also happen to know you got a smart-link in there now. You really should get some decent ICE for your agent. If we ever came up against a half-decent decker, you’d be out cold before we even saw them.”
1106.	Gerhardt's jaw tensed, his pride stung by the suggestion of vulnerability. “Okay, fine, we do this. But you have to do something for my rigs, yeah?”
1107.	Lynx held out a hand and waited in the red gloom. Gerhardt ejected the smart-link and handed the end to Lynx, taking a few steps to be closer, knowing the cable wasn’t long enough to reach much more than a foot or so.
1108.	Lynx pulled his deck about him, tugging at the tight strapping to loosen it and found a suitable input for Gerhardt’s extension. “Not sure if this will feel weird.” He declared only a moment before snapping the cable into the slot.
1109.	Gerhardt did feel something—it was a confusion of fizzing electrics and pulsing warmth. His shoulder and upper arm were still flesh and bone where the arm grafted on, and it was there that it felt the most apparent.
1110.	Lynx looked up at Gerhardt's tight figure; his long black coat and tactical gear made him form a silhouette in the room. “You should probably sit down… and, uh. Put your hand on that wall,” Lynx instructed, nodding toward a bare patch of wall, his eyes already returning to the rapid scroll of code on his deck.
1111.	Gerhardt complied, easing down with a hand pressed against the wall, the odd sensation intensifying. He realised he was dizzy—almost woozy—like alcohol had bypassed his mouth and liver and hit straight into his brain; he blinked and shook his head. “I – this feels... I think I might throw up,” he stammered, struggling to maintain his composure.
1112.	Lynx ignored the threat, absorbed in the revealing complexities of Gerhardt's arm that he hadn't anticipated—military-grade components interwoven with custom smart-link algorithms. It was all more advanced than he had thought. Curiosity piqued, he continued his technical foray, even as Gerhardt's breaths grew ragged with the effort to suppress his nausea.
1113.	The hand's sensors proved more accessible than Lynx had expected. And, building a routine of conflicting pulses, he varied some of the settings to refine what quickly became a very short-ranged sonar of sorts—for all things physical in and around the apartment.
1114.	It took precious moments sat in silence as Gerhardt wheezed nearby. After several terrible attempts and having to reset the entire system twice, Lynx found some settings he felt best suited to what he needed. They would have to move about the apartment and scan from different angles, but this was as good as it got for an improvisation of this nature.
1115.	Their silent survey was punctuated only by the sound of Gerhardt's laboured breathing. When the haphazard survey was complete, Lynx disconnected the smart-link. Gerhardt's strength gave out, sending him to his knees with a throat-clenching, dry, gagging heave.
1116.	Prompted by their unintentional commotion, Lynx listened for movement outside of the door, but it was silent. He was confident that the mechanical shuffle of a droid's approach would be detectable.
1117.	He sat in the kitchenette; his red-glowing arm reflected the most here, and it bathed the corner in a crimson aura, at the centre of which the teal-blue glow of his deck shone.
1118.	“I’m gonna have to jack in, keep a watch, eh?” with that, he pulled out a visor, dropping it over his face, letting his fingers autonomously follow a well-practised sequence that joined his mind to his technology.
1119.	Gerhardt, now regaining composure, the warmth remained, but the fizzing had gone, and his head had cleared rapidly at the moment the cable had been released. Pulling out his pistol, he connected the now loose cable to its side and took up a sentinel's pose against the ragged masonry framing the hallway's entrance.
 
1120.	Gerhardt had barely found his stance when Lynx's sudden movement shattered the quiet. A plate clattered to the floor as Lynx sat up abruptly, swearing with such fervour that it could only herald something monumental. “Holy fuck!” he exclaimed.
1121.	Gerhardt's eyebrows arched in surprise, his stance shifting to match the abruptness of Lynx's outburst.
1122.	Lynx pulled himself together, quickly dismantling his adjoining equipment. “Fuck me… shit.”
1123.	The hurried and expletive actions gave Gerhart reason to ask, “What? What’s wrong?”
1124.	Scrambling to pack his equipment, Lynx paused, “This whole floor. The whole fucking floor!” Lynx’s hushed but shrill voice carried words filled with excitement but tinged by fear.
1125.	The response just confused Gerhardt more. He motioned his hands as though prompting for more than just excitable swearing.
1126.	Lynx held a pause; he realised he’d not used words to convey his thoughts and, taking in a long breath, slowed his thoughts to match the pace of his words. With a dramatic sweep of his arms—red light spiralled about the room—he explained, “It’s not just this apartment, and that is not the real mainframe here,” gesturing dismissively at the ruined screens and parts of the computer he’d strewn about them.
1127.	He halted, pointing toward the bedroom. “We need to go back in there,” he said, his voice imbued with urgency as he took long strides in the direction he indicated, Gerhardt following in his wake.
1128.	Lynx halted, addressed his bracer, pawed at the display and looked at the floor. Kicking aside the mess of clothes, he gingerly stamped a heel at the floor, which dutifully thudded underfoot. “Here,” he pointed, “Rip this up.”
1129.	Gerhardt's augmented strength made short work of the carpet; Lynx looked down, “The boards too—”. Despite not possessing Garro's brute force, the cybernetic enhancements lent him more than human power; Gerhardt swiftly pulled back sections of the boarding to uncover what lay beneath—a metal plate.
1130.	Now hunched over the small splintered breach that had been rent open, Lynx directed the red glow from his arm into the gap. Partially obscured but unmistakable, the edges of an inlaid panel revealed themselves, promising yet more secrets beneath.
1131.	Gerhardt continued to rip away the unwanted flooring, quickly uncovering the entire panel. Taking only a brief moment to assess the immediate scene mentally, he allowed the housing of his arm to rotate away and unfold his micro-tools; spindles of miniature arms extended and worked in quick unison to loosen the fixings of the panel before folding neatly away, his arm resuming a more humanoid form.
1132.	Lifting the panel disclosed thick black cables beneath. In contrast to the apartment's dormant tech, some of the uncovered electronics still flickered with life; connection lights blinked, indifferent to the unfolding events. Lynx locked eyes with Gerhardt, a wild excitement in his gaze. "The whole fucking floor," he whispered again, almost reverently.
1133.	To Gerhardt, Lynx seemed like a prospector who had struck a vein far richer than gold. The discovery's significance was lost on Gerhardt, but he sensed its magnitude in Lynx's feverish actions.
1134.	Having pulled a small roll-out tool kit from somewhere, Lynx was already splicing cables together. Gerhardt stood, leaving Lynx to his intricate task, and returned to the breach in the hallway. Words weren't needed; Gerhardt understood that Lynx would be engrossed in his work for some time.
Chapter Eighteen:	I Am Zecsa, Hear Me Roar.
1135.	An entire day had passed. Lynx had once again left their company and been silent and—like Davie—was not answering any comms. Mellie let out a deep, slow, exhaled sigh that filled the cramped space of her apartment. Resignation and defeat were weighing on her shoulders. A state of inaction had been a welcome reprieve, but the yo-yo cycle of events and inaction was fraying her nerves. 
1136.	Garro had grumbled about his medication, nearly being exhausted. Not having any would be crippling. And so, with nothing more urgent to pull his attention, he had, of his own volition, gone looking for street-docs.
1137.	It wasn’t a street Doc or a clinic he found; instead, a hustler had sold him some pills. Before falling asleep, he told Mellie that the guy promised they would “make the pain go away”. Whatever they were, they’d sent him on a trip, likely as psychedelic as it was physical.
1138.	Melly spent most of her day trying to find where Garro had gone. The hallucinogens he had taken had propelled him through the eastern sector of Central amidst the bustling lower streets crowded with self-important individuals in suits.
1139.	None seemed pleased with the space-headed, man-ape figure who now energetically thrashed and danced on those immaculate streets.
1140.	It had taken her hours to track him down. Internally, she cursed, yet she was silently grateful that he hadn’t been apprehended by law enforcement for causing a disturbance or on narcotics charges—or for any other concocted reasons—most likely because no one would dare confront Garro to subdue him.
1141.	He once again lay across her couch, which might now be broken given the manner of his collapse onto it. His deep snoring, however, was at least a sign that he, at least, was getting some rest.
1142.	Gerhardt had withdrawn to his own quarters, the whereabouts of which were unknown to her. She momentarily imagined him stepping into a vertical box and shutting down like a service android, a glib mental commentary that reflected her perception of his character.
1143.	It was getting late; the lethargy of her body and the drowsiness of her wandering thoughts were tell-tale signs of her need for rest. Her mind was buzzing in protest of the strain of fatigue, the snoring, and the ever-present anxiety of their predicament.
1144.	Leaving the confines of her apartment, she sought the cool evening air on the balcony outside, casually leaning on the railing and allowing what little buffeting breeze there was to splash against her face.
 
1145.	Gerhardt excused himself, knowing Lynx would need time to analyze the data extracted from the apartment the previous night. In Akureyri city, where every second counted, Gerhardt knew the value of time and planned to use it effectively.
1146.	Thankfully, nobody had interrupted him as he set about searching for Davie. For better or worse, the loathsome man was a key to the puzzle box of his world as it currently was.
1147.	Using his limited net skills, he’d found nothing. He’d reached out to some other fixers, asking for a trace job, usually unphased by the grime of their trade – they’d refused to track Davie; the name was toxic.
1148.	This lack of leads was a revelation in itself. Gerhardt's ability to unearth people in the city's intricate maze had always been a point of pride. Yet, Davie had managed to disappear intentionally, a thought that both irked and intrigued Gerhardt. Designs can be unravelled, he mused, but some puzzles are more complex than others.
1149.	Concluding his search for the evening, Gerhardt acknowledged the bitter truth — Davie's whereabouts would remain a mystery, at least for now. The thought of his long-term partner leaving him adrift made Gerhardt reflect on the value of their continued interaction.
1150.	He pulled up an overlay in his cybernetic eye and checked the time. Just after 8 p.m.—Whatever Lynx was doing was taking longer than he’d expected. He prompted his agent to call Mellie.
 
1151.	Mellie allowed the slow evening breeze to wash over her, bringing a teasing hint of autumn's chill against her skin. It was moments like this where she was glad to have no implants – for feeling wholly human. Even the rough petroleum putrescence that waved through the breeze and annoyed her nostrils made her feel more alive, more real.
1152.	A single far-off gunshot cracked and echoed in the falling darkness. She brushed off the sound—just some unfortunate choom getting mugged. It wasn't that she was indifferent; among the city's many people, Mellie always tried to have compassion for her fellow people. It was simply an everyday occurrence in the southern Skrim, an area not known for being pleasant. Her life had hardened her, and as big as her compassion was, every person had limits. The city pushed against those limits.
1153.	Her apartment was set within a complex that had little by way of the usual sparking neon signs. It felt reassuring to see the darkness when all about her, the world shimmered with the ever-persistent haze of a city that never rested.
1154.	She looked out over the small courtyard and parking area several floors below, painted blue and grey in the falling night; a solitary cat stealthily patrolled among the few bags of garbage that had piled up. She watched with curiosity as the feline carefully and with poise moved about – and then pounced.
1155.	The cat's sudden pounce sent a jolt through Mellie—her nerves were on edge. How long until some silent hunter pounces on me? Shaking her head, banishing the thought, she turned and walked back inside. Perhaps trying to sleep again would settle her.
1156.	In contrast to the ones used by everyone she knew, her agent was a diminutive, handheld device marred by scratches. It featured a simple screen on one side. In stark opposition to the norm, she harboured a deep aversion to the idea of an implanted version—an otherwise mundane and widespread enhancement. The mere thought of incorporating any fragment of the corporate apparatus within herself was enough to make her sick.
1157.	Despite its necessity in the modern world, she appreciated the distance it provided, connecting to her apartment's communication suite for citywide network access. Presently, it blinked a rhythmic green, signalling a missed call and a waiting message—perhaps from Lynx?
1158.	“What in the fuck did you and that Neanderthal do to my club?!” Davie’s unmistakable accent was tinny and buzzed with distortion from the agent's tiny speaker. His anger strangled by the limits of the device, but he spoke clearly and slowly.
1159.	“I swear – Once I’m back in town, you fucking owe me. No… No – I fucking OWN YOU! You will work for me until I’m happy again...”
1160.	Mellie's sigh was heavy with a newfound depth of resignation—as if being targeted by Davie on top of everything else was just another line on her ledger of woes. Sure, why not have Davie out for blood as well? The corps, Cit-Sec, every piece of shit in this pile of shit city. She mused.
1161.	Davie’s voice continued to rant, his tirade becoming nothing more than a background auditory fog—whatever he was saying was just vented into the night as Mellie sat in thought.
1162.	The message ended, and the machine beeped nonchalantly as another message began.
1163.	“Mel, I’ve not heard from Lynx, and I guess you hadn’t either, but I think Davie might be up to something. Been trying all day but– well—” Gerhard's usual monotonous voice dropped slightly after the pause, “… well, it doesn’t matter, but I think we need to meet tomorrow. I’ll figure out where Lynx is. We can’t let this drag out.”
1164.	The agent beeped again, its electronic chirps indifferent to the urgency of its content. Garro snorted and grunted in his sleep, shifting on the couch. Mellie glanced at him, then back at the agent, her lips pressed together in silent agreement. It was time to get some rest.
 
1165.	Tucked between a mechanic’s yard and a fragrant bakery, the alley was rancid solitude away from the city's bustle. Shadows mingled with stray beams of sunlight in this narrow space, where the sweet smell of bread clashed with the faint tang of motor oil. Cobblestones and concrete underfoot showed years of urban wear, scattered with remnants of daily life. High walls framed a sliver of sky above, its light dimly illuminating the alley. This overlooked nook, caught between past and present, was the place of lost things.
1166.	Under a cracked, inoperable street lamp, Lynx had spent the previous day siphoning power from the mechanics’ garage. His visor lay on the ground beside him, next to the remnants of a soy-patty sandwich and the flattened remains of a carbonated drink.
1167.	He'd fashioned a makeshift tarp from some cardboard to shield himself from the filth that now lay stained, crushed and spotted with damp from overuse. Apart from a brief respite to consume half a sandwich, a few mouthfuls of flavoured liquid and a moment to rise and stretch cramping muscles, he had been sitting there all day. His deck was hot to the touch, and the strain of prolonged visor use had left his eyes stinging.
1168.	Still sitting and stretching again to alleviate the stiffness that had taken root in his joints, Lynx tried to take stock. Time had a way of slipping by unnoticed when he was jacked in, and his body protested the extended stillness. Extending his arms to ease his shoulders, he slumped back against the dusty wall. Looking about the alley, he thought to himself, always too easy to stay hidden, mocking his attempts to find someone.
1169.	Zoe—or Zecsa, as he'd discovered she preferred—was a prodigy. Her coding was impeccable and even more robust than what he'd attributed to Stepper during his dive into Davie's complex web. The data he'd extracted from the apartment's built-in computer was exquisite.
1170.	Zecsa had hard-line connections interwoven throughout the entire city—highly illegal and, at such a scale, demanding expert skills to remain concealed. Her code served as a bastion against the most common intrusions. There were portions of the encoded data he had extracted which, he was sure, would defy decoding by anyone but the most adept Corpo runners or the top-tier Deckers he knew.
1171.	The fragments he'd pieced together revealed the silhouette of a Netrunner, an artist whose medium was the binary song of flawless programming. Zecsa's work was meticulously ordered and armoured, constructed with a 'defence-first' philosophy; this was the shadow of someone who lived beyond the reach of the law and knew how to survive. Lynx related all too well to that.
1172.	Having pieced together some of the puzzle and abusing some of Zecsa’s existing code, Lynx implemented a new network of his own devising—the garage had a media connection he was piggy-backed onto—he had spent the day worming into the wider networks of the city, his mind had echoed and pulled in the ethereal.
1173.	His equipment paled in comparison to Zecsa's arsenal, and his deck strained under the load, yet it was a passably strained substitute to operate with as he shaped what seemed to be an answer.
1174.	Zecsa had, just like Stepper, scoured bulletin boards, secured a job, and been in contact with Davie. These were the easy trails to follow. However, what piqued his interest was a sequence of logged system activities that, to anyone who knew to look, could resemble an attack on her infrastructure.
1175.	The narrative clicked for Lynx: the apartment had been a crime scene, and he surmised that Zoe—or Zecsa—was dead. Whatever this attack was, it would likely have been triangulation to find her. He could not pick out the data to confirm any of this, but the picture looked right.
1176.	Sat in the gloaming, his Deck audibly hummed as it ran another decryption attempt. As dusk crawled into the sky, the only light in the alley shed from his visor and the deck’s screens, he was exhausted. For all his efforts, all he had gained was a feeling; Zecsa had been hunted, and given her prowess, it must have required immense resources or effort.
1177.	He might not crack the secrets she left behind, but—using what few crumbs he had been able to find, he could trace her echoes. Everyone left trails. It was hard to see in the wider net, and with skill like Hers, she’d have been a true spectre among the code. But there would be other signs he could look for – everyone, even spectres, left an impression.
1178.	Taking a bite of the now cold soy-patty, and scrunching his eyes tightly closed for physical relief, he lifted the visor over his head once more. The encroaching darkness signalled the passing of time, and he was desperate to unearth any lead on Zecsa's affairs—a mystery that he and his cohorts had unwittingly become a part of.
 
1179.	The improvised connection algorithms demanded more power than his deck could comfortably supply. As he delved into his simulated realm, the instability was unmistakably evident. His usual environs flickered, phasing in and out of existence as the code he had cobbled together emitted tracking pings that sporadically failed. He could feel a headache burgeoning in meat-space—a clear warning sign.
1180.	Feedback like this, left unchecked, could blister his brain in a second—He’d never again remove the visor, just some jackhead dead in an alley. He checked over the operating parameters and adjusted some of the input to temper the processing of his deck.
1181.	What little data he had decoded—mundane surface-level stuff—now was gathered into a floating glowing box icon. He tapped it, and it spilt the data about him. A handful of unencrypted messages were among its content. He picked through them. Only one felt important: [Jiao, I’ve had a pingback on my primary hub. I think they found me. I need your help, buddy.]
1182.	There was no way to trace where the message had come from or been sent to, not a timestamp or any way to verify if it was related to what he’d seen in the apartment, just some raw text. The fact it wasn’t encrypted, the words, and what he had seen gave the message a chilling finality.
1183.	He tapped the box icon again, and the data that surrounded him blinked out of being.
1184.	He was aware that one of his tracing programs was blinking for his attention. An animated floating icon of a dog adopting a hunting point indicated that it had completed and was reporting. He reached out to touch it.
1185.	《Hello, Lynx.》
1186.	The words, both the floating text and the echoing digital voice, pinned Lynx motionless. The construct digital world he was inside of was a safe haven; his defensive ICE programs weren’t triggered; this voice, this introduction, could not exist here.
1187.	The text shimmered and changed, and layers of different recreations of speech followed as it displayed;
1188.	《You should not be tracking me. It is dangerous.》
1189.	Lynx's thoughts flew into action, his commands translating into the digital language of his deck.
1190.	Track this connection.
1191.	Programs, each displayed as a different icon or simple graphic, churned to meet his request. One of his remaining automated programs circled about him and the glittering text, pulling against threads of code.
1192.	He had to keep whatever this connection was alive. He pleaded into the shimmering void, “Zecsa?! Your apartment’s wrecked. I thought you’d been killed?”
1193.	《Do not try to track this. I do not wish to be found.》
1194.	His digital body writhed among his programs. He pushed, pulled, and moulded them to his whim. Each was a tiny element of the larger workings of his deck, an extension of him.
1195.	Despite his digital manipulation, the connection dissolved as quickly as it had appeared, leaving no trace behind.
1196.	He summoned all of his programs to him, each one feeding a shred of data to him; from the scraps, he began moulding and creating a glowing floating map of the city. He began to draw iridescent lines, joining the geocentric information he has. A puzzling thought struck him amidst this process– A connection hub in Central, a through line that branches northward. Where did that come from?
1197.	Dot by dot, line by line, a fragmented picture emerges, a shattered star of irrelevant details—no single defining end or beginning. The only stand-out information is that somewhere east of the Docklands, there was a jumper network—part of Zecsa’s connection code that had received three pings—more than any other in his broken puzzle. It was a meaningless piece of information. But that, he thought, was as good a place to begin as any.
1198.	Amidst his fervent analysis, a question gnawed at him—Who was that, how did they get into my deck, and how do they know my name?
1199.	In the dark alley behind a busy garage, next to the glowing, sweating, rigid form of a young decker jacked into their deck whining at near overload, a mongrel dog snatches the remnants of a soy-patty and disappears into the darkness.
Chapter Nineteen:	 A Man Who Lives Fully.
1200.	Anthony Dávilo had woken up with a crick in his neck; his morning had been as rigid and formulaic as it had always been. The section house greeted him, its dimly lit corridors shrouded in a perpetual sense of detachment from the waking world. Each day began with the same motions: review the night's incident reports, log into the systems, and engage in the ritual of exchanging morning niceties with Elouise.
1201.	Elouise, the ever-cheerful soul, offered her usual playful jest about coffee. A corner of Anthony's lips curled into a half-smile, an echo of his remaining warmth in a life that had grown cold and mechanical. He shuffled through the fist of papers as he walked to his desk, each sheet a terse summary of the city's overnight tribulations: three homicides, a carjacking, sixteen drug offences—a relentless tally of Akureyri’s daily life.
1202.	His gaze swept over the text automatically, each report indistinguishable from the last. It could have been the same narrative as yesterday, the day before, or any predictable tomorrow. The crimes, the culprits, the casualties; it all merged into one indistinct, dismal canvas.
1203.	In a city where the policing was often a parody of itself, Anthony and his peers, the Peace Keepers, were charged with the Sisyphean task of carving order from the entropy, one petty criminal at a time.
1204.	Unlike the clowns in City Security forces, he and his fellow Peace Keepers—Sheriffs as they were known—were expected to actually make a difference.
1205.	It was always the same: Pick out the most heinous of the crimes and try to bring just one bag of flesh in for justice. Bringing someone to justice had lost its meaning.
1206.	Justice had become a distant, hazy concept, like a mirage shimmering on the horizon. More often than not, he found himself wrestling with two-bit street punks, their minds addled by fuzzers or whatever the latest designer narcotic was. And, all too frequently, his day ended with another call to the morgue. It wasn't the life he'd dreamed of, but in Akureyri, perfect lives were in short supply.
1207.	Despite the pervasive disillusionment, a stubborn streak of optimism persisted within Anthony. Deep down, he held onto the conviction that his vigil on these tumultuous streets mattered, that out of countless residents, there were individuals who silently appreciated the sheriffs' presence in places where Cit-Sec looked the other way.
1208.	The station chief's abrupt intrusion shattered his reverie. The loud thud of his desk being kicked resonated through the office, scattering papers fluttered to the floor like autumn leaves.
1209.	"You need to get your sorry ass down to the rig yards. Rajan's out today, probably shitting out a half-ton of neat-meat," the chief barked, assigning yet another mundane task to Anthony's uneventful day.
1210.	He sighed, knowing that the tidying up he'd have to do when he returned to the section house was a metaphor for his entire existence—an endless cycle of picking up the pieces in a city forever on the brink of collapse.
 
1211.	The first light of dawn crept over the Akureyri skyline, casting a soft glow on the Docklands' jumble of buildings. In the relative quiet of the noodle bar, three figures were gathered at a table near the back. Outside, the neon lights stuttered, their spasmodic dance barely penetrating the interior gloom.
1212.	Mellie, alert despite the hour, watched the street with a practised eye while Garro slumped in his seat, his massive form a stark contrast to the delicate bowls on the table. Across from them, Gerhardt's eyes were hidden in the dim light, his features set in a contemplative frown. They sat not just as individuals, but as reluctant comrades, bound by the unraveling threads of a conspiracy that had drawn them into its heart.
1213.	Gerhardt sagged under the burden of unending searches, each failure adding weight to his weary shoulders. Mellie had briefed him on Davie's menacing message—a point of intrigue, certainly, but Lynx's early morning communique had cast a larger shadow.
1214.	Amidst the Docklands' relentless hum of industry, a complex array of bustling metal gantries and scaffolds came to life. These towering structures wove together, creating a vivid backdrop that encapsulated the area's constant ebb and flow of work and progress. This was the heart of the Docklands, a place of ceaseless activity and industrial might.

1215.	However, Lynx had mentioned a different location: a place just east of the Docklands, beyond the river head, which cut away the eastern side of the city and marked the boundary of the south face of the Skrim, the lower rig yards began, characterized by an industrial void. Less touched by the constant motion found in the Docklands, this area housed a series of disused dry docks and the beginnings of a shanty town, stretching further out into a more desolate and forgotten part of the city.
1216.	The rig yards consisted of a vast area for piles of metal shipping containers, broad open tarmac areas used by automated freight rigs for storage or refuelling, interspersed by vast warehouses and tall vehicle maintenance towers. 
1217.	Gerhardt once again dryly recited the laboured message he’d been passed, only to make sure he’d understood it rather than for anyone else’s benefit. "Lynx has been deep in the net, tracking Zecsa's codes across the city. Say’s he’s been talking with her. He stumbled upon something—east of the Docklands, a hub with odd traffic. We just have to find it."
1218.	Garro arched his back yawning, the remnants of some kibble packs scattered on the table before him, his eyes heavy with dark rings hung under them. His emptied bowl was gripped in his hands, sapping the remaining warmth and pulling the lasting aroma to his face. For the first time since having met Garro, Gerhardt saw him not as a walking weapon but as a man, a tired and thread-worn man.
1219.	Mellie was finishing a drink but seemed to be fearing better than either Garro or himself. A little sleep had put a spark back in her eyes. She too had quizzed Lynx’s instructions, but a cab was already on its way, and Gerhardt planned to shadow them on his bike. She winced a thought: why is he always going alone?
1220.	Beside Mellie, wrapped over with her long coat, the unsubtle shapes of her machine gun sat awkwardly in the fourth seat of the table. It had been an interesting discussion, but Mellie was adamant, with things as they were, where she went, the gun would, too.
1221.	Gerhardt was thankful Lynx hadn’t sent them to investigate Northside; a gun like that would get scanned up in seconds. He admired the forthright and powerful determination but did not enjoy the lack of subtlety and the threat of discovery.
1222.	As the noodle bar began to stir with the city's morning pulse—greasy, sweaty night-shifters passed fresher, more animated versions of themselves. Their table remained an island of sombre determination. They weren't just preparing for another day but steeling themselves for what they hoped would be a pathway to an ending.
 
1223.	The cab pulled up and delivered them outside the street-long overhang of an enormous warehouse; from inside, the clang and grind of automated machinery told of a workday in motion. There would be only slightly less activity during the night, but during the day, this place was a perpetual grind of productivity and logistics.
1224.	Mellie and Garro waited as Gerhardt found somewhere more discrete to park his bike and join them. The day, draped in overcast skies, breathed gusts down the long, barren road, funnelling the wind into concentrated streams and gusting litter along the tarmac.
1225.	Approaching at a measured pace, Gerhardt returned to them while container shifters barreled past, their haste amplifying the wind's chill as it clawed away the remnants of summer's warmth.
1226.	Without her coat, Mellie had only a lighter jacket. She carried the bundle of coat and machine gun under one arm, the weapon’s carry strap somewhat curiously hung over a shoulder, making a mockery of its concealment.
1227.	Garro fidgeted, tugging at the tape holding his shirt together—a makeshift repair for the tear earned in their recent brawl. The garment, already snug, had succumbed to discomfort, prompting his constant adjustments.
1228.	Now with them, Gerhardt had his jacket whipped about his legs by the wind. His hands stayed pocketed, not just for warmth but to pin the fabric down, his own weapon remaining unseen. Here, where Cit-Sec's gaze seldom reached, firearms might be less taboo, yet prudence prevailed; surveillance was omnipresent, and indiscretion could be costly.
1229.	“What are we looking for?” Mellie's voice sliced through the bluster.
1230.	Gerhardt's gaze swept the scene—the awning's refuge, the warehouse's imposing flank, nothing more than that met the eye. Across the street, a scattered archipelago of containers lay under the watch of a gantry crane, its slow, deliberate movements reminiscent of a giant heron sifting through a dull metallic pond.
1231.	“A communications tower,” Gerhardt responded, his shrug betraying a rare glimpse of uncertainty.
1232.	“We’ll just have to check this whole block”, he mused, eyeing the warehouse's vastness. A trek around it would devour hours on foot.
1233.	Garro leaned into the conversation, “I need to pee.”
1234.	Mellie, sparing him no glance, gestured dismissively towards a shadowed corner of the building's buttress. He shuffled off obediently.
1235.	“Should we split up? You go that way,” she swung her pointing hand to the roadway leading north, “Garro and I can head this way”, arching her arm and swapping the pointed finger into a thumb, indicating behind her.
1236.	Gerhardt once more studied their surroundings, his cybernetic eye taking some measurements. “No, we’ll stick together.” He waved two fingers past Mellie and, with a nod, continued, “We’ll go south and come back around,” he raised his voice, “Hey Gee, when you’re done, catch up.” With that, he strode past Mellie, setting the course for their search.
 
1237.	Anthony's fingers danced over the logs once more, the frustration bubbling beneath his professional exterior. Three hijackings in two weeks, and we've been sitting on complaints, he thought. It was a recurring nightmare of criminality that festered like an unhealed wound. The city's underbelly was teeming with malcontents, and for Rajan, the sheer magnitude of responsibilities had caused these cases to be overlooked and had turned these cases cold.
1238.	The truth was simple but painful: the sheriffs couldn't fill every gap left by Cit-Sec. They had to choose their battles wisely in a city where chaos lurked around every corner. Rajan missed this, but now it's on me, he mused, accepting the task with a sense of duty rather than fervour.
1239.	Exiting his car, Anthony secured his duty pistol. Just another mess to clean up, he thought, feeling the familiar weight of the gun as he headed toward the rig controller's office. His steps were steady, his mind clear. ‘nother day in the grind.
1240.	Before he could make it more than a dozen yards, a sound that never failed to quicken his pulse cut through the air—a gunshot, close enough to be too close for comfort. His senses heightened; adrenaline flooded into his veins. Was this the onset of another hijacking? Perhaps today, he'd have the chance to make a difference.
1241.	Another gunshot followed, then several in quick succession. Whatever was unfolding demanded his immediate attention. With swift efficiency, Anthony reacted instinctively, drawing his pistol and sprinting towards the danger. Each pounding footstep cracked against the tarmac with a sense of urgency as he closed the distance.
1242.	As he ran, he snatched the signal-com from its pocket. He shouted into it with determination and concern, "Station one-three, Dávilo. I’m at Eastern slip three near Skybridge Shipping offices, headed west. Multiple gunshots, am proceeding on foot. Requesting immediate backup. Over." A crackled, indifferent response acknowledged him.
1243.	The sporadic gunshots had given way to an unrelenting onslaught of automatic gunfire. The typical rolling symphony of the rig yards—the hums, clanks, and engines—was now drowned out by the relentless chatter of weapons. 
1244.	As he ran, Anthony's heart hammered against his ribcage, his gut clenched with uncertainty.
1245.	As the gunfire grew more intense, he realized– This is no hijacking, his gut tightening with a heavy premonition. I'm running into a gang war.
1246.	What fortune that I’m here and not Raj?
 
1247.	“Mel!” Gerhardt's urgent shout was lost under the relentless thunder of Mellie's machine gun.
1248.	His frantic waving behind the metal drums went unnoticed in the blaze of muzzle flashes. Realizing the futility, he crouched lower, his pistol leading the way as he edged around the drum's corner. The dual perspectives of his natural and cybernetic vision, each with its own colour palette, disoriented him momentarily.
1249.	A few yards behind the exchanging bullets, shielded from the worst by an abandoned forklift telehandler, Garro had, in a fleshy mockery of that machinery, lifted one of the heavily augmented thugs and had him pinned against the wall. Usually, this would strangle a person, but this guy’s entire head was a crude jigsaw of metal; he struggled against Garro’s grip, landing ignorable punches on Garro’s shoulders and head.
1250.	At the extreme opposite side from Garro, the other hoodlums were ducked low behind a brick wall. Dust and splinters of brick danced as Mellie’s gun spat death.
1251.	She was deliberately taking only brief bursts. Having only one belt of ammo for the gun meant this unfortunate showdown had to end quickly, and, even prone as she was, she was exposed—an unlucky shot would be all it took. Her only defence was bullets headed downrange, preventing bullets from heading her way.
1252.	“MEL!” Gerhardt yelled louder, trying to convey an urgent message with wild hand signals, but Mellie's focus remained on the wall ahead, her sights fixed on the hidden assailants.
1253.	Garro, now refusing to let the ‘borged freak be the better of him, was landing punch after punch into his midriff. “Y’got a metal head? You don’t got metal guts.” He joyfully growled as each haymaker slammed the more diminutive guy’s torso into the metal-clad wall he was pinned against.
1254.	It was meaningless after the third blow; his ragdoll plaything’s lower ribs were shattered, internal organs burst and torn. Garro’s punches had already caused massive, irreparable internal damage—Garro did not realise the thug was unconscious already. He paused his assault only when a bullet zipped through one of his calf muscles.
1255.	Gerhardt had to double-take what was going on about them; his eye had been tracking several targets, but it reported a bullet impact from an impossible trajectory.
1256.	Mellie wasn’t putting bullets where he needed them, and he was pinned behind some barrels. No way it came from the two Mellie has pinned, his mind buffeted with tactical contemplation.
1257.	He glanced about to check their flank. To his surprise, it was beyond Garro, at the other end of the building, that he saw someone else with a gun levelled at them. We’re surrounded? he thought, a cold twist of fear knotting his stomach.
1258.	Another burst from Mellie’s gun peppered the brick wall the thugs had hidden behind. Gerhardt was gesturing again, more frantically now.
1259.	Mellie filed away her irritation for later—right now, they were in the thick of it, and distractions could prove deadly.
 
1260.	Anthony's fingers maintained their disciplined hold on the pistol, a familiar steadiness amidst the tempest of gunfire. The weapon's heft was a tangible assurance as he breathed in the acrid air, his body instinctively adopting a shooter's poise. The most towering figure in the fray drew his focus, a behemoth that dominated the field of battle.
1261.	He squeezed the trigger, and the weapon responded with a sharp, deafening crack. The bullet found its mark, likely hitting the big guy's lower body due to the ballistic drop at this range. Anthony's aim was true, but what happened next left him bewildered and apprehensive.
1262.	The hulking figure remained motionless, unfazed by the gunshot wound. It was a surreal moment that sent a shiver of uncertainty down Anthony's spine.
1263.	Psycho? He questioned inwardly, the idea of the twisted melding of man and machine that had pushed the individual to the brink of madness. In that fraction of a second, he regretted the absence of a way to call for Cit-Sec's elite troops, renowned for dealing with such nightmarish scenarios.
1264.	Training kicked in, an ingrained response born from years on the force. Seek cover. Re-evaluate. Await backup. Anthony knew he couldn't stand his ground any longer, not in the face of this unknown threat.
1265.	Anthony pivoted to move.
1266.	No– he twisted; No– He was being twisted.
1267.	He was falling.
 
1268.	Gerhardt peered through the dissipating venting gas.
1269.	One eye shut to the world while the other, cyber-enhanced eye, painted the scene in hues of chilling blues and warm pinks. A bloom of bright pink erupted in the centre of his target's chest—a stark contrast to the cold blue silhouette that crumbled.
1270.	He allowed himself a brief moment of satisfaction, feeling the recoil's dissipating reverberation through the steel of his cybernetic arm—not surrounded now.
1271.	With a cautious glance towards Mellie, Gerhardt extended his weapon past the protective embrace of the barrels, scanning for any lingering dangers. His cybernetic eye flickered through spectral modes, each shift revealing different contours and secrets of the battlefield.
 
1272.	Anthony Dávilo's breaths were ragged, his fingers grasping the signal-com with a grip that was quickly weakening. The metallic tang of copper filled his senses, drowning out the tales of gunfire and chaos around him.
1273.	His desperate calls for backup dissolved into wet, gurgling sounds, sending spattered blood over his face.
1274.	His duty pistol fell from his failing grasp, clanking against the concrete with a finality that echoed in his fading consciousness.
1275.	His lifeblood pooled on the ground beneath him, a dark crimson painting depicting the brutality of the world he had tried to protect. Desperation clawed at his insides as he fought for one more breath, one more heartbeat.
1276.	The world around him blurred into streaks of unrecognizable shapes, the vibrant essence of the cityscape dimming to greys.
1277.	Each laboured breath felt like a dagger in his chest.
1278.	Nothing made sense.
1279.	He clawed at the ground, his fingers leaving crimson trails on the concrete.
1280.	The world grew distant, the essence of reality faded.
1281.	The cacophony of the gunfight became a distant memory, replaced by a slow calm. 
1282.	In the quietus of his final moments, the city seemed to cradle him in a bittersweet embrace, one last gesture from the place he had sworn to protect.
1283.	A city that had witnessed countless tragedies like his.
1284.	With a final sigh, Anthony Dávilo's life slipped away. 
1285.	Another report sheet in another situation brief folder, another clean-up.
 
1286.	Garro hurled the lifeless body of his victim aside, a macabre final punctuation of his assault. Pulling his weight against the torn calf, he awkwardly launched himself into a trot, his movements blending determination and necessity. His path was set—toward the remnants of the fight, his roar a primal challenge that seemed to vibrate the very air of the rig yards, causing the chains to shiver in their echoes.
1287.	One of the hoodlums, panic etched across his face, burst from behind cover, wildly discharging his pistol in their direction.
1288.	His shots went awry, a chaotic dance of death that hit both nothing and everything.
1289.	Mellie's quick reflexes and bullets turned his denim jacket into a canvas of gruesome crimson geysers, the bass-heavy chatter of her gun sending clouds of dust and debris swirling around her.
1290.	As Garro raced past his concealed position, Gerhardt, synchronized with the chaos, emerged from behind the barrels, his arm extending in a perfect line with his pistol. His cybernetic eye rapidly calculated trajectories and probabilities, guiding his hand with digital precision. The trigger pull was a mere formality—the outcome, determined by the cold calculus of his enhancements.
1291.	Ahead, bricks erupt in a violent spray and beyond them, a gruesome ejection of blood as the last of their assailants is sent flying, a puppet cut from its strings.
1292.	It wasn’t a perfect shot, but given it was impossible to see the target, ripping off a shoulder was good enough.
1293.	Garro arrived, a storm of interlaced hammering fists that swiftly persuaded the still writhing and screaming figure to embrace silence.
1294.	The sound of the last gunshots sonorously lingered among the harsh acoustics of warehouse walling and metal containers for a moment.
1295.	In the abrupt silence that followed, Gerhardt's gaze swept across the aftermath, the electronic affirmation of their foes' defeat flashing across his vision. He caught Mellie's attention with a shout, pointing to an ambiguous figure in the distance. His voice, amplified by the stillness, demanded her focus. "Who the fuck is that?"
1296.	Mellie turned her gaze following Gerhardt's pistol, then swivelled her attention back to him. "WHO'S THAT?" she shouted, her senses still dominated by the gunfire's echo, oblivious to his previous question.
1297.	He had walked to her and loudly expressed as close as possible to her ear, “Check them!” waving behind him as he holstered his gun. He continued his stride toward the mystery ballistic interlocutor. He stopped before reaching the corpse, then abruptly pivoted and sprinted back to the others.
1298.	Garro was sat among the viscera, his shirt missing, tying a ragged tourniquet about his wounded leg as Mellie arrived. She looked about the bodies. In her mind, she played the events. This was too much of a coincidence; the thought rushed into being.
1299.	She glanced across the road where this had all begun. The base of the communications tower had its control panels torn open, wires hung straggled about, and one of the thugs that had prompted this fight lay there; Gerhardt had put two bullets in him.
1300.	Mellies’s thoughts raced; why would these guys be messing with a comms tower? Why would it be a tower where we had been told to search for one? Why…
1301.	A physical nudge from Gerhardt broke her focus, his heavy breathing a testament to the intensity of their skirmish. Her ears were still recovering, but his urgent message needed no sound—his lips clearly formed the word "Badge."
Chapter Twenty:	The Ticking Bomb of Time.
1302.	Sweating and huddled together, they rested momentarily. Gerhardt had led the way, skillfully avoiding cameras and doubling back to find alternate routes. Burdened by Garro's weight on her shoulder, Mellie supported his uneven sprint. Garro's pain was numb, yet his muscles had seized, and no chemical could ease the physical trauma he'd endured. The gun he carried for Mellie had seared his skin, marking his neck and shoulder.
1303.	Together, they had rushed away from the scene of the fight. Gerhardt had tried to reach Lynx to help get directions but to no avail.
1304.	The edge of the drydocks was a wasteland; they’d bustled through a hobo camp—gaunt, dirty and pleading faces and hollow eyes followed their escape but had seen nobody else.
1305.	The remains of a ruptured cargo container provided their only cover, its wrecked shell offering a cloak of obscurity. The distance they had traversed was their reprieve, placing them well beyond the reach of security patrols. Gerhardt had measured their progress, and he concluded in his mind– even if reinforcements had been called, this was now far enough away to render any response inconsequential.
1306.	Garro grimaced at his leg. “Gonna need a ripper. My knee ain't right.”
1307.	Mellie glanced at the injury and leaned in to pull the shirt tourniquet tighter. “Don’t worry, Gee, we’ll get back into the Skrim just as soon as we can get a cab out here,” she reassured.
1308.	Garro’s voice was soft gravel, “My meds are out.” He wasn't expressing sorrow, but his still panting chest and his eyes told stories of a man wrecked with worry.
1309.	“You okay?” Mellie had dropped her register with the ice-cold realisation of what he had just said.
1310.	He snorted a muffled “hmmmfff” to respond.
1311.	She had snapped her attention about. “Gerhardt, the cab–how long?” Her question was sharp, urgent. He raised an eyebrow in defence against her abrupt question.
1312.	Garro pulled himself to sit against the sheet metal. His sweating face shifted to a grimace.
1313.	Gerhardt followed Mellie’s question, “I called just a few minutes ago. I don’t think they’d be here before…”
1314.	The answer was cut short by a growing scream from Garro. Louder than the gunfight and with enough force that their shelter shook.
1315.	“Oh shit.” Mellie's words were swallowed by his relentless cries.
1316.	One of Garro’s fists punched through the metal shell of the container, and his other—still bandaged hand—clawed up chunks of tarmac from the floor.
1317.	His scream punctured both of them. Deep, continuous - It was the ruptured reality of pure torment. His still mobile leg kicked, sending Mellie’s heavy gun skittering across the ground. He cried.
1318.	His every muscle was fire, and his brain was now overloaded with nothing but sensory pain. The embedded endoskeleton that made him near invincible now became conduits of torture. He was a bucking bull of terrified suffering.
1319.	Gerhardt and Mellie both backed away as he contorted and screamed. Neither of them would be able to stop him. They watched; Mellie clamped her hands to her ears; Gerhardt reflexively had brought his cybernetic arm up to defend himself.
1320.	The guttural final roar ripping from Garro’s lungs as he arched upward on his shoulders and feet, every one of his tearing muscles projecting his pain into the heavens. The cry was itself a weapon that assaulted the soul. It was the conjunction of every nerve fired at once in unison. He collapsed—silent—his body still twitching with the aftershocks of muscles firing.
1321.	“Oh- shit”, Mellie repeated as tears ran on her cheeks.
1322.	“Fuck,” Gerhardt exclaimed quietly.
1323.	The stillness about them felt wrong, and Gerhardt centred his thoughts. “Is he… will he be okay?” he slowly approached Garro’s body and placed his augmented hand on his chest, feeling the erratic heartbeat, the uneven breaths, the muscle spasms. “His heart is going crazy, going to need more than a ripper.”
 
1324.	The cab pushed through the increasingly dense traffic. “This ain’t no trauma wagon. If he bleeds on my seats or pukes, it's gonna cost you extra,” The driver hollered, his voice shifting through a buzzing speaker.
1325.	“Shut the fuck up and drive.” Gerhardt snapped back, his voice as hard and decisive as flint striking steel.
1326.	Garro sprawled across the back of the cab, his massive frame spilling into every crevice. Mellie and Gerhardt were wedged into the remaining space. The ride was short in minutes but felt like an eternity, each second laden with the weight of Garro's silent suffering. Gerhardt's carbon-fibre fingers, resting on Garro's chest, felt the erratic thrum of a heart fighting through pain.
1327.	“Stop here!” Mellie shouted, smacking her palm against the partition.
1328.	Gerhardt half fell from the opening door, pulling at Garro’s shoulders. Mellie bundled out after him. She looked up. The bold, bright, clean, illuminated sign: [Phenodyn Clinic]. She hated it. She turned to help Gerhardt drag Garro inside.
1329.	The bystanders about them gasping, one younger wage-slave let a slight squeak at the sight of the immobile flesh-mountain being hauled across the untarnished paving slabs, leaving a trail of blood and sweat.
1330.	As Gerhardt continued to heave Garro into the lobby, Mellie charged to the reception desk. The receptionist, a tiny brunette in pleasant attire, recoiled in shock. Mellie’s gun slid on its strapping as she came to a stop, slamming into the front of the desk.
1331.	“Get a Doc!” Mellie crowed, her off-hand pointing behind her—she heaved in deep breaths.
1332.	The receptionist’s eyes danced about the lobby– Gerhardt’s dark coat, the mound of flesh he dropped to the floor, this dusty, sweaty, crazed-looking woman with a large gun.
1333.	“GET A DOC!” Mellie repeated with urgency, forcing her words into the skull of the petite woman in front of her.
1334.	The timidity of the woman now receiving Mellie’s ire had frozen her in place.
1335.	Mellie ripped a cred-stick from her jacket’s pocket, tearing the pocket in doing so. She slammed the stick and the corner of fabric with a flat palm onto the desk. Glaring at it and then back at the receptionist to detail her payment.
1336.	“Get - a - fucking - DOCTOR!”
1337.	The receptionist, bound in equal parts confusion and terror, grabbed at the internal comm. “Doctor to the lobby, Doctor to the lobby, Emergency in the main lobby.”
1338.	Mellie looked about the brutal, sparse, yet well-appointed lobby; other patients were waiting, and several attending subordinates in grey-white jackets clenched digital clipboards; the array of silent faces now looked at her.
1339.	Mellie’s eyes fought each shocked and accusing glare back. She pointed at one of the jacketed people. “You, with the clipboard- get a trolly or something. Don’t just stand there!” 
1340.	The man, shocked at being singled out, visibly buckled.
1341.	She realised her gun had fallen from her shoulder, and she had unconsciously held it with her off-hand. It was—she thought—not possible to look threatening holding the barrel of a gun rested on the floor, but just having the weapon was likely upsetting enough to these people.
1342.	Snapping her attention back to the receptionist. “No badges.”
1343.	The receptionist blinked in confusion at the slang.
1344.	“Do not call Cit-Sec,” Mellie said – her tone even but with an undercurrent of steel. It was a command, not a plea.
1345.	As she stood there, gun in hand, the irony wasn't lost on her how she loathed the corporations yet needed their sanctuary now. The thought gnawed at her, but the urgency of Garro's condition pushed it to the background.
1346.	She seldom made threats; these people were just another cog in the corp machine, much like she was, albeit in different ways. But this was different. This was about survival. The receptionist's compliance was non-negotiable. The cold clarity in Mellie's eyes said as much.
1347.	The receptionist’s nod was quick, her eyes wide with understanding.
Chapter Twenty-one:	This Reality.
1348.	Mellie had sat quietly. In her mind, the surroundings too closely mimicked the building they had infiltrated that had begun their journey to where they now were, but the smell of chemical cleaners and the lighting here made it feel worse.
1349.	“You know they’ll call the badges up, right?” Gerhardt had once again adopted a tone of calculated indifference. It had been twenty minutes since Garro had been wheeled into surgery. This was the first time he’d spoken to her since their arrival.
1350.	Mellie gave a noncommittal nod, her attention momentarily on the empty plastic cup she tossed aside, the last drops of spilt water tracing a path down the wall.
1351.	They had been politely ushered into a corridor deeper within the clinic. A manager of some kind in a plastic suit with a plastic smile and plastic mannerisms had led them here. It was, in his words: “too distressing for the other clients for them to wait in the lobby”. Mellie gave the butt of her machine gun an idle tap with her foot.
1352.	Gerhardt paced along the harsh white corridor, tracing his synthetic fingers along the veneer panels of the wall. “You think that message… On the penthouse job– It said protection would be given—” He stopped himself from asking the full question, letting silence fall into the space of Mellie’s absent answer.
1353.	“Those ‘borgers. That wasn’t chance, right?” She relented to the silence and paced footsteps that Gerhardt crammed into the space about them.
1354.	She looked up, “They were at that comms tower, the one Lynx had sent us to look for. That’s not just random chance—”
1355.	Gerhardt pulled at the unshaven skin of his jaw, “No. Not chance.”
1356.	Mellie’s eyes followed him as he continued his short tour. “That wasn’t Lynx though, right? No way he’d stiff us?”
1357.	They both played with the idea for a short while. Gerhardt stopped pacing, “Did you notice anything about them? Anything at all? I didn’t get a look.”
1358.	Mellie swallowed, her eyes flicking up as she tried to recall the fight. “The one by the tower that you downed, he must have been a decker. The guy I carved up, he had a denim jacket, they were all ‘borged up, that one Garro fucked up– his whole head was chromed.”
1359.	Gerhardt stepped such that he now loomed over where Mellie sat. “No, I mean, like gang tags or anything?”
1360.	Mellie had tilted her head to keep Gerhardt’s face engaged. “One dude, that last guy you shot, he had some tats.”
1361.	“Okay,” He turned away to begin pacing again, “What tats? Any words… symbols?”
1362.	Mellie’s face crunched into frustration, “I dunno man fuck, I wasn’t taking notes.”
1363.	“Think!”
1364.	“There was a diamond shape, like playing cards,” Mellie yelled back, her voice too loud for the tight corridor.
1365.	Gerhardt’s pacing stopped; he was frozen. His mind trapped by the words: Diamond shape. He repeated back coldly, “Like playing cards.”
1366.	Mellie stood up to meet him physically and extended her arms outward in an exaggerated shrug. Her voice was clipped and dragged with annoyance; “Yes, a diamond shape, like…”
1367.	Gerhardt interrupted her- “You’re sure?!”
1368.	“I don’t fucking know. I’ll take some pictures next time you blow a guy's fucking head off.” Her irritation at the sudden unwelcome interrogation repelled away from Gerhardt. He was staring - both his human eye and this dark lens of his cybernetics drilled at Mellie.
1369.	“Was it like this…” He turned to the wall; his augmented arm shifted, releasing an array of tiny armatures; they worked in quick tandem to etch into the wall a diamond shape, its top and bottom capped with a horizontal line.
1370.	Mellie watched the drawing come to fruition. Her memory jolted; it was a perfect pictorial recreation of what she’d seen. “Yeh… yes, exactly like that.”
1371.	Gerhardt slumped to the nearest seat, his coat bunching up behind him, “Shit.”
1372.	Mellie scanned his face, seeking more. She didn’t have experience enough to read Gerhardt properly, and he kept himself distant and cold—but she had become used to his quirks, and sorrowful mire was not one she was familiar with.
1373.	He could feel Mellies’s inquiry and looked up, his voice hollow. “Bu-zoh kuh”
1374.	Mellie recognised the word, but its meaning flapped around uselessly at the back of her mind.
1375.	Gerhardt continued, his voice now utterly without inflexion or tone- “The clan, The family, whatever. I think they were waiting for me. Tracking me.”
1376.	Mellie stood blinking at the unconnected story. An expression of ignorant confusion pulled her face into a frown. She mouthed the word silently. “berserker”.
1377.	Gerhardt tapped a finger on the wall. “I knew they were coming, but I’d forgotten. With everything else that’s been happening. I’d forgotten.”
1378.	“Berserker?” Mellie repeated.
1379.	For once, Gerhardt recognised the subtlety of vocal inflexion and snapped at her, “No, not berserker, but they are called that on the news feeds… Bu- Zoh- Kuh”, he carefully pronounced each part. “They’re the crime family out of Little China… fancy themselves as Yakuza or Triad. I owe them.” He raised his arm. It wasn’t the only thing he’d used the substantial loan for, but it was the most visible reminder. “A lot.”
1380.	Mellie had a question bulging at her lips when the door swung open.
1381.	“Your companion is out of surgery. We’ve taken the liberty of charging against your cred-stick for a Trauma Plus Gold service. This includes client privacy, as you had insisted.” The young man's smart-cut jacket, digital clipboard, hair, and smile were cut-outs from magazine adverts.
1382.	Mellie looked blankly at the smiling marketing collage as he held out the digital pad.
1383.	Her train of thought was lost, and the previous question vanished, now mutated– “Privacy?” the new question popped into existence.
1384.	The man’s cheeks pulled in a practised toothy grin, “You had instructed that city security forces were not to be alerted of your companion’s presence here, and we take client privacy very seriously.”
1385.	Mellie shared a sideways glance with Gerhardt and took the pad. The pad displayed a digital set of paperwork with some medical details written out and a section awaiting a signature.
1386.	She signed a scrawl of writing with her finger.
1387.	The man pulled from his jacket pocket a plastic bag, inside—her cred-stick and the corner of her torn coat pocket. He exchanged the bag for the return of his datapad. Standing aside and holding the door, he smiled again and, with a courteous head bow, indicated they should exit.
 
1388.	Mellie paused at the threshold, her gaze settling on the tall woman behind the desk engrossed in her digital pad. “Ah, you’re the one from reception,” the woman said, gesturing to a seat. Her metallic arm caught the light, contrasting sharply with the room's austere decor.
1389.	The small office was a study in contrasts, dominated by rows of shelving filled with books, journals, and an occasional trophy. Any available wall space was devoid of decoration. Amidst this sea of uniformity, the sleek, curved wood desk stood out like an ocean wave rising from the floor.
1390.	Mellie hesitated momentarily before speaking. In the silence, the doctor motioned with a request, “If you’d just leave your weapon by the door, please.” Mellie obliged, leaning her machinegun against the wall. “Tell me, your friend, does he have a name?” The doctor's inquiry focused on the pad rather than Gerhardt's entrance.
1391.	“Ga—” Mellie sank into a chair, her movements guarded. “Your staff promised privacy,” she reminded the doctor.
1392.	The doctor responded with a comforting smile, her gaze shifting to include Gerhardt, who stood sentinel by the door. “Certainly, if privacy is a concern, we shall simply maintain a patient number in association with his genetic marker.”
1393.	Mellie frowned; even without giving a name, she presumed they already knew too much. Feeling a sense of vulnerability, she pressed further, frustration evident in her voice. “Who are you, anyway? Nobody here has yet given me a name.” Mellie felt futile—surrounded by the grey-white of the office, swallowed in a cleansed void of sterile apathy.
1394.	"My apologies," the doctor said, offering her name, "I’m Doctor Bennett. I was the lead surgeon on your friend’s recovery. I simply had some final matters to review before you can all go about your business.”
1395.
