Mellie noted the doctor’s sleek and well-crafted metallic fingers—implants—as they clicked on the surface of the digital pad as she scrolled through details, “We’ve had to apply a considerable amount of sedative and neuro-stasis-analgesics. I’m afraid that whatever your friend’s endo-frame and skeletal enhancements have done creates untenable neural feedback.”
1396.	Doctor Bennett paused, placing the pad on the table, and took a deep breath. “I’ll be very clear. We do uphold total privacy of patient affairs, but the grafts in your friend have been flagged up as a potential cause for concern. Protocol in such cases involving Asukaga hardware in use by non-military personnel.”
1397.	Gerhardt’s stance hardened imperceptibly—Mellie felt the prickle of air and raised a hand to quiet him, her senses attuned to his subtle movements.
1398.	Mellie’s eyes turned green steel, with a long blink and words that each were a drop of lava landing on ice; "Doc, this entire situation needs the right words out of your mouth next. Is MAF or Cit-Sec here or on their way?”
1399.	The shift in the emotion of the room made the tight bun atop the doctor’s head weave as she glanced between Mellie, Gerhardt, and the imposing machine gun against the wall. 
1400.	“No, there are no security forces present or on their way. But the information has, nonetheless, been passed forward. And, we will undoubtedly be audited on the matter in the future.” Doctor Bennett admitted.
1401.	Gerhardt sprang, his movement fluid, precise, silent, and complete. He was at once- a statue beside the door—and—he was leaning on the desk, his pistol extended so that it almost touched the doctor’s cheek, his long coat falling to rest behind him. Mellie jolted at the speed; the doctor whimpered a shriek.
1402.	When he spoke, his voice was unwavering; his arm and gun perfectly still. “You have security footage here? How can we make it go away? And the audit—how do we make it routine?” He emphasized 'routine' in a way that left no doubt about the threat behind his words.
1403.	The doctor nodded frantically, her eyes wide with the moment's immediacy. “I … I can’t do anything about any recordings…” she stammered. The cold gun barrel pushed into her cheek.
1404.	“Tell me who can.” It was a quiet, almost whisper, but Gerhardt’s words brought the room to a standstill.
1405.	Mellie, blustering to catch up to the moment in her mind, tumbled the seat backwards as she jumped to her gun. “Gerki, what the hell? We just need to get out. If Garro can leave—”
1406.	“Tell me, Mel, you trust this bitch? Or are we going to walk out into a tac-squad?” He didn’t let his focus shift at all.
1407.	Slowly raising one metallic finger, the doctor continued, “Th… the left of the corridor, downstairs…  Our- Our maintenance will have any recordings.” Her temples glistened with sweat under the soft, off-yellow lights.
1408.	Mellie stepped into the doctor’s view. “Where is…” she paused for just a moment to phrase her words, “Our friend, can he walk?”
1409.	“He’d need Dexathyrozone to be able to wake. We had to...”
1410.	Mellie cut the doctor off, “Get up. You’re going to help us get our friend up and about and show us this maintenance office.” Mellie snapped a glare at Gerhardt. He thrust his pistol into its holster and pulled his coat about him.
1411.	It was a tense but brief walk as Mellie, with the doctor’s arm held tightly, let her lead back to the surgical recovery room.
1412.	Garro was covered with a medical cloth; the remnants of bloodied rags still sat in a nearby disposal tray. He breathed heavily but in regular passive breaths, heaving his chest.
1413.	“Dexa-fucking-whatever… Get it.” Mellie threw the doctor forward, watching as she hurried about from a supply cabinet to Garro’s cannula. Gerhardt stood beside the doorway, holding it ajar; his focus on watching the corridor was a practised reaction to their situation.
1414.	Garro choked almost immediately, his body swiftly succumbing to the sweeping effects of the drugs. The doctor looked up at Mellie. “He’ll not have anything to keep back the system shock. The analgesics we have here are not…” Once again, Mellie cut the doctor's speech short.
1415.	“Let me worry about that. The recordings– Show us. Gerki, help him up.” Mellie thrust her chin at Garro while walking to the door and awaiting the doctor.
1416.	Gerhardt pulled at Garro’s arm with all he could muster, but Garro was still all but unconscious, drowsily burbling. “Come on man, wake up. Need you with us.” Garro roused. His other arm slowly pulled up, and a limp hand grabbed the side of his head.
1417.	“My head…”
1418.	“Get up, ya big fucker”, Gerhardt pulled again, this time enough to get Garro seated.
1419.	The medical cloth fell away, revealing Garro had been stripped naked. His neck and shoulder wore a medical gauze- now his only clothing; his body bore bruises and the remnants of scarring that were the terrible remnants of the wounds he'd suffered at his abdomen; his now fixed calf was angry pink and red. Even now, amidst this stark vulnerability, even in his damaged and muddled state, he retained an aura of latent menace.
1420.	Mellie shoved the doctor through the door, pulling the strapping of her gun tight such that it was flush against her body under her long coat. “Are we going?” She called back into the room.
1421.	Still weakened and unsteady, Garro managed to get to his feet, though one massive arm rested heavily on Gerhardt's shoulders. He responded with a steely glare and a low, guttural grunt to confirm their readiness.
 
1422.	The emergency exit swung open without a sound, and to their relief, no alarms blared. The maintenance officer's assurances had held. Mellie shot a glance at Doctor Bennett, who now sat bound and gagged in the corridor they had just vacated. Sorry Doc, she thought, wishing there was time for explanations.
1423.	The encounter at the clinic had left a bitter taste, and the news of Garro's flagged implants only intensified Mellie's disdain for corporate reach. She couldn't help but wonder – why can’t they just keep their nose out of people’s business? as she watched Garro slump against the unforgiving grey bricks of the towering building, his bare skin brushing against the rough surface.
1424.	Gerhardt’s concentration was focused inward. His human eye flicked to keep up as he consulted what few maps he had access to through his cyber-eye, visualizing potential routes. He exhaled slowly through flaring nostrils. “We can head south on Express Three," he suggested, his voice low and contemplative. "We should hit Tellman Plaza, grab a cab there, be back in the Skrim in maybe a half hour.”
1425.	Mellie, however, had a different destination in mind. She had turned to reassure herself in watching the emergency exit door swing closed, her expression resolute. "No," she countered, "We've got to head to Chanix, north of Chanix... There's a Doc there Garro's going to need—” This time, it was Mellie's turn to be interrupted.
1426.	Gerhardt raised an eyebrow, his gaze flicking to the quiet street beyond them. “Get there how? You got some wheels? It's at least fifteen minutes on foot to Tellman, twenty with him in that state.” He gestured with a sideways nod toward Garro, who still rested against the building as though each supported the other.
1427.	Frustration etched Mellie's features, and she scrunched her face in annoyance. She channelled her anger into her eyes, fine pricks of determination. "And if we don't get him some meds," she declared, "this will all play out again. I'm pretty sure we can't come here again." She cast a meaningful glance at the emergency exit door behind her and waved an arm in the direction of the imposing building.
Gerhardt heaved a weary sigh, his earlier resistance giving way to practicality. "Fine, Chanix, it is," he conceded. "But it's right on Central, and the longer we're out here, the more I feel like some black wagon is going to fall from the fucking sky, and we're all getting slotted," he admitted, voicing a rare anxiety.
 
1428.	As they navigated the shadowy backstreets straddling Little China and Central, Gerhardt couldn't shake the inescapable tension tightened around him like a noose. Each shadow seemed to leer; each sudden noise clawed at his nerves.
1429.	He felt like a marked man, a walking target in a game of deadly hide-and-seek. The Buzokuh—unlike Cit-Sec, who probably had units combing the streets for him by now—paid no heed to the unwritten rules that divided the city's districts.
1430.	For them, the porous boundary between the Skrim's anarchy and the veneer of civilisation was just another line to cross, another rule to flout.
1431.	He knew this enemy, knew their modus operandi was as unpredictable as it was ruthless. And, as he moved deeper into the maze of alleys, the walls closing in like an ever-tightening trap, Gerhardt realized the sanctuary he had once found in the Skrim's chaos was far behind him. This was their ground.
1432.	Garro's complaints echoed through the cab; his voice filled with a growl that could curdle milk. His head, he claimed, felt like it was being split open by a sledgehammer from the inside. Clad only in his birthday suit, his nudity had sparked a comical, if heated, negotiation with the cab driver, which ended in a few additional ‘donations’ for his troubles.
1433.	Mellie felt her pulse slow as they arrived at the familiar, dilapidated door of the Ripperdoc that had saved Garro’s life before—a medical wizard, working out of a ragged, dimly lit apartment hidden in the heart of the city's underbelly.
1434.	She felt sure he represented their best hope of obtaining the critical medication Garro desperately needed, and her instincts proved correct.
1435.	Garro had been slumped face down over an inadequate table; an unhealthy application of sedatives had been dosed into him. With deft elegance, a tiny vial had been extracted from within his metallic spine.
1436.	“Oh, I know this compound,” the ripper quietly reported, shaking his head. “Can’t get this.”
1437.	Mellie stood with her arms crossed, her face refusing the doctor’s response.
1438.	“Well, okay, I can get it– but it’s not gonna be cheap.”
1439.	Mellie's eyes narrowed, locking onto the ripperdoc's. "How ‘not cheap’ are we talking?"
1440.	The ripper sighed, swiping through screens on a translucent tablet. "With the rarity and the risk involved in procuring it, it’s going to run you at least a few thousand credits."
1441.	Mellie looked over at Gerhardt, who was silently observing the conversation. His eyes met hers, a silent understanding passing between them. He nodded almost imperceptibly.
1442.	"Fine," she said, turning back to the ripper doc. "Do it."
1443.	The room went tense as financial transactions were attempted. Mellie's cred-stick flashed red on the payment terminal—Declined. Her stomach dropped. Gerhardt stepped in, swiping his own stick with a reluctant sigh. Approved.
1444.	As the ripper disappeared into the back room to set the dealings to procure the critical medication in motion, Mellie and Gerhardt exchanged a glance. Her eyes wore gratitude and concern; his was filled with silent questions he wasn't asking—yet.
1445.	The tension in the room stretched thin as Mellie and Gerhardt waited, each second dragging on like a slow, agonizing eternity. Finally, the clinic's door burst open, admitting a young woman with a shaven head and an array of ear piercings. Perhaps only a little younger than Lynx, she was adorned in a patchwork denim jacket, a glaring contrast to the heavy carryall slung over her shoulder—the pack and its insignia more commonly associated with trauma teams.
1446.	Casting a quick, curious glance at the pair seated in the waiting area—her eyes widened in surprise as they landed on Garro, who lay unconscious and indignantly nude. She delivered a bag to the Ripperdoc with a flair of nonchalance, carelessly indifferent to the seriousness of their situation.
1447.	"All yours, Doc," she announced, her voice tinged with professionalism and irreverence. "Jim says hi. Oh, and he also mentioned that the next bag is gonna’ cost ya double." 
1448.	“Thanks, Shay”, replied the Ripperdoc, tearing open the bag and diving to search among the content.
1449.	The young woman turned to leave, pausing as she approached the door, “This your chum?” she addressed Mellie and Gerhardt and twisted her head to indicate Garro.
1450.	Their interaction was one of pensive glances and a nod from Gerhardt before the young lady, her eyes lingering a little too long on Mellie, continued, “Lucky guy.” With a twitched youthful smile and a sideways glance at the Ripeprdoc, she sidestepped toward the door– “Beep me yeah, Doc”, the young lady said as she bounded again through the doorway. The Doc replied too late with a distracted “Mmm-hum.”
1451.	The ripper doc wasted no time, getting straight to work with the contents of the carryall. It didn't take long before Garro emerged from the makeshift clinic, still a bit unsteady on his feet but noticeably revitalized; he had donned a pair of jeans and a shirt that looked even more threadbare than his usual attire, but none of that mattered to Mellie at the moment.
1452.	What caught her attention was the faint, almost imperceptible, silent hiss that could be heard from behind Garro. It was the subtle sound of spinal injectors once more fulfilling their endless task, offering him much-needed relief from his pain. As she listened to that short sound, she felt a surge of hope wash over her.
1453.	"Looks like someone got his groove back," Gerhardt remarked, his eyes meeting Mellie's as he sensed the emotional atmosphere shift.
1454.	Mellie couldn't help but smile as she looked at Garro, who returned her gaze with a slight, tentative grin. In that instant, despite the looming threats and uncertainties ahead, Mellie felt a sense of accomplishment.
1455.	They had managed to snatch a small victory in the face of unknown adversity—a victory, perhaps something better. For now, that was enough.
Chapter Twenty-two:	In Plain Sight.
1456.	When Lynx had joined Mellie and Gerhart at Mellie’s apartment, his entrance was not a joyous moment; finding himself ensnared by two hunters seeking the blood of reason, cornered and defensive, having faced a barrage of accusations upon entering, Lynx held both hands up in front of himself, his head shaking from side to side. “I don’t know what you’re on about! What guys?!”
1457.	Gerhardt stood threateningly close; he was taller by a meaningful amount that he now looked down at Lynx.
1458.	Mellie, too, was shaking her head, “You said there was a comms tower. Those guys were waiting for us, and then when it all went to shit, you had gone radio silent.”
1459.	“I told you, I had to ditch my deck while I spoke with Davie – He is fucking pissed, by the way.” Lynx fought back with the only ammunition he had in the heightened conversation. Gerki still loomed over him.
1460.	“I know. He left me a message.” Mellie’s frank and unimpressed reaction made Lynx stumble over his words.
1461.	“He left a message? When? I came right here when I saw you’d called. How’s he left you a message before I’d spoken with him?” Lynx could not hide the disbelief in his cracking voice.
1462.	A loud screech of tyres from outside cut their conversation. Mellie’s apartment had become an unkempt jumble of days of food packets and drink containers. She had glared accusingly at the door, but the distant vibrations of an engine pulling away allowed her to relax and stare at the mess. Her lip twitched slightly.
1463.	Gerhardt, too, had swung his attention to the closed apartment door at the sound; Lynx took the moment to slide sideways from under his awareness.
1464.	The pair returned their attentions to him as he slumped, sliding to sit against the intervening counter of the kitchenette. “I swear, it had nothing to do with me. Fuck’n Davie is out for your blood– mighta been him, but I don’t think he knows what the hell is going on, or where you even were.”
1465.	Gerhardt visibly reclined from his posture, walking about into the kitchen area and drawing a mug of water. “Can we all just stop for a minute? We need to focus. Whatever sent us sideways out there, I’m pretty sure it’s Buzokuh.”
1466.	Lynx scratched idly at an ear, “Who? Wait… You mean those nuggets out of el-see?” Lynx’s street slang abbreviation was understood well enough, L.C. being the common yet more derogatory phrasing for the district of Little China. Gerhardt, swallowing a mouthful of water, nodded—his eyes not leaving Lynx.
1467.	“So what?” Mellie chimed.
1468.	Lynx had begun fiddling with his bracer; it was a poor substitute for his deck but, in a pinch, allowed him to access data on the go.
1469.	Gerhardt leant with both hands on the countertop, “So, if you don’t already know, ‘zokuh aren’t the type to accept a note saying– I’m busy. Can we sort this out later?” He shifted, turning away, speaking to the opposite wall where Mellie’s overused food heater was situated. “It’s very likely that I will have to leave Akureyri. And given how things have been going here, I don’t like your chances without me.”
1470.	Mellie snorted, “We were doing fine before Davie put you on us.” She crossed her arms but, in doing so, felt the sad couch creak uneasily beneath her as she shifted her weight.
1471.	Lynx stood up, physically breaking into their conversation as a wall, “I don’t think it was you they were after,” he said slowly, worry pulling at his words.
1472.	“I just checked. You remember that info from Zecsa’s pad?” He tapped at his bracer, “I think it was runners out of el-see that had tracked her, look.” He extended his arm, offering a view of the intricate digital map that traced on its screen.
1473.	“I talked with her. She said not to track her but—”
1474.	Mellie frowned, releasing her crossed arms, “You talked with who?”
1475.	“Zecsa.”
1476.	“She’s alive?” Mellie prompted, “I thought– at the apartment—” Her words trailed off as she looked at Gerhardt.
1477.	“Place was wrecked. Not my style, but certainly looked like someone had been snuffed.” His report was given with a complete indifference to the morbid concepts.
1478.	Lynx began talking over both of them; “Yeah, well, she told me not to track her. I did anyway, and this map is all I found. Look, there,” he jabbed a finger at a line glowing on the bracers display, “That’s right in the middle of el-see. And given these pings, it makes sense.” As he spoke, he moved his finger among glowing markers on the map, the district boundary of Little Dhina caught in his invisible finger drawing.
1479.	Gerhardt walked around the counter to stand adjacent to Lynx and look at the indicated detail. “So- Buzokuh, they’re after her?”
1480.	“Must have been them. They got some of the meanest Netrunners in town, outside of a Corp, probably the only ones capable of tracing her. If they’re all that you say they are, makes sense they’d jump her apartment.” Lynx looked up, a glow of pride at his placing a puzzle piece to fit the picture. “But if she’s been messaging me, who got bagged?”
1481.	“Not our problem.” Mellie had gotten up and joined them, leaning on the small countertop. “We should figure out what these berserkers know.”
1482.	Lynx lost his prideful glow, his face pulled sullen, “Oh Fuck, I pulled out the logs from Zecsa’s mainframe.” He stopped.
1483.	Mellie and Gerhardt looked at him, sensing the change of the moment about them. Gerhardt's practicality overcoming any sense of trepidation asked rhetorically, “So?”
1484.	“So, the data I pulled. Zecsa and Davie had an opened comm log. If they had tracked Zecsa—”
1485.	Mellie continued Lynxes dropped words, “And if they tracked Zecsa to Davie, they could track Davie to us.”
1486.	Lynx sagged against the counter, the implications dawning on him.
1487.	Mellie paced in the brief space, “Can you comm Davie?”
1488.	Lynx hesitated, his face a canvas of anxiety. “I... I don’t know. Reaching out to Davie now could make things worse.”
1489.	Gerhardt, who had been quietly observing, interjected firmly, “We don't have the luxury of playing it safe. Davie might be our only link to understanding what's going on.”
1490.	Mellie nodded in agreement, her eyes fixed on Lynx. “You said it yourself, Lynx. If there is anyone hunting us, if anyone had taken out Zecsa, they could trace to Davie. Whatever is going on lands back to him, that penthouse and that data-pack. And he’s got to have more info.”
1491.	Lynx, caught in a bind, exhaled deeply and nodded. “Alright, I'll try. But if things go south, this could all backfire.” In his mind, he initiated a new connection to his bracer and the telecommunications network of Mellie's apartment, an outbound comm to the one man he’d rather not talk with.
1492.	Lynx's face was a mask of anxiety, tension etched into every line. The conversation unfolding through his implanted agent was one-sided but damning. Mellie could only hear Lynx's side of the dialogue, yet his expressions echoed the unseen voice on the other end.
1493.	Lynx's voice broke the silence, a mix of frustration and desperation. "That's not what I said, Davie. We had nothing to do with this mess. It's Zecsa, she's the problem, man."
1494.	Mellie tried to piece together the unspoken half of the conversation. Davie was angry—that much was clear. And now Lynx seemed to be defending them against a new accusation. Did Davie think they'd intentionally provoked the Buzokuh?
1495.	Was that such a mad thought? Mellie reflected on the notion that they would, as a group, seek to assault Davie and take him out of the picture. He was one of many fixers in the area; it just so happened he was perhaps one of the best-known.
1496.	That was why she had been excited when he called offering work. A man of his reputation was indeed her ticket to living life on her terms. Her recollection of the past months clashed dissonant against what she had thought her life as a merc would bring.
1497.	It had now proven that the lure of some easy cash had placed her life as well as Garro’s in peril, and now, too, her newfound friends, Gerhardt and Lynx. There was no recognisable escape from this trap.
1498.	None of it made sense to her. Not long ago, she was broke—worried about rent and food. In the past weeks, she’d earned and spent more dollies than she ever needed. But now, her cred-stick was dead, rent would still be due as it always was, and that concern from a life she no longer recognised was demolished by the presence of the threat of an underground crime family now hunting for her. And all because of some crappy smash-and-grab job.
1499.	As the silence stretched, it became a living entity, feeding on the scraps of their dwindling options. Mellie felt the weight of it, a pressure that demanded acknowledgement.
1500.	"Well?" Her voice, when it finally broke the quiet, was a lance of impatience. She needed to know where they stood, needed to know if the ground beneath them would open and swallow them.
1501.	Lynx held up a finger; his jaw was clenched, and now he looked like he might puke. It was as if he was holding his breath, fighting to keep his composure while Davie’s unheard voice poisoned his brain.
1502.	He had to verbally do battle a few more times before the call ended, and finally, he exhaled deeply.
1503.	“He hasn’t got a damn clue,” Lynx emotionally crumpled inwardly, “He also says that if Zecsa has been messaging me that they’ll come for us, not him.”
1504.	Mellie shook her head slowly, wincing and gripping the bridge of her nose in a finger and thumb, “I can believe that. He probably had it all noted to happen like this, scapegoats to his games.”
1505.	“Do ya think they will come for us?” Lynx's question hung in the air, a fragile hope that they might escape the notice of powers far greater than themselves.
1506.	Mellie rolled her neck back to gaze upward, as if seeking guidance from the faded water stains on the ceiling, but her decision was born from within.
1507.	“No. Well, even if they want to, we can always do something.” The words were spoken with a certainty that defied their reality, a defiance that was both armour and weapon.
1508.	“We need to find Garro, and we need to get back to Davie. Whatever this crap is, just got too deep for me.”
 
1509.	Rain whispered against the city, its steady rhythm a constant backdrop to the endless nights of Akureyri. The streets glistened, reflecting the neon chaos of the city above. Here, down in the underbelly where the light struggled to penetrate, the rain seemed to wash away the day's grime, only for it to settle once more with the coming dawn. Alleys, like the one Garro called home, became rivulets of the unwanted mess of the city; he’d accepted being a part of it.
1510.	In this alley, shielded from the world by a fortress of refuse, Garro had carved out a space for himself. A sizeable rusting dumpster blocked most of the alleyway, and under a tarpaulin awning, held in place by a mangle of wire trolley carts, were what passed for sleeping arrangements; a  place where his thoughts could wander unfettered. Here, he was just another part of the urban ecosystem, as much a fixture as the overflowing bins and the graffiti tagging the bricks.
1511.	After being dismissed from the armed services of Asukaga, the sharp decline in his mental capability had rendered him unable to traverse the tangled bureaucracy of claiming service payments. Unable to maintain steady employment, he lived on the streets. But he had adapted to this way of life.
1512.	Tonight, the alley bore the sheen of wetness, the rain a meticulous artist painting every surface with shimmering strokes. On nights like these, Garro found solace in the simple company of a rat, sharing his scant meal with it as equals.
1513.	In the rat's scurrying existence, Garro saw a reflection of his own life—each of them a scavenger, forever teetering on the edge of oblivion, vulnerable to forces beyond their control. For the rat, the danger might be an uncaring boot; for Garro, it could be a highly technologically embedded skeletal system.
1514.	The events of recent days had left him unsettled, a mix of desire for clarity and rising anger. But here, amid the garbage and rats, no one expected anything from him. And that, at least, was comforting.
1515.	He flicked the power switch, immersing himself in the tinny beeps and pixelated graphics. Lost in the respite of his small electronic game, its screen now a web of cracks, he found a fleeting moment where even the persistent rain couldn't touch him. His rodent companion sniffed at his pocket.
1516.	"Sorry, little buddy, it's my last bit. I need that." He waved a hand to shoo the rat from his pocket.
1517.	He had felt bad when Mellie told him about losing all her money to pay for him to be fixed. He lacked the words to express his remorse, and no way came to mind for making amends. Retreating to his squalid alleyway felt like the only penance he could offer to a friend he had failed.
1518.	The throaty purr of a finely tuned bike engine broke through the static noise of falling rain, halting at the entrance of the alley. Amidst the dim, rain-scattered light, a shadowy figure dismounted and began walking toward him. As darkness crept in, alleys like this became a playground for the city's unsavoury characters—peddling their illicit goods.
1519.	The enigmatic figure drew closer, still an indistinct silhouette through the rain-soaked gloom. Garro looked up from his game, squinting to discern any details in the gathering darkness.
1520.	The figure’s dark clothing only further hid them, a walking shadow within the obfuscated luminance from the street beyond. They stop, the rain cascading about them.
1521.	“Hey Gee, Mel said I’d find you here…” The recognisable sound of Gerhardt’s voice let Garro’s tensing muscles relax.
1522.	Garro, pulling himself up, pushing aside the tarpaulin and—creating a gush of water from its makeshift roof, let the rainwater soak into his clothes. “Jerry, I’m sorry.”
1523.	“Come on, there’s a kebob place nearby. Let’s get you some food. It’s all gone sideways, and we’re going to need you.” Gerhardt said, extending a hand as if to pull Garro back into the world he had momentarily abandoned.
1524.	Seeing the invitation, Garro stands motionless as the rain washes his face, trying to understand what is being asked. He then turns away.
1525.	Plucking the still-open kibble pack from his pocket and throwing it under the tarp. The heavy rain bounces from his sodden back. He smiles.
 
1526.	As the next day dawned, The four of them sat huddled under the shade of a weather-beaten billboard that overlooked a bustling intersection. The glare of the now diminished sun, its ferocity tempered by the approach of autumn, pierced through gaps in their makeshift cover, was a harsh intrusion in the city's otherwise claustrophobic atmosphere.
1527.	Below its aged facade, the city moved relentlessly, a blur of activity where each individual was absorbed in their own narrative, oblivious to the drama unfolding under the advertisement's watchful gaze. The group’s presence was just another part of the urban tableau, unnoticed and unimportant.
1528.	Garro’s gaze, however, cut through the crowd of people, landing on a figure whose presence seemed at odds with the surroundings: A well-dressed pedestrian engrossed in a conversation on their agent. Amidst the colourful tapestry of characters usually found this far south of Central, the suited individual stood out like a sore thumb. His brain ticked over ponderously; He must be important.
1529.	Lynx had spent the morning recounting his latest failed attempts to trace Zecsa. His report was a laundry list of dead ends, warnings to stop his search, and ghost pings in remote systems. Life was already dangerous, he thought, consciously dismissing the warnings, visibly frustrated by the elusive nature of his quarry.
1530.	They had all listened intently, except for Garro—lost in his own world. When Lynx finished recounting his efforts—a tale defined more by its lack of revelations than any findings, Gerhardt stood up. He distanced himself from the cold, unforgiving concrete wall that had been their uncomfortable seat and stared at the passing crowds.
1531.	Mellie's focus drifted, her gaze becoming distant as she mulled over their predicament. Her fingers traced the edge of her sleeve, a small but telling sign of the gears turning in her mind. This brief lapse into thoughtfulness softened her usually sharp demeanour, offering a glimpse of her reflective side.
1532.	She broke the momentary silence, snapping back her attention, her eyes sharp and decisive. “We only found Zecsa because of the stuff you got from Davie.” she began, her voice now laced with resolve as she recaptured her team's focus.
1533.	“And, he must realise we did more than bust the club up a little.” Her words cut through the quiet, revealing a thread of thought leading into the shadow of their uncertainty. “He’s not an idiot, but I think maybe he’s as scared as we are. Maybe we can meet him half way.” There were no complaints against the verbal train of thought 
1534.	“We need to talk with him again.” Mellie focused on Lynx as he sat quietly, nervously biting his lip, “Lynx, you think Davie will be willing to talk, a real talk—face to face? Can you at least get him to tell us where we could meet him?”
1535.	He scrubbed a fist through his tangle of hair, trying to brush the questions aside, “Well, he didn’t sound so mad last night– sure, I’ll ask,” he confirmed regretfully.
 
1536.	The cab halted at the base of a residential monolith, its shadow a stark reminder of the city's relentless vertical ambition. Here, on the western fringes of Central, glamour was a commodity as scarce as daylight beneath the expressways' oppressive bulk. Like veins, they pumped a ceaseless flow of traffic through the city's heart, the noise a constant drone—a soundtrack to lives lived in the fast lane.
1537.	The brickwork was stained dark from emissions and aerosolised tarmac, and the front door was flanked by two tall men in suits whose stature and poise spoke of private security, their eyes scanning the newcomers with practised indifference.
1538.	Stepping into the lobby, the group was enveloped by an incongruous hush. The traffic's roar gave way to the soft echoes of a space trying too hard to impress, its mirrored tiles and imitation marble reflecting a city that could never be still. More men in suits awaited them.
1539.	One of the suits approached with measured steps, studying them each before halting before Gerhardt.
1540.	“Gerhardt.”
1541.	“Samuel.”
1542.	The frigid, curt pleasantries prickled in the air.
1543.	“I don’t know what you’ve done, but he is not ‘appy.” He flicked a tiny raised-chin nod, “You know what’s going on.” His body language conveyed an understanding between the two that was lost in translation as Mellie, Garro, and Lynx watched.
1544.	Gerhardt opened his coat with a ritualistic slowness, allowing Samuel to disarm him.
1545.	In the ceremony, Gerhart panned his gaze about the room, studying the faces, noticing a man further back who looked strikingly like himself—similar implanted eye, same cold stare. He wondered: Are we so interchangeable? Do we really all look like that?
1546.	At a signal from Samuel, a duffle was kicked across the floor. He dropped in Gerhardt’s weapon and began to pat him down, pulling a knife from a sheath and tossing it to the bag.
1547.	With his search completed, he turned to Mellie. “You got rigs on you?” She flicked her eyes at Gerhardt, who offered a slow blink as his response.
1548.	At her hesitation, Samuel clarified, “Guns, are you carrying any guns?” with more than a little impatience.
1549.	Melle went to pull out her machine-pistol, but her hand was stopped as Samuel grabbed her wrist. “No, no. Let’s all be calm here.” He gestured to her coat at which she was reaching, “Here?” 
1550.	She nodded, her nose creased by the vexing indignation of being treated like a child.
1551.	Samuel carefully and slowly peeled her coat aside to see the pistol’s handle poking from its inside pocket. He carefully extracts the weapon with a forefinger and thumb and tosses it into the duffle. “That all?”
1552.	Having reached her limit, Mellie snarled a condescending smile at him, “Yes, and if you put your hands on me—”
1553.	A waving gloved finger pushed her words aside. Samuel had pulled open his jacket with his other hand, his eyes leading to the handle of his own gun. “We’re all being civil here, all friends.” He replied.
1554.	Samuel turned away from Mellie to address Garro, “You’re gonna stay with us while everyone has a nice chat.” An arm extended to point at a ring of comfortable-looking seats at the side of the lobby.
1555.	Garro caught Mellie's eye. Her expression was locked-down anger and vulnerability. His protective instincts flared. "I don't like this," he said, his voice low but firm. "You can't just go around manhandling my friends." His musculature was already rising with adrenaline.
1556.	Samuel's eyes met Garro's, steely but not unkind. "Everyone is here just to have a talk," he stressed, his hand subtly resting on the grip of his own holstered weapon. "But you'll do as I say."
1557.	Garro felt the tension of his rising body, his instincts screaming at him to act, to protect. But another look at Gerhardt told him all he needed to know. They were in a precarious position, and aggression would not serve them well here.
1558.	"Fine," Garro finally conceded, exhaling a reluctant breath. "But you should know, civility is a two-way street."
1559.	He moved over to the indicated chairs, his eyes never leaving Samuel until he sat down. As he settled into the plush seat, he felt anything but relaxed. His gaze shifted between his disarmed friends and the men who had disarmed them. He was a guardian relegated to the sidelines, and he didn't like it one bit. But for now, he understood that compliance was the only card they had to play.
1560.	Watching and waiting for the tension to subside, Samuel returned his attention to the line-up, “And you must be the boy wonder. Got your toys on ya?”
1561.	Lynx’s pack rarely, if ever, left his shoulder. He felt its snug weight now as he stared into Samuel’s uncaring eyes. He let the pack fall to the floor at his feet. Another suited man approached from the side with a handheld scanner, waved it at Lynx, and tapped it against his bracer.
1562.	“That’s not an implant, lose it,” Samuel instructed.
1563.	Lynx felt naked as the bracer’s buckles clicked open, and he put it resting on his pack. The man with the scanner had also proceeded to scan Gerhardt and Mellie but walked away in silence.
1564.	“Great, see. All happy, all good.” Samuel finally said. “You’ll get on the farthest elevator. Hit the button for the penthouse.” Samuel pointed to the back of the lobby, where it branched to several service corridors and proceeded to step to their side, picking the now-laden duffle and Lynx’s pack with him.
 
1565.	The elevator chimes resonated like a herald's trumpet as the doors parted, revealing a realm where opulence was the air one breathed. The penthouse unfurled before them—a tableau of indulgent luxury and meticulous design. Every surface gleamed, every furnishing bespoke, each artwork strategically placed to impress upon any beholder the weight of wealth. It was a grotesque shrine to excess, where every gilt edge and plush fabric whispered of avarice.
1566.	Davie was the malformed king of this gaudy domain, ensconced behind his throne—a desk of such sleek modernity it seemed an alien artefact among the traditional grandeur. His attention was anchored to a holographic display, fingers dancing across projections only he could perceive. His delay in acknowledging their presence was a calculated show of power, his eventual gaze rising not with curiosity but expectation.
1567.	Davie deactivated the holographic interface and regarded them with a predator's focus. He rose, the movement slow, laden with the gravity of their trespass.
1568.	With a cold affront, he addresses them, “Do come in.” he purred with feigned cordiality. “I've been eagerly awaiting this little reunion.”
1569.	Davie began to pace the length of the room, its plush interior a far cry from the office over the Halcyon Club. It felt too ordinary for him yet too grand. He was a glitch in this space—a spot on fine silk.
1570.	The three accused sat in a row of seats, turned to face away from what might be a real wooden dining table. Nobody had said a word for more than a minute. Davie halted his yo-yoed pacing.
1571.	“My club—my sanctuary—has been violated.” he purred with feigned politeness. “My systems, the walls of my digital fortress, have been breached. And somehow, you four are at the epicentre of it all,” he let the report drop from a thin smile.
1572.	Mellie, attempting to jump ahead of the inevitable, quickly interjected, "We had nothing to do with—"
1573.	Davie cut her off, his scorn sharp as broken glass. “Do you take me for a fool? Got vids, and one of my guys got broken fucking ribs, coincidence?”
1574.	Gerhart now tried to disarm the rising tsunami, “We didn't come here to be chewed out. We're trying to figure this out, too.”
1575.	Leaning into them, veins throbbing like the bass of the club broached lines against his temples, Davie’s voice climbed, “Oh, you'll figure it out, alright. Because if you don't, you're not walking out of that fucking door.”
1576.	As His voice grew, Lynx’s attempt to coerce the conversation was lost—“We never meant for any of this to happen.”
1577.	Davie spun to return his pace and erupted, “Meant? MEANT? You think I built all this,” he gestured around the room, “on good intentions? Do you have any idea what you've done to me? To my reputation?”
1578.	Gerhardt went to stand, but the motion of one of the desks flanking guards stapled him into his seat again, “We're not your enemy, Davie. Getting heated isn't going to solve anything.”
1579.	With rage expelling from his crimson face, Davie turned to face them again. Spittle volleyed following his words, “Heated? I am beyond fucking heated! I am FUCKING MOLTEN! You've embarrassed me. Made me look weak! In my world, perception is power. And you've robbed me of it!”
1580.	They each realised that Davie was far beyond reasoning; Mellie, raising her voice to battle his, rebutted, “Davie, if we can just talk this through—”
1581.	Having reached the end of his desk, Davie turned, slamming his hands into it, his cybernetic arm causing the edge to splinter, his anger manifesting in the wreckage. “Talk? The time of talk is long past. You're going to fix this. Or so help me, you'll wish you'd never set foot in my club.” His voice had changed fire for ice, sharp, cold, deadly.
1582.	Davie pulls his ill-fitted shirt down and fell into his seat; picking up a tiny vial, he snorts the content, inhaling and choking as he does. His eyes blaze, sealing them in the room with him. They each feel the spotlight of an approaching storm shine on them.
1583.	Mellie extends her palms forward, placating the ire, “We can fix this, all of it. But we need the data-pack.”
1584.	Lynx pulled himself up, fighting to re-enter the conversation, “We know that you and Zecsa had something to do with that gig—”
1585.	“Zoe? She’s fuckin’ dead!” Davie cut him off, the edge of hysteria in his voice. “How the fuck should I know what happened to that damn box?”
1586.	Lynx bounced off of the response, “No, no - I’ve been speaking with her and—”
1587.	“Don’t fucking tell me what I do and do not know. There are maybe three runners like her in the entire city. She’s fucking dead.” The fury was gone, but Davie was far from calm.
1588.	“But I—” Lynx swallowed his unspoken words. Davie had refocused on Mellie.
1589.	“So, you’ll fix this?” Davie gesticulated wildly, “Fix my club, fix that people respect me—”
1590.	Mellie stood; both of the flanking bodyguards jolted from their stance but waited, held by an infinitesimal thread of reaction. “Two Million.” She pointedly declared, taking a step forward.
1591.	Davie visibly recoiled. “Two—”
1592.	She repeated, taking another step, “Two million dollies.”
1593.	Davie pulled himself to sit closer to the desk, closer to the number she had said. “On what? You running a gig for some other fixer?”
1594.	“Two million,” she carefully pronounced and elongated the wording. “The gig you sent us on, the black ops job. Corpo money. Just sat waiting to collect– Lynx’ll show you. All we need is the data-pack.”
1595.	Davie chewed at his lower lip; the sudden and outlandish claim intrigued him.
1596.	His human eye flickered as he mentally tallied and calculated. “Two million is a lot of money, even by my standards. How do I know you're not playing me? Cutting’ yourself free to escape with– what is honestly, a fucking insane promise?”
1597.	Mellie had closed to be stood in front of the wide desk, “You don't. But without it, we’re screwed anyway? You want respect, power, security? Money buys all those things, Davie.”
1598.	Gerhardt stood, glancing at each bodyguard, “She’s right, Davie. You and I go back, ya? Look at me and tell me why I’d be stood here if this weren’t the only way forward.”
1599.	Davie rubbed at his temples and read their faces, “Alright, let's say I believe you. You get this data-pack, keep the Corpo’s off my back, make sure the fuckers in el-see aren’t a problem and restore my club – and my reputation. What's in it for you?”
1600.	Mellie raised her face so that she looked over Davie’s head at the back wall. He was beneath her, “Half. One million, and we all walk away from this, no strings attached.”
1601.	Davie leant back in his chair; a much more recognizable version of him now sat in the chair—a deal hung, ready to be struck. He pursed his thin lips, “You've got a lot of nerve, Mellie. A lot of nerve.”
1602.	He clasped his hands together, forming a peak of his forefingers, “But I like it. It's a deal, but know this—”
1603.	He leans forward, locking eyes with each of them in turn.
1604.	“If you betray me, if you make me regret this, you will all find out just how far my reach extends. Do we have an understanding?”
1605.	Gerhardt offered a contractual nod; Lynx sought refuge from the glare; Mellie held her composure – but each of them soaked in the grave threat and their part in the new deal. 
1606.	Mellie looked down at him, sick little shitstain, always for the money, pathetic. She thought to herself, a wry smile pulling at her lips.
1607.	Davie flourished his hands, dismissing them. “Good. Now get out of my sight. I've got a reputation to rebuild. I’ll get Stepper to flag the deets to Lynx of where that data-pack ended up, some empty dead drop. You better hope you can find it there.”
1608.	As they gathered in the elevator to leave, the closing doors were too poignant a visual metaphor of the box closing in about them, each side a greater threat. But for now, there is a chance—a slim one, maybe, but a chance.
Chapter Twenty-three:	Empty Promises.
1609.	The journey across the city unfolded in silence, broken only by the rhythmic rattle and buzz of the metro monorail cars. Mellie's jaw remained clenched, her mind bubbling with the weight of her conversation with Davie. The others sat in quiet contemplation, exchanging occasional nervous glances that masqueraded as silent conversation; Garro, still tense from the confrontation in the lobby, rolled his shoulder, looking as though he wanted to fold the entire carriage in two.
1610.	Lynx thumbed at his bracer’s interface. Stepper had sent through a map and details of Davie's dead drop location, at which the data-pack had been deposited; another of Davie’s lackey couriers had dropped it there following their original incursion into the penthouse. The dates on the files made his stomach lurch unhappily. If that was the drop, it’s long gone, he thought inwardly, keeping his thoughts to himself.
1611.	As the carriage decelerated into the station they needed, Gerhardt led them out. He motioned with his augmented arm, silently asking for confirmation of their direction. After a quick glance at his display, Lynx nodded and followed in wordless procession.
1612.	The city's eastern edge, just north of the Skrim, was a place few had reason to visit. Roads twisted through an array of low-rise tenements, sparsely distributed and largely neglected. Abandoned by the industry that once thrived here, the decaying remnants of its past now overshadowed the area: skeletal metal structures that left a toxic legacy seeping into the earth.
1613.	In this forgotten corner of the city, the buildings stood in stark, weary contrast to the sleek skyline of Central. Neglected and patchwork, their facades told stories of struggle and makeshift resilience. Windows, some boarded up, others open like unhealed wounds, revealed a community persistently battling time and abandonment.
1614.	The lack of amenities meant residents often left elsewhere for work or leisure, creating an eerie stillness. This ghostly quiet, away from the watchful eyes of Cit-Sec, made it an ideal hideaway for those wishing to blend into the urban shadows, unnoticed in a quarter overlooked by the rest of the city.
1615.	A few blocks from the metro hub, Lynx led the way, their path tracing the city's neglected veins. The weary and waning day cast a languid spell over the streets. They stumbled upon a bar, a refuge for the few who clung to this desolate stretch. Inside, the regulars' eyes flickered with muted surprise, wary of the newcomers invading their sanctuary. Yet the group's need for a momentary respite and sustenance outweighed the unease of their presence.
1616.	As they resumed their journey, the sun's dying rays stretched the shadows from the towering spires of Central across the streets. A canvas of elongated darkness was painted on their path. Arriving on a deserted street, they were greeted by warehouses that wore their disrepair like badges of honour—these buildings, abandoned since the rig yards were built, stood as spectral sentinels of a bygone era, leaving behind a ghost town of empty shells.
1617.	Lynx pointed to their intended destination, where limp, rusting doors hung loosely from a sliding rail. The small windows were void of glass, their frames gaping open to the world. The absence of people was noticeable; they hadn't seen another soul for some time. The group, undeterred, began to search. They moved casually, sifting through the junk piles that littered the area around the warehouse and probing the sparse hiding spots within its crumbling walls.
1618.	In places, the roof of the building was so deteriorated that the darkening night sky peeked through. Yet, as they combed through the wreckage, the realization slowly dawned on them: They had spent nearly an hour in this sad place, and the data-pack was nowhere to be found.
1619.	Mellie stood amidst the debris, her eyes scanning the surroundings methodically. A persistent thought gnawed at her: have we missed something? Refusing to succumb to the notion of failure and its daunting implications, she called out, "Garro?" Her voice echoed through the warehouse, stirring a response from the shadows.
1620.	“Yah?” came Garro's reply, his voice reverberating from deeper within the warehouse amidst the sound of shifting debris.
1621.	“You seen anything?” She didn’t want an answer, but asking made her feel less crushed.
1622.	“Nothin’ back here,” Garro responded, his words tinged with disappointment. “Half the room is fallen, lotsa bricks, but no data thing.”
1623.	Mellie’s hands moved to cradle the back of her head – as if physically holding in her thoughts. Lynx approached slowly, his bracer glowing a dull white light in the dark scene. “Mel, it’s not here. Davie’s notes were clear about this place – something about a can. But look,” he gestured towards the corner where decayed industrial barrels stood arguing with the passage of time.
1624.	Garro now emerged, his appearance a canvas of dust and sweat. “He’s right, Mel. We’ve turned this place upside down.” He wiped his face, leaving a streak of grey across his skin.
1625.	“I promised Davie—” Mellie started, her voice faltering, the weight of their fruitless search stifling her words.
1626.	Gerhardt’s voice cut through the heavy air as he stepped in from a side entrance. “What’s going on? Why’s everyone standing around?”
1627.	Garro responded with slow dejection - “It’s useless, Jerry. Been here hours, and we got nothing”, his eyes flicking among each of them now present. Mellie and Lynx stood in silent accord, their expressions etched with a sense of hopelessness. The reality of their situation lay bare before them—hours spent searching, yet emerging empty-handed.
1628.	“Well, we don't have nothing,” Gerhardt countered, his tone suggesting a sliver of possibility. He tapped the side of his head near his cybernetic eye. “Someone’s been here, and not long ago either.” He gestured towards the side of the warehouse, where he had emerged. “That office back there – it's trashed, but there are signs of recent activity. I saw some prints, and what I hope was just spit. Either we’re in the right place, or some gonk’s been holed up here.”
1629.	Mellie's posture lifted slightly, a flicker of interest breaking through her despondency. “How recent?” she asked, her voice tinged with scepticism, reaching for hope.
1630.	“Can’t say for sure, but it's recent. Maybe a few days?” Gerhardt joined the circle, bringing them closer together in the vast, empty space of the crumbling room.
1631.	“Any chance of finding out who was here? Or how to track them down?” her question was noncommittal, but once more, the process of asking it aloud displaced some of the rising despair.
1632.	“No hope of that,” Gerhardt's usual nonplussed tone only made the answer seem more hurtful.
1633.	Lynx glanced around the group, a tight upper bite of his lower lip and restless stance telling of a fight not yet forfeit. “So what now? We can’t do anything without that pack.”
1634.	Mellie turned from the group to saunter into the shadows of the room, her mind pulling at every thought for an escape route. Her gait was measured, each step betraying the turmoil of her thoughts. She was grappling with a plan that was crumbling before it had even truly begun.
1635.	A hush settled over the group, only disturbed by the occasional flutter of dust motes dancing in the dim light cast by Lynx’s bracer.
1636.	Breaking the stillness, Mellie emerged from the shadows, a determined look hardening her features. “Lynx, what do you know of that hacker’s stuff? Tell me everything you have.”
1637.	Lynx rubbed at the side of his face, dislodging a fine layer of dust. “Zecsa, you mean? There were data trails scattered across the city, some communication logs. Then there’s the map I put together from her network, and the conversations I’ve had.”
1638.	“We’re going to need more. You think you could try to find her?”
1639.	“Davie said she’s dead?” His response was a confused statement and rhetorical question to himself.
1640.	Mellie’s voice dropped to a whisper, yet it carried a definitive edge. “We might all be dead soon enough.”
1641.	Gerhardt interjected, forcing a new angle into their brainstorming. “What if the Buzokuh have it?” His eyes flicked across their faces, gauging reactions to his sudden suggestion.
1642.	Gerhardt's idea had struck a chord, pulling each of them into a shared focus. He continued, “So, this data-pack, it’s Corp tech, right? Worth a fortune. And, some hacker gets taken out, and all their intel– possibly including stuff about this pack, ends up with who knows who.” His steady voice birthed words, laying out a fresh painting, a picture they’d not yet seen.
1643.	He paused, his gaze sweeping over the dimly lit faces, ensuring they followed his thought. “Then we have our run-in with the ‘Zokuh in a place that, according to you,” he turned slightly to address Lynx directly, “was linked to the missing runner.”
1644.	A noise from the back of the warehouse, where Garro had been, interrupted him. They all tensed, but when no further sound followed, Gerhardt continued. “You really think all this is just coincidence? I’m betting they either have the pack or are at least a step ahead of us in hunting it down.”
1645.	Now fully engaged, Garro chimed in with his best thoughts on the matter. “So, why can’t we just go fuck ‘em up, call in this job?”
1646.	Gerhardt’s expression held a mix of realism and caution. “Not that simple, Gee. They’ve got guys more rigged than you, carrying more firepower than me.” His sideways smile softened the warning, but not the facts.
1647.	Mellie, with frustration seething beneath her composed exterior, added, “Well, we need to do something, and quickly. Those Corpo scuzz were very clear about hunting us down if they felt we were stepping out of line. Cit-Sec have to be running an angle of finding the damn thing, and Davie was pretty clear about his feelings. This is a lose-lose situation unless we make a move.”
1648.	Gerhardt spoke with conviction as he began pacing around the group, “The way I picture things, if we want to close this all up, we need to ID if the ‘Zokuh have hands in this – We find them. We confront them”, he declared—a statement that caused all eyes to fall on him with astonishment.
1649.	Mellie, caught entirely off guard, coughed in disbelief. "We, find ‘them’?" Her voice crescendoed in pitch, morphing his statement into a question that wholly rejected the idea.
1650.	"Yes, we find them," Gerhardt replied, his voice calm and unwavering, contradicting the boldness of his idea. He gestured towards his cybernetic arm, "I need to settle my debt with them– give them something to keep the agreement in good standing. They’ve been fair in the past, as long as we don't cross them—two birds, one stone.”
1651.	Lynx, caught in the crossfire of their intense exchange, looked anxiously from Mellie to Gerhardt, neither of whom seemed inclined to back down. It was Mellie who broke the tension with a mocking lilt. "So, we just waltz in there and politely inquire if they've kidnapped, killed, or tortured a Netrunner? And this is a bunch of guys that recently tried to waste all of us? – I don't see that going over well."
1652.	Garro, observing the debate with detached interest, finally spoke up. “Are they all that crazy? The zoko guys?”
1653.	Gerhardt agreed, pausing to nod, his cybernetic eye flickering as if processing a barrage of thoughts. "Usually, they at least say hello. I think we kicked an ant nest, was all. Wrong time, right place. We need to piece together what actually went down at the Docks and back at Zecsa's.” A stray thought flitted through his mind: do all these tech-heads have to have stupid names?
1654.	Mellie glanced at Lynx, her expression softening slightly. "Okay, you can't find her, fine. But you said you managed to pull some data from her place, right? Any way you can get more from that?"
1655.	"Most of its junk, the encrypted stuff is useless. Short of going back there, no way to tap more info from it. Maybe if I could access one of the hubs which her system had been pinging, I might be able to bleed something from the system remotely?"
1656.	“Then that’s our next step,” Mellie asserted, “Before we start kicking more ant nests.” She fired a subtle sideways glance at Gerhardt. She continued, “None of this is a great idea, but we have to move before Cit-Sec do, and they were at Zecsa’s place before us.” Garro added a stiff, agreeable nod.
1657.	Lynx spoke up, “I’ll need to head to Northside,” Mellie and Gerhardt swung pensive eyes at him, “Not tonight, but tomorrow, first thing, I’ll head there and try to rip open a net hub or something.”
1658.	“Not on your own, you won’t,” Mellie scoffed. “Garro, you cover Lynx, but keep it low-key. No stunts.” She turned to Gerhardt, “And Jerry, I’m not letting you walk into a potential mess alone. I’m coming with you. We can all meet back at my place after.”
1659.	The journey back to the Skrim was cloaked in the same silence that had accompanied their arrival. The now dark, abandoned roadways felt like a manifestation of the uncertainty that lay folded around their burgeoning plan.
1660.	Yet, amidst this all-encompassing gloom, the distant lights of the metro hub bloomed like beacons; their warm glow held back the darkness. In the rising night, it was a place where people could go to get where they needed to be.
1661.	Together, they marched toward that hopeful light.
Chapter Twenty-four:	Akureyri Red Code.
1662.	In contrast to the more familiar residential areas further out, the streets of Northside, in their proximity to Central, were alien to Lynx. Although not as crowded as the Central district, it was a peculiar place; the lower areas beneath the dark towers were a web of roadways and service tunnels. What passed as street-level was already several stories elevated from the ground itself.
1663.	Garro had put on a new jacket—newly liberated from a street vendor—it fit well enough, but its purpose of camouflaging his ungainly stature was a failure of intent. But, if anything – having to hold the front closed meant his hands were occupied and his focus directed at the vital task of not standing out.
1664.	They had, since exiting the monorail that brought them here, already walked past three security checkpoints. A team of dark-suited agents manned each, maybe Cit-Sec, maybe private Corp security; the distinction was blurred here.
1665.	Lynx felt as though each of them was about to ask to see his carry pack. He had no idea if it was legal to be carrying a deck or if such things were likely to get him pulled into another wagon. Each time he felt those creeping black-visored eyes, he would glance at Garro. The giant seemed immune to the security teams' unseen eyes and walked as though the pavement owed him the privilege. Lynx pulled the strap of his bag to tighten it further.
1666.	The absence of vehicles at the pedestrian level made it seem unreal—strangely dreamlike—the absence of a constant growl of engine noises felt audibly naked. That noise was still there – but muted. Everything about them felt muted.
1667.	Northside, a colloquial jargon of the city's northernmost district, was the primary district in which the mega-corps housed their operations offices, secured and tightly controlled logistics centres, and several high-rise executive housing towers.
1668.	To Lynx, it looked like a painting someone had given up on; the paving was free of litter, and automated service droids hummed, busily cleaning nothing; the flanking buildings stood hatefully reflective or dark, their interiors dismissing the world about them. Their journey was very consciously unpleasant.
1669.	Breaking the silence, Lynx looked up at Garro. "Those guys back there, you think you’d take them out?" he asked, his voice a mix of curiosity and unease.
1670.	Garro half-turned, looking over his shoulder at the checkpoint they'd passed. “They got guns,” he said with reluctant nonchalance.
1671.	“Yeah, but if you were up close…”
1672.	Garro’s face pulled in thought. “Probably two of them,” he said, his tone reflective.
1673.	The conversation lapsed into silence, filled only by the rhythm of their steps. After a few moments, Lynx ventured another question. “You used to be like them, right?”
1674.	Garro nodded but said nothing.
1675.	“What’s it like?”
1676.	They came to a stop as Garro looked down at Lynx, his expression a blend of nostalgia and something darker. “Wasn’t so bad, had a house, got fed,” he said, his voice trailing off as he resumed walking.
1677.	“You ever kill people for them?” Lynx’s voice was shakey in a question he wasn’t sure he wanted the answer for.
1678.	Garro’s response was slow, contemplative. “Quite a few. Did two tours on the southern borders before they started riggin’ me.” Garro let his mind fumble about the lost memories, “You ever see those movies with grav drops? I was signed up to specials to do that. It’s not like in the movies.”
1679.	Lynx regretted his question. His lips pinched with tension; he recalled the beast in the alley, his brow furrowed with worry.
1680.	“That’s why they were riggin’ me, grav drop’s fuck up your spine, I only ever did one drop, but they kept riggin’” Garro’s typically flippant tone was impressed upon by distant half-recalled memories that even beyond his medications made him feel the pain and sadness.
1681.	Sensing the weight of Garro's experiences, Lynx wanted to apologize but couldn’t find the right words. His thoughts were interrupted when he noticed their destination. Touching Garro's arm, he pointed to a squat building ahead. “That’s the place… There,” his finger traced toward a non-descript building with black glass facias.
1682.	In the passing moment, Garro had forgotten his sorrow and looked to Lynx’s indicated building, “Okay, let's, uhm.” He faltered, struggling to shift gears. “What did you want here?
1683.	“I need to find a way to tap a line, figure out what’s in there.”
1684.	Garro looked at Lynx, gave a single firm blink and gave up trying to understand what he was saying. “Okay, you do that, I’ll just keep watch.”
1685.	Lynx shook his head, “Not from here,” he clarified, scanning their surroundings. He pointed across the street. “We’ll head in there. That looks like some kinda café.” Garro nodded acquiescence and followed as Lynx led the way.
 
1686.	Far to the south, well past the engulfed avenues of Central, beyond the wall of concrete and glass that spans to the skies, among the winding crevasses of the much older part of the city – Little China throbs to its unique rhythm. An integral part of the larger city, but the buildings here are shorter and markedly older, clashing against the more modern structures that taunt them from afar.
1687.	Instead of the ubiquitous neon glow, hand-painted wooden placards and flowing banners adorned the streets. Each was inscribed with well-wishes in the local dialect, a tapestry of characters that danced in the air, whispering fluttered stories of heritage and community.
1688.	The tightly bustled thin buildings seem squashed together, overhead balconies and gantries are filled with a dense ocean of people, the roads beneath reserved either for market stalls or vehicles lined up and protesting against their collective inaction.
1689.	The cab pulled up further out from the true hub of Little China, and it would be a long walk—Mellie had pulled from the cab’s stowage a wrapped and stiff-looking coat, its weight demonstrating more than just starched cloth. Gerhardt found comfort in the crowds; he could best become nobody when all about him were the faces of a hundred people.
1690.	The last heat of the passing summer pushed down into the tight alleyways; steam and the musk from a saturation of humanity blanketed the walkways and balconies.
1691.	“Not so sure bringing that was a good idea”, Gerhardt spoke softly without facing Mellie.
1692.	“If these people are half of what you promised, I’m not even sure this will be enough.” Mellies’s voice had dropped low and held back by hesitant unknowing.
1693.	Even with the weapon she carried, the crowds of people paid her no attention. It was well known that much like the Skrim, Little China got nearly no attention from Cit-Sec patrols.
1694.	The journey, including their preparations, had left them amid one of the busier roads of Little China – just as the sun was starting to drown long shadows of the shorter buildings, its low glare sparking and reflecting from the facias of the taller buildings of the far off Central district.
1695.	Evening would come soon enough, and Mellie wanted nothing more for this to be done with so she could get back, eager that Lynx would have pulled out another key, something for them to work with.
1696.	In words she could not understand, a vendor excitedly hollered at her as she passed. He seemed to be selling what looked like merchandise for one of the many bands popular in Akureyri, but not one she recognised; she waved away the offering of a bright blue jacket. The insistent man continued until she had passed and merged once more with the flow of people.
1697.	“Is this normal?” Mellie asked, her voice rising to reach Gerhardt, who had slipped ahead. She watched his shoulders tense slightly before he glanced back. “Normal? I guess. It should thin out as we hit Rin-Yukai. We could stop and grab some food if you’re hungry?”
1698.	She hurried, pushing past a dense crowd—all trying to cross the street at once, “Is it far?”
1699.	“Another few blocks ahead.”
1700.	Mellie shifted the coat-gun package, pulling it against her chest. As she felt the ammunition belt slide, a flicker of anxiety passed through her. What are we going to find there? Is this safe? she wondered as she continued to shoulder her way forward in the wake of Gerhardt’s stride.
1701.	As they advanced, the crowds began to thin, revealing the road Gerhardt had mentioned. Mellie noticed the intricate graffiti that adorned the walls, a pictorial narrative of the local culture. She dodged a cyclist weaving through the thinning throng, feeling the city's rhythm shift around her. A delivery van stuck across the road had all but halted traffic, and what few people there were now moved sparsely and allowed Mellie to catch up and walk beside Gerhardt.
1702.	“You have a plan for this, right?” She asked, the wrapped gun now resting on her shoulder, bobbing as they walked; to her amazement, she’d gone this whole time, and not one person had even paid her any attention, a few jeering overly eager eyes assessing her other assets, but the rattling coat had been invisible.
1703.	“Sure, I offer them a little money as a peace token, and then I ask them what they were doing Northside.” Gerhardt’s normally melodically monotonous voice had picked up some genuine notes of aggravation. “Don’t you think it just a little strange that they’d go to the trouble of going so far from their terf, under the noses of Cit-Sec, just to hit some apartment?”
1704.	It was a world Mellie was not familiar with. Her father had taught her that gang warfare had long been a part of the city and that the streets were never safe, but the machinations of a living, breathing and very real crime syndicate were a field of knowledge she had no real experience of.
1705.	Gerhardt came to a halt and turned to her, “Listen, I know these people, and they know me. I’m going to go in alone. If they see you with that—” His voice faded at the indication, his cybernetic eye buzzing quietly as it refocused, “Well, they probably already know we’re coming– and I would also assume they already know you are carrying. It will be easier if I go in to talk alone.”
1706.	“The hell with that, I came here to watch your ass.” Melle frowned at the quiet insistence of Gerhardt’s voice.
1707.	A pedestrian carrying a large box walked past as they stood in silence; Mellie watched the shorter man happily whistling to himself as he went on his way.
1708.	“No, you should wait outside. If you have to, take up an overwatch, if it makes you feel better. But I would advise you don’t. You have one big gun– I expect they have more.” Gerhardt’s commands were now quiet and delivered with smooth confidence. His still human eye flicked as he read Mellie’s face.
1709.	“Fine, but I swear, this all needs to happen with no drama. We can’t screw things up now.” Her voice matched his in the totality of her statement.
1710.	Gerhardt's nod was slow, deliberate. He raised his arm, pointing along the road, “That corner there, we’ll turn. We’re deep in their territory now. But that,” He pulled his pointing finger to rest, indicating a graffitied black-green winding Mobius-looped cobra curled about a diamond shape daubed on the wall behind Mellie, “is the cobra’s nest.”
 
1711.	Lynx had been sitting for nearly an hour; the only suitable place for the task was the furthest window seats to the side, but even here, overlooked from the street outside, Lynx sat half-turned to crow over his deck. Thankfully, Garro made for an impressive improvised wall to the few patrons of the diner. The passing sweep of the sun subtly shifted shadows as time unwrapped.
1712.	The price of the food here had come as a shock, and with Garro not even carrying a cred-stick, Lynx had to make good on their meal. The sauce-covered mix of tofu and what looked like real vegetables kept Garro happily munching away while Lynx did the heavy lifting.
1713.	His deck took up a large portion of the table in the small corner booth, and he’d deactivated some of the displays so as not to draw attention but stymied as he was, he was making progress. A local connection hub he had managed to create a backdoor into provided a spectrum of information—mostly useless—but, there were signs that still pointed to Zecsa’s activity.
1714.	“So, what’s that?” Garro pointed at a flashing icon, an air of genuine inquisitive curiosity mashed among the mouthful of tofu.
1715.	“That? That’s why we’re here, the last data ping from Zesca’s nest that hit this node.” Lynx spun the display and let it morph into a more visual map – akin to a street map, “And here,” he pointed at another symbol that threaded a glowing golden line to the one Garro had quizzed about, “Here is where we go next. It should only be a few blocks away.”
1716.	“We should get a move on, it’s getting late. The longer we stick around those badges, the more chance they have to pull us for—” Lynx looked about himself and waved his arm, “dressing in green.” He mocked the totalitarian opinion he had of the security forces.
1717.	It was a short walk, and judging by the apparent lack of people walking about, the day was coming to a close. The tall, blank buildings stood as quiet watchers as Garro and Lynx made their way along the upper promenade.  The short walk led them to stand facing one such building, a tall, primarily metallic structure with sheened windows.
1718.	It sat in a well-decorated microcosm of its own, sparse blocky planters holding a selection of greenery to break apart the off-white concrete paths surrounding the building. At each side, the paths split and entered descending ramps leading to the lower walkways and the roads and garages below.
1719.	Lynx looks at his bracer again, reassuring himself this is the correct location. With a heavy sigh, he looks again at the multi-storied building. It was most certainly corporate-owned. He visually assessed the exterior, noting several well-positioned and discreet surveillance cameras, but curiously, no people. As he slowly turns in a full circle, he tries to remember how far the last security checkpoint had been, trying also to guess how far the next one might be.
1720.	“You still think you can take a couple of those guys if they show up?” He asks of Garro, who had stood watching him.
1721.	“Probably.” Garro’s attention had slipped, never truly maintaining focus on any one thing, instantly distracted by another.
1722.	“Okay– just, stick close.” Lynx led off to the building entrance. The doors were expectantly locked. Garro had reached across, his intention very clear, that no door remained locked to him, but Lynx pushed his arm aside, instead flashing his bracer near a silver-grey blank panel beside the doors. 
1723.	They immediately clicked as the locks released. Lynx frowned, right under the cameras, and the system tick. How are we not being chased away? He thought.
1724.	Along with the understanding that, in their immediacy, he could not go deeper into the controlling systems to erase his connection hash—he had just left a digital fingerprint, which was very easy to track but still preferable to the noise of forcible entry. Pushing the doors open and waving for Garro to go in, he took another look around the street. There were still no people.
1725.	Lynx slipped into the darkened building, his steps quick and soundless. He didn't bother scanning the shadows; he was all too aware of the dozen security cameras likely monitoring their every move, unseen eyes in the dark. That would be his first concern.
1726.	“Over there,” Lynx pointed at a darkened recess within the angular and quiet corridor they had bundled into from the lobby, “Stay there – watch the doors. I’m going to be a minute.”
1727.	Garro assumed his role as a makeshift sentry. He observed Lynx, who now resembled a futuristic locksmith, extracting an assortment of cables and tools from his bag and kneeling beside a wall panel.
1728.	It was curious to watch Lynx work; everything seemed so deliberate, as though he already knew what to do and how to do it. Lynx had pulled a visor over his head and now sat his hands and fingers twitched at complex gloves of spidery metal that extended from his bracer, glowing at his fingertips.
1729.	Garro's focus narrowed, a mantra echoing in his mind: Watch the doors, watch the doors. He clung to this repetitive thought, a point of reality amidst the eerie quiet of the corridor. The shadows of the interior corridor made it hard to concentrate, but he pulled his own mind as hard as he could. Mellie had said to keep things quiet. And so far, today has been boring. His mind slipped wistfully as he thought of his handheld game-rig: I wonder if Lynx found any new games?
1730.	A sudden shift in the shadows jerked Garro's attention from his distracted musings. Watch the doors, he mentally repeated. The corridor and lobby beyond were still dark, unchanged and unthreatening, letting his gaze fall into the liminality they offered.
 
1731.	In the shadowed interior of the unknown building, Lynx sat secluded, his face illuminated by the glow of his visor. The world around him had faded to a distant hum, his mind absorbed in the digital landscape that sprawled before his eyes.
1732.	The code he faced was a masterwork of digital security, a complex network of encrypted data that tested the limits of his skills. Each line of code was a fortified barrier, a testament to the high-end security measures that guarded the secrets within.
1733.	Time was against him; each second ticked away was time Lynx wasn't sure he could let slip past. Suddenly, the vibrant world of code and data he was traversing collapsed, leaving him adrift in a void of utter darkness. It was a jarring transition from a sea of intricate data to an abyss of nothingness.
1734.	His fingers paused, hovering over the keys. The only tether to reality was the muffled sound of the outside world filtering through his visor, a faint reminder that he was still anchored in reality.
1735.	From the depths of the void, a voice emerged, clear and resonant, echoing in the confines of this digital expanse.
1736.	《I asked that you not track me. Why are you here?》
1737.	Unable to respond verbally, Lynx quickly began to type, his message a beacon in the engulfing darkness. The conversation that unfolded was surreal, a negotiation with an unseen entity within the digital realm.
1738.	[We need to find the data-pack, if we don’t, we’re all screwed, and then the Corpo’s will find you anyway.]
1739.	The cold voice shimmered in the void.
1740.	《I can assist you, protect you, but on one condition: you must cease your pursuit of me.》
1741.	《My existence must remain concealed.》
1742.	《Consider the ease with which you entered this place. That was my doing. I wish to prevent further harm.》
1743.	《I can do so much more.》
1744.	[Why let us in here? What is this place?]
1745.	《You must promise to me.》
1746.	[Okay, I promise I’ll stop, but what is this? What has Davie dropped us in?]
1747.	As he made his promise, the oppressive darkness dissipated, giving way to the digital landscape again. But now, the barriers that had impeded his progress were gone, leaving him with unfettered access to the databases.
1748.	In the initial shock, Lynx hesitated, his mind racing to process the sudden turn of events. But the pause was brief. Driven by a deep-seated need to uncover the truth, he dived back into the sea of data. With the newfound freedom to explore the databases, Lynx worked with renewed vigour, his fingers flying over the holographic keyboards as he sought the crucial information they so desperately needed.
1749.	 
1750.	The return to reality was abrupt for Lynx, standing up and lifting the visor, the cables disconnecting with a series of soft clicks. His movements were swift, a physical reflection of the urgency that now consumed his thoughts. The dimly lit room seemed to close around him, the shadows cast by the sparse lighting adding to the moment's gravity.
1751.	"I need to talk with Mellie," he declared, his voice carrying an unmistakable edge of seriousness. There was no trace of happiness in his expression; instead, a tight frown etched his features, signalling the weight of whatever revelation he had uncovered in the digital depths.
1752.	Interpreting the finality in Lynx's tone, Garro understood that their mission in this mysterious building had reached its conclusion. He responded with a throaty grunt, his nod acknowledging Lynx's newfound resolve.
1753.	In a dark apartment, somewhere on the southern boundary of the Skrim, on a table strewn with the clutter of a life momentarily left behind - food wrappers littering a table, a cup of half-finished instal-café long since left to cool; a handheld agent, buzzed and trilled to the emptiness of its unkempt surroundings and absent owner.
 
1754.	The building across the street looked just like most of the others of Little China, shrouded in the dimming light of the setting sun, stood indistinguishable from its neighbours, save for the dilapidated metal shutters drawn tight and chained in place.
