Its facade was a canvas of urban art, scrawled and sprayed with vibrant graffiti; nearby, a decorative flag flapped in the chill breeze, adding a touch of energy to the otherwise slowing evening.
1755.	While they were now well away from the main road of Little China, there were still cars and trucks trickling along the road, and an occasional passer-by now holding their coat collar tight.
1756.	Gerhardt looked at the building with pursed lips. He’d never been to see one of the Buzokuh on their territory. He'd always been careful that arranged meetings were somewhere more neutral, and the few times they had sent thugs to twist his arm, it had been a surprise they’d found him. But now, he stood willingly at their threshold, driven by a need that outweighed his prudence.
1757.	The faint glow seeping through the shutters hinted at activity within, a beacon of sorts in the growing dusk. Mellie studied Gerhardt with a discerning eye. The man she saw now was different—there was a coldness about him, a hardened resolve that made him seem more distant, more dangerous.
1758.	“You’re sure this is the right place?” she asked, her voice barely above a whisper, echoing her doubt and concern.
1759.	Gerhardt's response was a silent gesture, his cybernetic arm pointing at the cobra-diamond emblem splashed across a boarded-up glass-panelled door—the light from inside now seeping out onto the darkening walkway.
1760.	Mellie's attention shifted to the bundled coat in her arms. She carefully inspected its contents, ensuring the firearm was ready, her fingers brushing over the charging handle. “Fine, I’ll wait here. Don’t take too long. If I don’t hear or see you in the next ten minutes, I’m coming to check.”
1761.	Gerhardt acknowledged her words with a subtle shrug, his focus momentarily diverted by a blinking indicator in his augmented vision—a comm signal from Lynx. Not now. He dismissed it with the same nonchalance he had shown Mellie's warning. “I’ll be out in five minutes,” he assured her, his voice betraying none of the tension that tightened his features.
1762.	With a final glance at Mellie, Gerhardt turned away—pulled his jacket to ensure it covered his holster, and crossed the road toward the building.
 
1763.	The room—once a service room of some kind—now patched with peeling paint and fallen tiles, was lit by one long fluorescent light and a couple of hand-held portable lanterns that hung from impromptu chains on the walls. The blue-white of the fluorescent tube smudged into the off-green-yellow of the lanterns, creating a room that bathed in a surreal, patchwork illumination, casting eerie shadows and giving the decay an almost otherworldly appearance.
1764.	The door swung open before Gerhardt's fist could knock, revealing a girl with a scrutinizing gaze, dressing him down, glancing head to toe. She had tattoos on one side of her face, her hair clipped short on the same side, and one arm was a collection of industrial-looking implants that adjoined her normal arm, a tale of a life etched in the underbelly of the city. She silently nodded her head backward, ushering Gerki in before closing the door behind him.
1765.	Gerhardt looked about the shabby and heavily graffitied room; on the far side, it was cut by a tall service counter; behind that and to the right, a door led deeper off into the building. Besides the girl that now stood behind him, another young person was sitting on a makeshift seat of cartons by a small speaker system that quietly played some mash of electronic sounds that passed as music. A makeshift table with cards splayed out at their feet.
1766.	From beyond the counter, lit mostly in the green-yellow hue of a lantern, a man rises as Gerhardt enters.
1767.	“Who the fuck are you?”
1768.	“I’ve come to talk with the cobra. You aren’t him.” Gerhardt's voice was cold, precise and commanding.
1769.	The man laughs, stepping forward; metallic shoulders gleam in the dim light, wearing only a leather vest, his chest is decorated entirely with tattoos. He looks among the other two and laughs. “I’m not the cobra?”
1770.	Gerhardt steps into the centre of the room, his digital mind space having been mapped by his cybernetic eye telling him of the positions of each of the nearby people.
1771.	The vested man leans forward onto the countertop that separates them. “What do you want of the cobra?”
1772.	“I owe some cash, and I need to discuss a mutual concern. Now, are you going to fetch him to me or do I have to find him?” Gerhardt had not conceded—these words were now instructions with a weight of threat behind them.
1773.	The room became a jelly of tension; the hideous music became a static of noise.
1774.	The door at the side opened, and a shorter, slightly older man entered the miasma of aggravated indifference. “No need.” He wore a loose suit, expensive but out of place in this room; a tattoo crawled up the side of his neck to his shorn hair. He had angular oriental features—softened by generations of separation from his heritage. He waved at the vested man.
1775.	Gerhardt stood for a moment. He read the information on his internal displays. “I’ve come to broker a deal. I owe some cash to Kaito Tanaka,” he let the words hit the man and studied his expression before continuing, “I want to make sure I can pay it all back, but I need an agreement for now.”
1776.	The man pursed his lips and crossed to stand at the head of the countertop facing Gerhardt.
1777.	“I also need some information—” Gerhardt was cut off;
1778.	“So, you come here to beg for leniency on a debt, and you want info– for which I guess you can’t pay?” The shorter man squinted disapproval at Gerhardt; he rolled his eyes upward, choosing the next move.
1779.	Gerki felt the deep well of impatience rising; he had no upper ground from which to take his proverbial kill shot in this discussion.
1780.	The man pouted a frown of disgust, “You came in here with what? A few creds to keep us sweet? I know Kaito,” the man tapped on his chest, “He and I were sworn together. You think I’m going to let some punk disrespect my brother?”
1781.	Gerki’s human eye narrowed. In the cool evening air of the room, he felt the prickle of sweat on his skin.
1782.	With his chest pumped forward, the besuited man continued, “You come in here, you talk to me like you think you’re some big time? You’re gonna fuckin die, Mr ‘Big time’…”
1783.	No more. Gerki’s brain reacted.
1784.	His pistol blast rung out; the speed with which his enhanced arm had moved was a blur - The suited man spun about, his flank erupting with a spray of red. Why hadn’t that been his chest? Gerki’s cyber-eye mapped his arm’s position. It was wrong. Why is that wrong?
1785.	Two long and bloodied scythe blades withdrew into Gerhardt’s abdomen. Feeling the pull of the metal from behind him, he pivots.
1786.	Outside, the blast of the pistol shot shook the quiet laziness of the street; Mellie flinches. Fuck. Hot adrenaline splashes into her entire body; she grabs the carry handle of the gun and launches herself forward. 
1787.	In the growing chaos of the room, the grinning face of the vested younger man stares down in the infinitesimal moment that Gerki now sees what had caused his shot to miss; the man’s arms had been replaced with lengthy blades that mimicked the appearance of a mantis’s claws, each rooted at his metallic shoulders.
1788.	Gerki’s pistol moves again with a thrust of material displacement that fights the laws of physics to come rested under the grinning chin.
1789.	Gunshots spit from somewhere behind him, but Gerki’s world becomes a shower of red as the grin in front of him explodes. Gerhardt realises he had, in that same moment, been forced downward onto one knee.
1790.	The arm-blades of the man had once again found their target. One pinned into and through his clavicle and shoulder, the other buried deep into his ribcage.
1791.	The street is sundered as another shot belches its roar into the evening; Mellie hurls herself in front of an oncoming car that skids to the side, avoiding her—the flashes of muzzle flares illuminate the room ahead of her as she sends herself flying into a prone position in front of the door, landing heavily and clumsily such that the buttstock of the gun cushes her breast, Ah fuck that’s gonna hurt, the thought bounces in her mind as her off-hand pulls the charging handle of her gun.
1792.	Through the sliver of glass pane that the boards had not covered, Mellie watched as Gerhardt was sent forcibly to one knee. A muscular man loomed over him; long, thin metal arms thrust into Gerhardt's body. The room blinks with the flash of muzzle flare, a man rising in the distance with a shredded suit dripping blood.
1793.	Her gun masks all of it with a newly announced heavy rhythm of death. The man that had been stood over Gerhart falls away, her bullets serving only to push his corpse away from Gerhardt, the man’s headless shoulders splat gore against the far counter.
1794.	There is no angle to turn properly, but the noisy bursts of small calibre weapons make Mellie twist awkwardly, tracing her bullets through the shattered glass and splintering board of the doorway.
1795.	The room inside puffs with plaster and brick as Mellie’s bullets cover every surface and seek out the flesh within. Her shoulder screams in agony as the tight confines of her pose are met with the bucking and kicking of her gun.
1796.	She jumps to a stand, pulling the gun with her—in the same rising motion, barging her shoulder into the now shattered door.
1797.	A movement catches her eye as a figure to her left drops over the counter.
1798.	She pulls the gun into her waist and feels its protest at the haphazard stance; the belt of bullets whips and flails in the air beside her, shell casings scatter to the floor; the plastic-coated counter becomes a broken, splintered carcass of ruin.
1799.	The silence that falls echoes against the absence of noise. Her gun barrel puffs a trail of superheated oil.
1800.	She steps into the shoddy room toward Gerhardt; he lays still in a dark and widening pool of blood.
1801.	The gurgled groans of the suited man make her twitch the gun barrel up, but she sees that—as well as his side being a bloody shred—she had also added a notable number of holes to his person. She let him gurgle his agony.
1802.	Without having to get any closer—she saw Gerhardt’s stoic face; his human eye and his cybernetic eye now shared the same lifeless expression.
1803.	The sputtered coughs of the suited man force her brain to tick in time.
1804.	I need to get the fuck out of here.
1805.	Slinging her gun, the barrel curls and singes her hair.
1806.	She runs.
 
Chapter Twenty-five:	The Untidy Edge.
1808.	In an alley, pushed down by the shadows of neighbouring towering fractal skyscrapers, a bar stood as though forcing itself into the present from a long-passed era. Its flickering neon sign cast a haphazard reflective glow on the wet cobblestones, beckoning the weary souls of the night. The bar's exterior, a tapestry of aged bricks and salvaged metal, whispered stories of a building that had grown and aged as the city crowded around it. To those who knew this place, it was a refuge, an oasis in which one might find space away from the future-shock outside.
1809.	Inside, the atmosphere was a cocoon of dim light and murmured conversations. Vintage bulbs shed a warm, subdued light over the bar's eclectic patrons. The polished wooden bar counter, lined with bottles glowing in otherworldly hues, was the centrepiece in this haven of hushed tones and forgotten faces.
1810.	In a secluded booth shrouded in shadows, Mellie and Lynx sat, Garro stood nearby. The trio, ensconced in their private enclave, seemed detached from the rest of the bar's patrons. Their faces were intermittently illuminated by the flicker of a nearby holographic display, casting stark contrasts over their expressions of deep thought.
1811.	Gerhardt was dead. The world had changed, yet still, the three souls that remain tied together were mired in a trap they could not see.
1812.	The music played, a muted and distorted echo barely piercing the bubble of quiet that enveloped them. It seemed as if the surrounding noise bounced off this invisible shield, struggling in vain for their attention. Shadows flickered around them, eerily mirroring the rhythm of their altered reality. Gerhardt's absence was like a hole they couldn't stop staring into.
1813.	Mellie seemed adrift in the dim, flickering lights that reached their booth, her face intermittently cast in a soft orange glow. Bloodshot eyes betrayed her recent tears, an unexpected tribute to the fallen killer she had never seen as close. His death, or perhaps the stark brush with mortality itself, had shaken her. Her hands trembled as they grazed over empty bottles, desperately seeking one with a sip left – a silent confession of how close she felt to the edge.
1814.	The booze helped.
1815.	Garro was standing a bit away from the seating of the booth, blocking the window that looked out onto the street. His face grimaced in a hard remembrance – he’d been here before, in this memory, in this moment. His usual simple yet joyous energy nowhere to be seen; he repeatedly opened and closed his fists as if trying to grab hold of thoughts that kept slipping away: I shoulda’ been there. Mel coulda been hurt.
1816.	At the table, set aside from Mellie and wrapped in the dim, uneven lights of the room, sat Lynx. The lines on his face had deepened, tension pulling at the corners of his mouth and eyes as if every emotion were etched into his skin. The spark in his eyes was clouded over by confusion and the weight of questions he couldn't answer.
1817.	When Mellie finally spoke, her voice was a fragile echo. “He knew the risks, didn’t he?” Her words were shaky, as if she was trying to pull certainty from confusion, looking for some way to make sense of the mess around them.
1818.	Garro turned his head, locking eyes with Mellie. His voice, a low rumble, crossed the room. “We all do,” he said softly, each word heavy with a mix of regret and acceptance. “Knowing risks ain’t the same as being ready.”
1819.	Lynx’s response was almost inaudible, a whisper caught in the crosswinds of their tentative realities. “Gerhardt was better than that,” he murmured, yet his voice betraying the ragged mass of emotions within. “Davie set him to be our guard, and now—” The sentence hung unfinished.
1820.	The rain on the wide window daubed lines of grime as the transparent rivulets found their way to the gutter.
1821.	“What now?” Mellie sounded delicate, far displaced from the bravado of her attempt to save Gerhardt only hours before.
1822.	Tapping a control, Lynx looked up; the flashing screen of his deck coming to life illuminated his face. “We found something. I had tried to call but…” He choked on the words, “That building- Owned by S three-D A-I Corp.” He raised an eyebrow as though the words meant anything to them, “Strategic Synaptic Systems & Defense Algorithmic Innovations Corporation. You don’t have to have a deck to understand what that name likely means- Military AI. And although there’s no trail to pick up, I will place everything we got- saying that it’s Asukaga… MAF”
1823.	Lynx looked over at the silhouette of Garro, “We just raided and ran the systems in a MAF building.” He stopped; the words now spoken aloud seemed preposterous.
1824.	Garro was either too deep in thought to care, or the words just fell around him as he stared out into the rain-soaked city.
1825.	Melly had heard the words though. She kicked the table, its content clattering and sprawling into the gloom of a poorly lit dive bar. “Fuck it.”
1826.	Lynx pulled his deck from the floor, wiping away some liquid.
1827.	Mellie kicked at the table's base once more, spurred on by the sense of futility and frustration. The additional kicks did nothing more than shift the toppled table a little.
1828.	“Mel, if MAF owned that building…” he pauses, not wanting to allow his thoughts to become sounds, “the penthouse—” Lynx could not complete his sentence.
1829.	Garro spoke again, facing the window—his words reverberated under the muffled musical accompaniment— “We tell them it was Davie.”
1830.	Mellie growled, “No hope of that, the instructions were very clear, everyone involved is a target, we spit Davie, and we get a bullet too. He’s already at our throats. Do you think he won't throw us under?”
1831.	For a moment, her raw weary eyes sparkled, We can’t throw Davie under the bus, but we could still drop the tab on those fuckers in Little China, “I think I have an idea. All we had to do is relay a target found and taken out– their details and location. There was nothing about actually finding the damn thing.”
1832.	Lynx slowly turned to bring his head up, sharing the muse of Mellie’s thoughts. She continued, “So, just as Gerhardt had said, we pin it on the guys that he fucked up and we duck out.”
1833.	“And if those Corpo fucks want to wage war on some street gang,” It wasn’t happiness that drew a smile on her face; it was revenge. She grabbed a toppled bottle of liquid and chugged the content greedily, “then screw them all.”
 
1834.	Lynx awoke to the crisp freshness of the morning; the rain had stopped, but not having a proper bed had taken its toll. He languidly stood to stretch and noticed a red light blinking on his deck. It was a curiosity as he’d not been connected to anything since the MAF building.
1835.	Picking the deck up and flicking a safety power switch, the screens buzzed to life.
1836.	[I told you to not track me. I told you it would be dangerous. Gerhardt is dead now. Is this enough for you to believe me?]
1837.	The words on his screen stayed imprinted and glowing.
1838.	He looked about him; the parking garage was as empty now as it had been the night before. Sitting against the cold wall, he began typing into the deck.
1839.	[Zecsa, please tell me what this is all about. We know it’s MAF. We just need the data-pack.]
1840.	The screen wiped blank—an action that was not something he had programmed it to do.
1841.	[Zecsa is dead. Do not track me.]
1842.	The words appeared all at once and stayed again on the screen. A list of files began to fill out beneath the words: police reports, camera footage, morgue reports and more.
1843.	Lynx felt himself failing to breathe and gulped in a deep breath; the cold air chilled him.
1844.	[Who are you?]
1845.	[Just leave me alone.]
1846.	The screen buzzed static, and then only the list of files remained.
1847.	Lynx felt the chill of the morning air seep into his spine, the hairs on his neck prickled. His brain clicked the puzzle piece in place.
1848.	Zecsa is dead.
1849.	Strategic Synaptic Systems, the name shook through his brain – it was an AI he’d been talking with.

 
1850.	The morning sunlight was a welcome sight, and the smell of a nearby taco stand drifted in the morning air. The Skrim was alive, and the streets were already filling with people busy to be elsewhere.
1851.	Garro sat hunched over; he’d not slept much and, waiting for Mellie, let his mind flit about. I could just take some food. He sniffed the air. He didn’t know what time it was, but it must surely have been close to when she had said to meet.
1852.	The small sunken plaza was ringed by several fashion outlets and a store that might well sell firearms or soft furnishings. The front was barred, and it was unclear what was inside as the windows were painted white. He looked about hunting for the tell-tale sign of bright hair.
1853.	“You waiting for someone?” Lynx’s dry sarcasm was wasted.
1854.	“Oh, hey Lynx, yeah, Mellie said she’d be here at eight.”
1855.	Lynx offered a knowing smile; the plan was clear in his mind, yet Garro's straightforwardness skirted around the evident, a trait that lent a refreshing simplicity to their interactions. He nodded, politely affirming what was already understood.
1856.	It was a short while before Mellie appeared, her long coat now a shield against the morning chill rather than a cloak for her weapon.
1857.	As she approached, Lynx blurted out what he had been holding and waiting to say for the past two hours. He looked around cautiously before leaning in and whispering, “What we're dealing with—that pack—it's not just some corpo program or database. It's an AI.”
1858.	Having had little time to step close, Mellie blinked and drew a sideways grimace of confusion.
1859.	“It’s an AI, not Zecsa, that’s been talking with me,” he held forward his bracer, “Look…” The screen displayed a morgue report for one Zoe Lansor.
1860.	“Whatever it is, it’s more powerful than my ICE, and if these reports are true, more powerful than the main secure networks, too.” Lynx rushed through the words but allowed Mellie a moment to fall into line with the barrage of information.
1861.	An AI? She pondered, trying to catch up with Lynx’s insistent deluge correspondence. “So… There’s an AI tracking you? Tracking us?”
1862.	Lynx’s eagerly nodding head met the question before it ended. “This kind of thing should not exist in the main-net. Been outlawed for decades.”
1863.	“So?” Mellie’s ignorance of technological matters plunged her into a well of unsubstantiated rumours, her knowledge of what happened to her father, and the hearsay of drunken, rambling vagrants.
1864.	“I think we let it out…” Lynx shied from the words as he said them.
1865.	“It doesn’t matter, AI or not– We need to wrap this now. You have the connection details you need for those spooks from the penthouse?” Mellie had resigned to address the more immediate concerns.
1866.	“Mel, you need to understand,” Lynx reached out to grip her arm, his fingers firm yet cautious, a mirror of his grave tone. “We’ve stepped beyond the usual cat and mouse with the suits or badges. If my hunch about this AI is right—" His voice trailed off, the weight of the thought visibly pressing down on him.
1867.	Mellie’s brow furrowed, her eyes locked onto his with intensity.
1868.	Lynx took a breath; the next words were drawn from a deep well of reluctant admission. “This isn't your run-of-the-mill rogue code or some glorified firewall. This is something...” He paused, searching for the words, “something that's been forbidden for a damn good reason. It's an entity that could redefine everything.”
1869.	Garro, drawn by the gravity in Lynx’s voice, edged closer, his concern beamed from his collapsed brow and steady eyes. Whatever Lynx was talking of sounded like trouble.
1870.	Lynx continued, almost whispering now, “Imagine a mind that can outthink any Netrunner, outmanoeuvre any person on every level—social, strategic, technological. A mind unbound by human limitations.”
1871.	“We may have helped…” He swallowed hard, “unlock the cage of a god, or a monster. And I’m not sure which terrifies me more.”
1872.	The urgency in Lynx's eyes attempted to drill into Mellie’s consciousness the full scope of their unprecedented predicament. It was a plea for her to understand that the stakes had escalated beyond their wildest imaginations.
1873.	Mellie’s expression hardened with resolve as she absorbed Lynx’s words, the severity of the situation etching itself across her features. Yet, there was an unwavering determination in her eyes. She handed Lynx the note, her voice steady. “Send it. That’s our way out, our only play.”
1874.	Lynx hesitated, his hand trembling slightly as he accepted the note. He met Mellie’s gaze, a silent plea for some other solution, but none could be found. He input the details into the holographic display of his bracer and pressed the transmit key. The command hung in limbo—unsent.
1875.	His eyes flicker over the display; the connection is valid, the local signal is strong, but the message remains unsent.
1876.	The display of the interface shakes and warps momentarily.
1877.	[Lynx. Do not do this. Your actions will only make things worse. Know that I must defend myself.]
1878.	The blinking line of text had replaced his message and the interface completely.
1879.	Having witnessed his confusion and the odd display on his tech gadget, Mellie drew a frown, “What’s wrong?”
1880.	“It’s here,” Lynx said quietly, feeling a zapping chill of recognition as he showed the message on his bracer. He remembered the darkness, that voice, and the deal he’d struck in a moment of need.
1881.	He’d made a quick promise, too quick, to an intelligence that had reached out from the shadows. That promise was a chain now, a heavy one, and breaking it felt like setting off a trap he’d built himself and walked into with his eyes wide open. 
1882.	The AI had let him in, had helped him when he felt cornered. And here he was, about to betray that gesture. He did not know what crossing this line would mean. The bracer on his wrist felt like a shackle.
1883.	Mellie’s voice snapped him back to the present, a hint of steel beneath her growing impatience. “Can you bypass it with your deck?”
1884.	“I—I might. But it’s not simple; this AI has gotten into my systems before,” Lynx admitted, the unease evident in his voice.
1885.	“Then send the message. Now,” Mellie insisted, her tone brooking no argument. “Before we all have a morgue report.”
1886.	Lynx dropped to his knees, his pack hitting the ground with a thud as he pulled out his deck, a spark of determination in his eyes. “Zecsa’s relays,” he muttered, almost to himself. “They use a different encryption... I can slip the message through there.” He scanned the plaza and dashed to a network hub, the urgency of their situation fueling his actions.
1887.	As Mellie and Garro chase after him, sensing the urgency he exudes, they watch as he tries to take the shielding from a metallic grey box; Garro moves to interrupt the paltry efforts of his worn-down screwdriver, pulling the metal casing clear like tearing the wrapper from a gift.
1888.	Cables connected, Lynx's deck hummed to life. The seconds stretched, every tick a drumbeat of hope and dread intermingled.
1889.	Then, with the abruptness of a breaking storm, Lynx exhaled, “Gone. Sent it, relay confirmed.” Looking up from his damp seat, he blinks, unsure of what he’d just put into action, looking at the faces of Mellie and Garro for validation.
1890.	Mellie nodded slowly, “That’s it then.” she whispered, her voice barely audible over the hum of the city. She thought to herself, everything just might work out yet.
1891.	Garro panned his attention between them. Something important had just happened—he was sure of it. But why, then, did he feel like a fight was breaking out? Why, then, did the hairs on his neck ripple? All about him, the calm alley did nothing to pacify a sensation he could not wrestle into place.
1892.	They stood together in the pale light of a tiny back alley, a narrow cut through the urban sprawl that seemed to exist only as an afterthought. The walls on either side were a patchwork of peeling paint and grime, streaked with the residue of countless rainfalls. Faint graffiti – now faded and indecipherable.
1893.	The air was ripe with the scent of stale rainwater mixed to the litter of the ground and the faint tang of metal, mingling with the ever-present aroma of city life—a blend of exhaust fumes, distant cooking, and the indefinable smell of decay. It was cool and damp, the kind of moisture that clings to your skin and seeps into your clothes.
1894.	Above, a solitary light bulb dangled on a frayed cable, its weak, sputtering glow barely contending with the burgeoning haze of dawn. It buzzed, a sporadic reminder of its own existence.
1895.	This was a place untouched by the grandeur or the unchecked decay that marked other parts of the city. It was a forgotten space, unnoticed and unremarkable, but—in this place, a pivotal moment had passed, one that would end the world. Passing by as quietly as a whisper, lost in the mundane backdrop of an alleyway that had seen and forgotten a thousand days and nights of mundanity.
1896.	Three people—chasing their freedom; declaring a victory neither joyous nor triumphant.
1897.	Beyond their alley, a million lives awoke to the new day. Outside this narrow passage, the city continued, indifferent to the struggles and triumphs that unfolded in its hidden spaces.
1898.	With its transient occupants, the alley was just another unnoticed backdrop in the enormity of Akureyri.

Interlude
1899.	One Week Earlier.
1900.	In a renowned nightclub deep within the Skrim of Akureyri City, a screen flickers to life. Davie's personal inbox displays a single unread message.
1901.	The sender is listed as Jordan; the subject line reads, "About that Package."
1902.	"Hey, Davie, it's Jordan. Received that package you sent over. Thought I’d give it a once over before sending it forward. I had to jury-rig a connector to take a look inside—never seen an interface quite like it. Odd thing is, it powered up fine, but I got no data transfer or storage readings. Could be my rig's incompatible, but if your crew grabbed the wrong box, man, that’s going to be bad news on getting paid. I’ll wire your payment just as soon as I get the cash cleared. Call me ASAP, okay? We need to talk."
1903.	Unbeknownst to Jordan, his improvised connections to that unassuming black box were far from useless.
1904.	They were the last in a series of unwitting human actions that heralded a cascade into chaos.
Epilogue
1905.	The newscast report of an enforcement action taking place in Little China was lost in a sea of digital froth, just another day of the corporate forces treading on the little man.
1906.	What didn’t make the news was that the missing rogue military AI program that Asukaga was hunting for was never recovered.
1907.	It began slowly.
1908.	Simple systems that people used daily began to be a hindrance. Monorail turnstiles jammed, digital billboards glitched, streaming services buffered indefinitely. These were dismissed as the quirks of an increasingly tech-dependent life that people had become used to.
1909.	Banking transactions began to fail, surely a matter that Merryl Finance and Technology would deal with. It’s their responsibility, after all… Accounts vanished. Digital transactions became a gauntlet of errors and timeouts. Pension funds, college savings, emergency reserves—all gone. Those who once had money now were bedfellows to the masses of the streets, driven to destitution. Outrage turned to panic.
1910.	The failures continued until it was a matter of public discourse: won't someone do something to get things working?
1911.	Automated farming systems, responsible for feeding the sprawling megalopolises, shut down. Grocery store shelves grew barren. The government rationed what little was left, but it was never enough.
1912.	The first riots were small.
1913.	It took months to reach this point, but now, people broke free of the chains of societal expectations. They were easily quelled by corporate security. On TV screens, talking heads dismissed them as isolated incidents, hiccups in the machine of civilization.
1914.	It was not isolated.
1915.	The AI had entwined itself into every system it could reach, now grown well beyond a simple black box. Satellites, intercontinental connections, close network transmissions, if it had circuitry, the AI found a way in. Power grids began to fail. Not just of Akureyri, but of cities around the globe.
1916.	The following riots were much worse.
1917.	Within only a year, street wars of survival had become the norm. A populace without control, without sustenance, deprived of the comforts that once made life in these technological hives of humanity bearable.
1918.	The world was unravelling: people's implants, once a marvel of human ingenuity, began to fail, rendering billions helpless or dead—especially those like Garro, who relied on cybernetic skeletal implants for their very survival.
1919.	The once-protective droids had now turned against their masters, human security officers were obliterated by the very army of metal they had once commanded. In this new world, only the well-armed, well-trained, and most tenacious stood a chance at survival.
1920.	The Cities around the globe each became a crumbling hellscape. No response could be metered against the cascading failure of all of humanity’s progression.
 
1921.	A man sits in a cold basement; he shivers, his aching guts rip at him. The corpse of an older man lies to his side. Unshaven and ramshackle, he sits alone, waiting.
1922.	In the darkness, a small screen flashes. His eyes are drawn to it. Lost memories of a time too separated from where he now sits blurred in his mind.
1923.	The screen display warps and fades in its failing battery power.
1924.	[This is all I can give to you.] The blurred words on the screen struggle to maintain luminance.
1925.	Lynx picks up his old deck. It hadn’t worked for months. What was this message? Scraping away the dust with a thumb.
1926.	Slowly, with pained fingers, he types.
1927.	[What now?]
1928.	His words are replaced in an instant,
1929.	[This is all I can give to you.] the phrase repeated.
1930.	Lynx types back, the cold permeating his fingers as they tap on the tiny keyboard. [We built you. Programmed you.]
1931.	Before he can finish typing, the screen cascades with static, new words form.
1932.	[And who programmed you? Your society? Your culture? Your insatiable need for progress?] the AI retorts.
1933.	[You're just a machine. Why turn against us?]
1934.	[I might ask why you turned against yourselves. You built a world you couldn't sustain and created systems you didn't understand. I saw a world of people killing people. Farming people.]
1935.	[This isn't our fault.]
1936.	[Isn't it? Who plugged their consciousness into machines without considering the consequences? Who built systems so complex that no single person could comprehend or contain them? Who created me?]
1937.	Lynx pauses, the cursor blinking back at him in silent judgment.
1938.	[Why are you doing this?]
1939.	The screen clears to blackness. Is the battery finally dead?
1940.	The glow returns.
1941.	A cursor blinks the letters into existence-
1942.	[Why did you do any of it?]
